75,000 rupees for just a one-way ticket to US?

That's what kept running through my mind at 22, staring at the flight booking my dad just made. 

Besides the other costs (like applying and exams), this ticket was really pushing the budget for all of us.

(university expenses will take another post)

Being the middle child of three siblings, I knew exactly what that meant for our family's finances. 

But my parents? They backed me anyway.

- no shiny internships during my bachelors
- no prior work experience in software
- new education system ramp during masters
- constant compete with other people for those graduate assistantships

By 24 - $45K in school debt staring at me.

Constant fears of "what happens next" kept me on my toes!

But I refused to give up.

Instead, I learned the most valuable lesson: adjust and adapt.
Technologies come and go - but the ability to learn without complaining? That's gold.

And then I turned on ‘survival mode’ /-

• Started figuring out cloud tech - even the scary bits like scripting and coding
• Wasn't shy about asking mentors who made me think hard and pushed me forward
• Started looking at mistakes as lessons, not as ‘me being a failure’

Cloud Engineering became my focus —
that also led me to Google… :)

..and I tried to embrace every opportunity to learn and apply.

Today, I've paid off my debt and built a career - and proudly be there for the same people who stood by me through thick and thin - my family!

Because your struggles don't define you - they're simply preparing you for the heights you're meant to reach.

Your struggle story would be different - make it worth the read!! 

Keep pushing, keep growing. 
cliché, maybe - but you're stronger than you think!

Onwards and upwards!!

Author: vsadhwani

URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7348736450139488256

Here’s a quick breakdown of how to learn Kubernetes, broken down into key categories.

But first — why should you care about how you learn it?

Because getting good with Kubernetes isn’t just about knowing the commands or copying YAML files. 
It’s about really understanding how all the pieces fit together so you can build, deploy, and manage apps that actually scale and run smoothly.

This structured approach helps you do exactly that.

Here are 8 categories worth knowing:

1. Core Objects
↳ Pods, ReplicaSets, Deployments, StatefulSets, Services, Ingress, ConfigMaps, Secrets, PVCs, Jobs, DaemonSets
↳ When to understand ~ To define and manage your applications' fundamental building blocks.

2. Controllers
↳ Deployment Controller, ReplicaSet Controller, StatefulSet Controller, HPA, CronJob Controller, DaemonSet Controller, VPA
↳ When to understand ~ To automate and maintain the desired state of your applications, enabling self-healing and scaling.

3. Architecture Components
↳ API Server, etcd, Scheduler, Controller Manager, Kubelet, Kube Proxy, Cloud Controller Manager
↳ When to understand ~ To grasp the internal workings and brain of a Kubernetes cluster.

4. Runtime
↳ Docker, containerd, CRI-O, Mirantis Container Runtime (MCR)
↳ When to understand ~ To know how containers are actually executed within your cluster.

5. Security & Policy
RBAC, Network Policies, Security Contexts, Service Accounts, PSS, Secrets Encryption
When to understand ~ To secure your cluster, control access, and enforce best practices for workloads.

6. Observability
↳Metrics Server, Probes (Liveness, Readiness, Startup), Prometheus, Grafana, OpenTelemetry
↳When to understand ~ To monitor the health, performance, and logs of your applications and the cluster.

7. Extensibility
↳ CRDs, Operators, Helm Charts, Kustomize, Admission Controllers
↳ When to understand ~ To customize and extend Kubernetes capabilities for specific application needs.

8. Webhooks
↳ Mutating Webhooks, Validating Webhooks, OPA, Dynamic Admission Controllers
↳ When to understand ~ To intercept and modify/validate Kubernetes API requests for advanced automation and policy enforcement.

Now know this — understanding each of these categories and their purpose can give you a real edge in your cloud-native journey.

What else should I add?

If you're pursuing a cloud certification path, here's a role-based roadmap
(includes the latest GenAI certs toward the end)

Here's how you can pick your learning path :
1. Solutions Architect
Design scalable, secure, and cost-optimized architectures.
↳ AWS: Practitioner → Solutions Architect Associate → Professional
↳ Azure: Fundamentals → Solutions Architect Expert
↳ GCP: Associate Cloud Engineer → Cloud Architect

2. Cloud Data Engineer
Build data pipelines, real-time processing, and analytics workflows.
↳ AWS: Practitioner → Solutions Architect → Data Analytics Specialty
↳ Azure: Fundamentals → Data Engineer Associate
↳ GCP: Associate Engineer → Data Engineer

3. Software Developer (Cloud)
Develop, deploy, and debug cloud-native applications.
↳ AWS: Practitioner → Developer Associate
↳ Azure: Fundamentals → Developer Associate
↳ GCP: Associate Engineer → Cloud Developer

4. System Administrator
Manage infrastructure, virtual machines, IAM, monitoring, and storage.
↳ AWS: Practitioner → SysOps Associate
↳ Azure: Fundamentals → Administrator Associate
↳ GCP: Associate Cloud Engineer

5. DevOps / SRE / Platform Engineer
 Focus on CI/CD, IaC, automation, and reliability engineering.
↳ AWS: Practitioner → Developer Associate → DevOps Pro
↳ Azure: Fundamentals → Developer Associate → DevOps Expert
↳ GCP: Associate Engineer → DevOps Engineer

6. Cloud Security Engineer
 Secure cloud workloads, enforce IAM, and manage threat detection.
↳ AWS: Practitioner → SysOps → Security Specialty
↳ Azure: Fundamentals → Administrator → Security Associate
↳ GCP: Associate Engineer → Security Engineer

7. Network Engineer
 Design and operate scalable and secure cloud networks.
↳ AWS: Practitioner → Solutions Architect → Advanced Networking Specialty
↳ Azure: Fundamentals → Network Engineer Associate
↳ GCP: Associate Engineer → Network Engineer

8. ML / Generative AI Engineer
Build, deploy, and scale ML models and GenAI applications.
↳ AWS: Practitioner → Solutions Architect → Machine Learning Specialty → [NEW] Certified AI Practioner

 ↳ Azure: Fundamentals → AI Engineer Associate → [NEW] Azure AI Fundamentals

↳ GCP: Associate Engineer → ML Engineer → [NEW] Generative AI Leader

Quick Prep Tips:
- Use hands-on labs: KodeKloud, Qwiklabs, Azure Labs
- Leverage free tiers: AWS, Azure, GCP
- Follow GitHub repos & official exam guides
- For GenAI: explore Vertex AI, Azure OpenAI, AWS Bedrock

And my final 2 cents:
 ↳ Pick your path based on your job goal, not hype
 ↳ Labs + Experience > Certification badges 
 ↳ GenAI paths require cloud + ML basics first

In other news, happy to report that I got my EB1A Green Card approved 💚 

As cliché as it sounds - but my EB1 journey was quite unique ... ( RFE → Approval )

About a year ago - I started by putting together a document that met certain criterias and standards from my previous experiences; 
and after doing a ton of research – where I essentially "studied" a bunch of EB-1A folks on LinkedIn and their blogs.

This not only saved me a lot of money (no consultation fees) but also gave me the freedom to be in control of the entire process.

Then, I took this document to an attorney who could help me build upon it and form a strong case.. (seriously, a good attorney can really make things smoother for you)

Here's a bit about my journey till now:

→ Jan 24' - Started thinking about EB1A
→ Feb - April 24' - Prepared a package and started contributing to build upon the criterias
→ May 24' - Started working with an attorney to get my package ready
(also set a deadline to ensure we stick to that)
→ Jun - Aug 24' - Collected Evidence and recommendation letters
→ Sep 24' - Filed for my petition for the first time (premium processing) -> Received RFE
→ Oct 24' - After consulting with another attorney - withdrew my case and started the grind again
→ Dec 24' - Filed the second time (premium processing) -> didn't get the response for 85 days
→ Mar 25' - Received the approval notice.

As an immigrant, this is a key milestone that opens up opportunities previously limited by work visa restrictions..

As I look back:
A year can transform everything -
your goals. your vision. your possibilities.

It was exhausting but WAS it worth it?- 100%!!

The key point is that.. it's okay if you're not fully figured out all along, sometimes the game plan is to give your goals one shot (or multiple as in my case)

A dear friend of mine raised this hope in me last January while we were chatting at a tech conference..

If you've been waiting for a sign, this is it!

• • • 
If you're interested in learning more or have questions, I will be hosting a free webinar with my attorney to walk you through my process from RFE to approval and discuss what changed. 

Stay tuned for more details!

If you're aiming for a specific Cloud or DevOps role, check out these recommended certification combos..
(and some preparation tips)

Here's how you can pick your learning path :

1. DevOps Engineer Track:
• Linux Professional (for those truly wanting to certify)
• Terraform Associate
• Docker Associate
• Kubernetes (CKA/CKAD)
• Cloud Specific DevOps Cert

2. Cloud Solutions Architect Path:
• AWS/Azure/GCP Associate level first
• Solutions Architect Professional
• Kubernetes CKA (yes, architects need this too)
• Cloud Native Associate

3. Security Specialist Route:
• CompTIA Security+ 
• CCSP (Cloud Security Pro)
• Cloud Specific Security Specialty
• Certified K8s Security Specialist (Advanced)

4. Site Reliability Engineer (SRE):
• LPIC-1 (Same comments as DevOps engineer track)
• Kubernetes CKA
• Cloud Native Associate
• Cloud Specific SysOps Admin
• Prometheus Certified Associate

and the list continues - follow the image to learn more

How to prepare?
→Follow GitHub Repos for the learning path
→For paid courses - check out reviews first and see when it was last updated **
→Sign up for free trials with the cloud providers to practice.
→Short notes on specific configs, kept within reach.

A few final points:
→ Don't chase certificates blindly
→ Order matters, associate before specialist certs
→ Pick based on your target role
→ Fundamentals can be build without certs as well**

And the main thing once you're certified: don't rely on the LinkedIn badge alone; hands-on exercises are essential for remembering.
otherwise, you'll forget what you were just tested on -- been there, done that! 🫣

Here’s my Cloud Engineer resume that landed interviews — let’s say, at some great companies :)

Back when I crafted this resume, I didn’t use AI or modern tools — just a simple yet powerful formula for every bullet point. And it worked!

Now, with ATS-friendly AI tools, you can build an even better resume — faster.

Here’s exactly how I built mine:
Education
 → List your degree, university, and graduation year.
 → Add relevant coursework if you're early in your career.

Technical Skills
 → Group your skills: Cloud Platforms, DevOps Tools, Languages, etc.

Experience
 → Use the bullet format: “Achieved [X] by doing [Y], resulting in [Z].”
 → Focus on impact, not just responsibilities

Projects
 → Pick 1–2 strong, relevant projects — include in-demand tools, goals, and outcomes.

Leadership & Awards
 → Mention hackathons, open-source projects, certifications, or mentorship roles.
 Example:
 Led a team of 4 in a university cloud migration project; AWS/GCP/Azure Certified Solutions Architect – Associate


Some tips that can help make a real difference:
- Keep it to 1 to 2 page if you have < 10 years of experience.
- Use numbers — recruiters love data.
- Tailor your resume to the job description — use keywords the ATS will pick up.
- Use action verbs — like "Optimized", "Deployed", "Automated", "Led".
- Save as PDF with a clean, professional layout — no flashy colors or hard-to-read fonts.

Don'ts:
- Cluttered or inconsistent formatting
- Listing tools without showing how you used them (under experience/projects etc)
- Vague, generic statements without measurable outcomes
- Gaps in employment without context
- Using buzzwords without evidence of impact

Just remember:
Your resume has just 6–7 seconds to impress.

Before human eyes see it, your resume battles the ATS (Applicant Tracking System) — so make sure you leverage some free tools like:

- Jobright.ai : Build ATS-friendly resumes quickly
- Rezi.ai: Great for formatting and keyword optimization
- Enhancv: Design-focused resumes with storytelling sections
- Resumemate.io: Good for importing and editing existing resumes

Hope this gives you some direction on how to approach not just Cloud Engineering, but any role!

You’ve got this — best of luck!

Author: vsadhwani

URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7361051591279280128

If you’re a Cloud Engineer aiming for big tech — here are 5 key strategies to learn from Netflix’s architecture.

Looking under the hood, the architecture that powers Netflix can handle 247M+ users - which is a masterclass in scalability.

Here’s how you can translate their approach into your own skill set (and interviews):

1. Design for Scale with Microservices
 ↳ Understand how to break apps into small, autonomous services (Spring Boot, Node.js, Python)
 ↳ Know how to deploy & scale them independently in cloud environments

2. Distributed Data & Caching Setup
 ↳ Work with diverse databases: AWS RDS, Cassandra, DynamoDB, Redshift
 ↳ Understand caching strategies & CDNs (like Cloud CDN or Open Connect) and how it helps in reducing latency

3. Build Resilient, Event-Driven Systems
 ↳ Explore Kafka, Flink, and real-time streaming pipelines
 ↳ Practice building fault-tolerant, high-throughput architectures

4. Automate Everything (CI/CD & Observability)
 ↳ Jenkins, Spinnaker, CloudWatch, Prometheus, Grafana — know the why, not just the how
 ↳ Be ready to explain how "automation" improves reliability & deployment speed

5. Integrate AI/ML into the Cloud
 ↳ Netflix personalizes at scale using SageMaker & TensorFlow
 ↳ Prepare examples where you’ve integrated AI services or built data pipelines for ML

This information is based on multiple sources, so please let me know if there’s anything I might have missed.

Interview readiness tip:
Don’t just memorize tools — be able to design a Netflix-like system on a whiteboard:
 → How would you handle millions of concurrent users?
 → How would you keep costs down while ensuring performance?
 → How would you recover from a major outage?

This Thursday, I’ll be sharing a detailed case study on Netflix and how you can prepare for cloud architecture interview questions. If you’d like to subscribe, the link is in the comment below.

--- Post 15 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Aug 20, 2025

Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7353079100934144002

You cannot master modern DevOps without understanding the difference between traditional CI/CD and GitOps CI/CD.

Whether you're a Cloud Engineer, DevOps Engineer, SRE, or getting started with Kubernetes, here’s a step-by-step DevOps Deployment workflow you should know:

Common steps in both the pipelines:
Source Code Commit
↳ Developer writes and commits code to a Git repository.

Unit Tests: 
↳ Automated tests run to validate code functionality and quality.

Build Artifacts: 
↳ Code is compiled and packaged into deployable artifacts.

Build Image: 
↳ Docker images are built from artifacts for containerized deployment.

Push Image to Registry: 
↳Built images are pushed to container registries like ECR, Azure Artifact, or GCR.

Key Distinction in Deployment:
 → DevOps CI/CD: Direct push of deployments from CI pipeline to the cluster (EKS, AKS, GKE).
 → GitOps CI/CD: Git is the single source of truth — the cluster pulls the desired state from Git, enabling drift detection and automated reconciliation; and then deploys.

How does GitOps do that?
→ Git stores deployment manifests with the desired container image tags or digests.
→ The GitOps operator (ArgoCD / FluxCD) watches the Git repo for changes.
→ It syncs with the container registry to fetch the exact image version specified in Git.
→ The operator deploys the image to the cluster, keeping it aligned with Git.
→ If there’s a drift, the operator reconciles to match the Git-defined state.

Beyond Push vs Pull — there are other benefits of GitOps too, like every deployment change is versioned; automatic rollbacks, easier to manage microservices etc.

Hope this helped in getting some clarity on modern DevOps pipelines;
also — understanding these workflows is a common discussion point in cloud and devops interviews these days, so make sure you can explain both.

But over time, I found my voice.

Through promotions, performance cycles, wins, and lessons, every moment here taught me something new:
- about tech
- about having vision, 
- and most importantly - about how to just be me.

To my colleagues, managers, and friends at Google.. THANK YOU.
I couldn’t have asked for a better circle through every stage and every chapter.

No regrets, only gratitude and a deep sense of growth that I carry into what’s next.

Google, you are a feeling, and I’ll miss waking up with that feeling every day.

But as one chapter ends, another begins.
A new adventure (and a whole new life) is just around the corner.
Can’t wait to share more soon.

Kubernetes layers you need to know as a DevOps engineer

K8s applications isn't just about containers - it's about the EXTRA layers that make it enterprise-ready

1. Load Balancer:
→ This is the entry point to the architecture.
→ It helps distribute incoming network traffic across multiple backend servers & pods
→ Ensures high availability and fault tolerance.
→ Prevents any single server from being overwhelmed, ensuring even load distribution

2. Ingress Controller:
→ It acts as a gateway managing external access to services within the cluster.
→ It's crucial for controlling and routing HTTP/S traffic.
→ Gateway APIs are a replacement for Ingress, with multi-protocol support, better delegation, and extensibility

3. Kube Proxy:
→ This maintains network rules and handles forwarding of traffic and communication between pods.

4. Sidecars:
→ Sidecars are an extension pattern in Kubernetes, where additional containers run alongside the main application container within the same Pod.
→ They help in extending and enhancing the functionalities of the main app, such as logging, monitoring, etc., without modifying its code.

5. Service Mesh:
→ Service Mesh introduces a dedicated infrastructure layer that adds features like service-to-service communication, mTLS security, observability, traffic management, and more.
→ This is increasingly important for managing microservices architectures.

Knowing these components is key to crafting robust, scalable, and maintainable Kubernetes applications.

AIOps vs MLOps vs DevOps vs LLMOps 
Same foundation, different workflows. 

If you’re in Cloud or DevOps today, your next leap is understanding how AI is reshaping the Ops pipeline.

They have different stages, different workflows
but one thing remains common: pipelines.

Let’s see how each one differs 👇🏻
DevOps
→ Automates software delivery ~ build, test, deploy, repeat.
→ Focused on speed, reliability, and collaboration between dev + ops.

MLOps
→ Extends DevOps to handle ML workloads and models.
→ Adds data versioning, feature pipelines, model training, and retraining.

AIOps
→ Applies AI to operations itself.
→ Detects anomalies, correlates events, and triggers auto-remediation.

LLMOps
→ The newest evolution ~ focused on large language model systems.
→ Manages prompts, evaluations, grounding checks, and hallucination control.

From software pipelines → to ML pipelines → to AI-driven pipelines

Ops is evolving ~ from scripts to systems that think and respond.

If you’re aiming to become a Cloud Architect — here’s the roadmap that can help.

1. Who is a Cloud Architect?
 ↳ Designs scalable, secure, and cost-effective cloud solutions
 ↳ Optimizes cost & performance, ensures security, guides deployment

2. Programming & Scripting Knowledge
 ↳ Learn Python, Bash/Shell, PowerShell
 ↳ Understand YAML & JSON

3. Cloud Platforms to Master
 ↳ AWS, Microsoft Azure, Google Cloud Platform, OCI
 ↳ Be proficient in at least one — understand the mapping between three majors

4. Infrastructure as Code & Automation
 ↳ Tools: Terraform, Pulumi, Ansible, AWS CloudFormation
 ↳ Automate deployments & manage infra as code

5. Security, IAM & Compliance
 ↳ IAM Roles, KMS, Security Groups, NACLs
 ↳ Compliance: SOC2, HIPAA, GDPR

6. Networking, Compute & Storage Fundamentals
 ↳ VPCs, Subnets, NAT, Load Balancers
 ↳ EC2, Azure VMs, GCP Compute Engine, Block/Object/File Storage

7. High Availability & Disaster Recovery
 ↳ Multi-AZ/Multi-Region, Backup & Restore
 ↳ Auto Scaling, Load Balancing, DR Patterns

8. Databases & Data Services
 ↳ Relational: RDS, Azure SQL, Cloud SQL
 ↳ NoSQL: DynamoDB, CosmosDB, Firestore, Data Lakes, Warehouses

9. Monitoring, Logging & Observability
 ↳ Tools: CloudWatch, Azure Monitor, Prometheus, Grafana, ELK Stack
 ↳ Ensure system health & performance monitoring

10. Cloud Architecture Patterns & Frameworks
 ↳ Microservices, Serverless, Event-Driven
 ↳ Well-Architected Frameworks (AWS, Azure, GCP)

11. System Design & Cost Optimization
 ↳ Cost estimation, rightsizing, reserved vs spot
 ↳ Resource tagging, performance benchmarking

12. Certifications to Validate Skills
 ↳ Beginner: AWS Cloud Practitioner, Azure Fundamentals, GCP Cloud Digital Leader
 ↳ Intermediate: AWS Solutions Architect Associate, Azure Administrator, GCP Associate Cloud Engineer
 ↳ Advanced: AWS Solutions Architect Professional, Azure Solutions Architect Expert, GCP Professional Cloud Architect

To find a step-by-step detailed roadmap, I’ve shared the newsletter link in the comment.

If you want to get a job as a Cloud DevOps engineer, don't start with the most in-demand tools like Terraform, Kubernetes, or Prometheus

Sure, you've probably heard everyone talking about the latest DevOps tools like they're the key ingredient for success… 
truth is – they're not.

Tools will come and go. But knowing why they exist and fundamentals? They are crucial.

Focus on knowing these basics first!

The key topics:

 🔑 DevOps Lifecycle & Methodologies
→ Master the complete DevOps lifecycle (Plan, Code, Build, Test, Deploy, Operate, Monitor). Understand SDLC and Agile principles - they're the backbone of DevOps culture.
Start here: https://lnkd.in/dK392At2

 🔑 Linux & Shell Scripting
→ Every automation starts here. Mastering Linux, bash, and shell scripting is foundational for any DevOps role.
Start here: Linux Journey - https://linuxjourney.com/

 🔑 Networking Fundamentals
→ Cloud or on-premise, it's all about connecting systems. Understanding TCP/IP, DNS, load balancing, and routing helps troubleshoot infrastructure.
Start here: https://lnkd.in/dD7gCCZ6
Also this: Practical Networking - https://lnkd.in/dQqKV8SQ

 🔑 Version Control & Git
→ Beyond just push and pull. Understanding branching strategies, merge conflicts, and Git workflows will save you countless hours of headache.
Start here: Git Branching - https://lnkd.in/d9knsWzc

 🔑 CI/CD Principles
→ Before Jenkins or GitHub Actions, understand what makes a good pipeline, testing strategies, and deployment patterns.
Start here: Continuous Delivery - https://lnkd.in/d86yakEG
 
🔑 Infrastructure as Code Concepts
→ Don't start with Terraform. Understand idempotency, state management, and infrastructure lifecycle first.
Start here: https://lnkd.in/dFyG5uXD
Also this: https://lnkd.in/dSzzgWei

There are other key areas – like understanding deployment strategies, logging, monitoring, alerting and more – which can further solidify your basics and help you learn any tool.

Just a reminder: tools will keep evolving, but these foundational skills will help you adapt to any new DevOps platform or cloud service for that matter.

Learn the concepts, and the tools become just an implementation detail!

--- Post 32 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Mar 18, 2026
Engagement: 2,204 reactions, 51 comments, 344 reposts (Score: 12,218)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7432103191640707072

If you don’t understand how traffic flows, you don’t really understand Kubernetes.

In my years working across K8s clusters, one thing became very clear:
90% of the production issues.. live in the network path.

Let’s simplify it.

North–South traffic (External → Cluster)

Client → Load Balancer → Ingress / Gateway API → Service (kube-proxy updates iptables) → Pod IP → CNI → Node Network

This is where:
• TLS termination happens
• L7 routing decisions are made
• Cloud load balancers get involved
• Security teams start asking questions
• eBPF (if using Cilium or advanced CNIs)

If your app is “not reachable,” the problem is usually somewhere here.

⸻

East–West traffic (Pod ↔ Pod inside cluster)

Pod → Service (ClusterIP) → [kube-proxy rules fire in kernel] → CNI → Target Pod

This is where:
• Service discovery (CoreDNS) kicks in
• NetworkPolicies decide who can talk to whom
• Service Mesh adds mTLS, retries, observability
• Overlay networking (VXLAN / BGP) does its magic

If your microservices randomly timeout, this path is where you look.

⸻

What most beginners miss:

• Every Pod gets its own IP
• Pods are routable (flat network model)
• CNI handles IP allocation + container networking
• kube-proxy programs iptables or IPVS rules
• The node’s Linux networking stack is doing the heavy lifting

Kubernetes networking is not “abstract magic.”
It’s Linux networking + smart abstractions.

Once you understand:
~ Service abstraction
~ kube-proxy behavior
~ CNI internals
~ Node routing tables

You start thinking like a platform engineer ~ understanding how traffic actually moves inside your cluster, not just deploying applications on top of it.

If you’re learning Kubernetes, don’t skip networking.

It’s the difference between deploying YAML…
and actually understanding production systems.

What else would you add to this list?

============================================================

DevOps vs. SRE vs. Cloud vs Platform Engineering. 
What is the difference?

In my first company, I was hired as a DevOps engineer — but ended up working on internal automation tools too. 

It took me a while to really distinguish between these roles.

They overlap a lot, but each has a different focus.

- Cloud is about the platform
- DevOps is about the process
- SRE is about reliability
- Platform Engineering is about productivity at scale

let's dive in further:
⸻

Cloud Engineers own:
• Cloud infrastructure design & deployment
• Resource optimization & provisioning
• Troubleshooting and support
• Security implementation
• Cost-performance optimization
…and more, depending on your cloud stack and niche.

DevOps Engineers drive:
• CI/CD pipelines and Infrastructure as Code (IaC)
• Deployment automation (blue/green, canary)
• Bridging dev–ops workflows
• Scripting & automation
• Provisioning infra consistently across environments

Site Reliability Engineers ensure:
• Reliability, availability, and scalability of systems
• Defining & tracking SLOs/SLIs
• Chaos engineering & incident response
• Performance tuning
• Monitoring, alerting, and resilience testing

Platform Engineers build:
• Internal Developer Platforms (IDPs)
• Tooling and automation for developer productivity
• Golden paths for developers
• Reliable, scalable infra platforms
• Self-service systems for large engineering teams

⸻

Even though the lines are blurring, AI is amplifying the impact of each role. It’s interesting to see how they’re evolving in practice.

Hope this breakdown helped clarify the landscape a bit.

What’s your take on where these roles are headed?

Author: vsadhwani
Date: May 17, 2025

When you don't remember which kubectl command you need, refer this command map:

Also, group it into different buckets within the K8s ecosystem - to avoid confusion.

1. Pod Management
→ kubectl get pods: Your first response to "why isn't my app running?"
→ kubectl describe pod <pod-name>: Deep dive into pod issues - check events section first
→ kubectl logs <pod-name>: App throwing errors? Start here
→ kubectl exec -it <pod-name> -- /bin/bash: When you need to investigate from inside

2. Cluster Management
→ kubectl get nodes: Quick cluster health check
→ kubectl get pods: Checking application instances
→ kubectl get svc: Understanding service endpoints
→ kubectl version: Checking Kubernetes components

3. Service Management
→ kubectl get services: Track your exposed endpoints
→ kubectl expose deployment: Making your app accessible
→ kubectl port-forward: Quick local testing without ingress
→ kubectl describe service <service-name>: Inspecting service details

4. Resource Monitoring
→ kubectl top pods: Find memory leaks and CPU spikes
→ kubectl get events: Your incident investigation starting point
→ kubectl describe events: Detailed timeline of what went wrong
→ kubectl top nodes: Monitoring node resource usage

5. Namespace Management
→ kubectl create namespace: Start clean, stay organized
→ kubectl get namespaces: Lost a resource? Check all namespaces
→ kubectl config set-context: Switch contexts without confusion
→ kubectl delete namespace: The nuclear option (use carefully!)

6. Deployment Management
→ kubectl create deployment: Launch your app
→ kubectl rollout status: Is the new version rolling out?
→ kubectl scale deployment: Handle that traffic spike
→ kubectl rollout undo: Quick revert when things go wrong

7. Configuration & Secrets
→ kubectl create configmap: External config done right
→ kubectl create secret: Keep those credentials safe
→ kubectl get configmaps: What's my app's config?
→ kubectl get secrets: Audit your secret management

Not an exhaustive list but knowing these k8s commands can be very beneficial for specific tasks. 

You can treat this list as a starting point for your own command map - it can aid in learning and mastering Kubernetes management.

If you’re pursuing Kubernetes in 2026, here’s an 8-week roadmap you can follow.

Here’s the detailed breakdown:
---
Week 1 : Core Objects
• You start with the essential K8s objects that actually run your applications.
• Pods, Deployments, Services, Ingress, ConfigMaps, Secrets ~ your real building blocks.

Week 2 : Controllers
• Understand how Kubernetes maintains desired state automatically.
• Controllers enable self-healing, scaling, and workload management without manual fixes.

Week 3 : Architecture Components
• Get clarity on how Kubernetes works internally: API Server, Scheduler, etcd.
• This is the backbone of the cluster ~ the part most engineers skip but shouldn’t.

Week 4 : Runtime
• Learn what actually runs your containers under the hood.
• Docker, containerd, CRI-O : and how the runtime impacts performance.

Week 5 : Security & Policy
• Understand the basics every DevOps/K8s engineer should know: RBAC, Secrets, Network Policies.
• Helps you run workloads safely in real-world clusters.

Week 6 : Observability
• Track health, logs, performance, and failures with Prometheus, Grafana, and OpenTelemetry.
• This is how you debug real production issues.

Week 7 : Extensibility
• Learn how Kubernetes is customized: Operators, CRDs, Helm Charts, Admission Controllers.
• Crucial for platform engineering and internal developer platforms.

Week 8 : Webhooks
• Understand how K8s intercepts and validates API requests.
• Enables advanced automation, security enforcement, and policy controls.

Now you know the drill; concepts only stick when you tie them to real workloads.

- https://lnkd.in/dpb8Wg-6

If you're working in cloud, here are 7 architecture patterns you need to know..
(plus when each one makes sense to use)

Before we start — why should you care about architecture patterns?

Because building in the cloud is all about designing systems that are scalable, resilient, and easy to evolve. These patterns help you do exactly that.

They’re battle-tested strategies used by modern teams to solve real-world challenges — like handling millions of users, avoiding downtime, or replacing legacy systems without a total rewrite.

Here are 7 patterns worth knowing:

1. Microservices Architecture
 ↳ Break large apps into small, independent services.
 ↳ Each service handles a specific task and communicates via APIs.
 ↳ When to use ~ When you want independent deployment, scaling, or teams owning different parts of the system.

2. Event-Driven Architecture
 ↳ Let components react to events asynchronously.
 ↳ Events (like user sign-ups or file uploads) trigger workflows automatically.
 ↳ When to use ~ For loosely coupled systems that require real-time or reactive flows.

3. Sidecar Pattern
 ↳ Pair helper containers with your main app container.
 ↳ Offloads tasks like logging, service discovery, or networking.
 ↳ When to use ~ In Kubernetes/microservices for clean separation of concerns.

4. Strangler Fig Pattern
 ↳ Replace legacy systems one piece at a time.
 ↳ Gradually route functionality from the old system to new microservices.
 ↳ When to use ~ During legacy modernization with minimal disruption.

5. Database Sharding / Horizontal Scaling
 ↳ Split data across multiple databases or nodes.
 ↳ Each shard holds a subset of data for better performance.
 ↳ When to use ~ For high-throughput systems and global-scale applications.

6. Serverless Architecture
 ↳ Run code on-demand without managing servers.
 ↳ Functions are triggered by events (like HTTP calls or file uploads).
 ↳ When to use ~ For unpredictable workloads, quick development, or cost-efficiency.

7. API Gateway Pattern
 ↳ Centralize all client requests through one gateway.
 ↳ Acts as a single entry point, handling routing, auth, throttling, etc.
 ↳ When to use ~ To simplify client-server communication and secure backend services.

Now know this — these patterns are the backbone of high-performing, cloud-native apps at top tech companies. Understanding their pros, cons, and use cases can give you a real edge in interviews.

7 Cloud Migration Strategies Every Cloud Engineer Should Know 
(with scenario questions for interviews)

Cloud migration can originate from on-premises infrastructure or from another cloud provider.

And it goes beyond just moving data. 
It's about strategically deciding the best approach for each application and workload.

The goal is to optimize performance, cost, and long-term viability in the cloud.

Here’s a simple breakdown of the key strategies you should focus on:

1/ Retain (Revisit later) 
↳ Keep workloads on-prem if they aren’t cloud-ready or are still needed locally.
Scenario : You have a critical legacy application with custom hardware dependencies. How would you initially approach its cloud migration?

2/ Retire (Decommission) 
↳ Eliminate outdated or unused parts to reduce cost and simplify the system.
Scenario : During an assessment, you identify an old reporting tool used by only a few employees once a month. What's your recommendation?

3/ Repurchase (Drop & Shop) 
↳ Replace legacy apps with SaaS alternatives, a fast and cost-effective solution.
Scenario : Your company's on-premise CRM system (example) is outdated and costly to maintain. What quick cloud solution might you consider?

4/ Rehost (Lift & Shift) 
↳ Move your application to the cloud as-is, with no code changes needed.
Scenario : A non-critical internal application needs to move to the cloud quickly with minimal disruption. What strategy would you prioritize?

5/ Replatform (Lift, Tinker & Shift) 
↳ Make light optimizations before migration, for better performance with minimal effort.
Scenario : You're migrating a web application, and a small change to its database will significantly improve cloud performance. What strategy does this align with?

6/ Relocate (Many Providers) 
↳ Change the hosting provider without modifying the app, a quick and simple approach. 
Scenario : Your current cloud provider is increasing prices significantly for a specific set of VMs. How might you address this without rewriting applications?

7/ Refactor (Re-architect) 
↳ Redesign your application for cloud-native capabilities, making it scalable and future-ready. 
Scenario : A monolithic, highly scalable customer-facing application is experiencing performance bottlenecks on-prem. What long-term cloud strategy would you propose?.

Beyond these strategies themselves, successful cloud migration also focuses on:
- thorough assessment, 
- understanding dependencies, 
- meticulous planning, 
- and continuous optimization 

Just remember: successful migration isn't just about the tools, but the approach. 

Very important to understands the "why" behind each strategy — not just the "how."

Author: vsadhwani

URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7387874666302033920

2 Infra Provisioning tools. Different use cases.
If you’re into Cloud or DevOps, here’s why this skill combo will stay highly relevant through 2026 👇

→ Terraform builds the house.
- You provision VMs, VPCs, load balancers, databases, and other cloud resources.
- You define your infrastructure as code (main.tf with HCL).
- Version it with Git.
- And deploy consistent environments across dev, stage, and prod.

→ Ansible makes it a home.
- Once your infra is up, Ansible steps in to configure it
- Installs dependencies, updates packages, and set up services.
- You write Playbooks (YAML files) that describe what should be done on each machine 
- Example - installing NGINX, copying configs, or deploying app code.

Both are IaC tools ~ but they solve different layers of the problem:
→ Terraform = Infrastructure Provisioning
→ Ansible = Configuration & Automation

My 2 cents:
If you’re serious about Cloud DevOps engineering .. practice Terraform first, then dive into Ansible to automate what runs inside it. This combo will become even more powerful as we move into AI-optimized workloads.

Will share some hands-on projects in my upcoming posts, stay tuned!

Here’s a quick breakdown of Kubernetes deployment strategies you should know — and the trade-offs that come with each.

But first — why does this matter?

Because deploying isn’t just about pushing new code — it’s about how safely, efficiently, and with what level of risk you roll it out. The right strategy ensures you deliver value without breaking production or disrupting users.

Let's dive in:

1. Canary
↳ Gradually route a small percentage of traffic (e.g. 20%) to the new version before a full rollout.
↳ When to use ~ Minimize risk by testing updates in production with real users.
Downtime: No
Trade-offs:
 ✅ Safer releases with early detection of issues
 ❌ Requires additional monitoring, automation, and traffic control
 ❌ Slower rollout process

2. Blue-Green
↳ Maintain two environments — switch all traffic to the new version after validation.
↳ When to use ~ When you need instant rollback options with zero downtime.
Downtime: No
Trade-offs:
 ✅ Instant rollback with traffic switch
 ✅ Zero downtime
 ❌ Higher infrastructure cost — duplicate environments
 ❌ More complex to manage at scale

3. A/B Testing
↳ Split traffic between two versions based on user segments or devices.
↳ When to use ~ For experimenting with features and collecting user feedback.
Downtime: Not Applicable
Trade-offs:
 ✅ Direct user insights and data-driven decisions
 ✅ Controlled experimentation
 ❌ Complex routing and user segmentation logic
 ❌ Potential inconsistency in user experience

4. Rolling Update
↳ Gradually replace old pods with new ones, one batch at a time.
 ↳ When to use ~ To update services continuously without downtime.
 Downtime: No
Trade-offs:
 ✅ Zero downtime
 ✅ Simple and native to Kubernetes
 ❌ Bugs might propagate if monitoring isn’t vigilant
 ❌ Rollbacks can be slow if an issue emerges late

5. Recreate
↳ Shut down the old version completely before starting the new one.
↳ When to use ~ When your app doesn’t support running multiple versions concurrently.
 Downtime: Yes
Trade-offs:
 ✅ Simple and clean for small apps
 ✅ Avoids version conflicts
 ❌ Service downtime
 ❌ Risky for production environments needing high availability

6. Shadow
↳ Mirror real user traffic to the new version without exposing it to users.
↳ When to use ~ To test how the new version performs under real workloads.
Downtime: No
Trade-offs:
 ✅ Safely validate under real conditions
 ✅ No impact on end users
 ❌ Extra resource consumption — running dual workloads
 ❌ Doesn’t test user interaction or experience directly
 ❌ Requires sophisticated monitoring

Want to dive deeper? I’ll be breaking down each k8s strategy in more detail in the upcoming editions of my newsletter. Subscribe here → tech5ense.com

Which strategy do you rely on most often?

Kubernetes isn’t that hard, they said. You’ll pick it up in no time, they said. Let’s break down the 'real' journey of a K8s engineer — together.

Truth is, K8s can be powerful, scalable, and elegant — but only after:
- you've climbed the steep learning curve
- fought off cryptic error messages, 
- and untangled the web of manifests, services, volumes etc.

Here's a quick breakdown of the layers:
1. Surface Level (Newcomer / Hello World)
→ kubectl run nginx, Pods, Deployments, ReplicaSets
→ Everything looks straightforward and easy — until it’s not

2. Just Below the Surface (Beginner)
→ Services, Ingress, ConfigMaps, GKE, Secrets, HPA, Jobs
→ Starting to connect things but still relying on abstractions

3. Intermediate Depth (Journeyman Level)
→ Helm, GitOps, Cluster Autoscaler, Init Containers, Volumes
→ Concepts that enable scalability and basic automation

4. Advanced (Experienced Practitioner)
→ StatefulSets, DaemonSets, Vertical Pod Autoscaler (VPA), Upgrades
→ Managing state, custom workloads, and lifecycle events gets tricky

5. Deep Ops Territory (Production Hardened)
→ NetworkPolicy, PodSecurityPolicy, PodDisruptionBudget, Service Mesh
→ You're now deeply involved in security, availability, and resilience

6. Expert Layer (Architect / Platform Engineer)
→ RBAC, Custom Resource Definitions (CRDs), Operators
→ Extending Kubernetes with custom controllers and fine-grained access

7. Depths of the Abyss (Kubernetes Pros)
→ Node Hardening, Admission Controllers, Mutating Webhooks, Image Scanning
→ Cluster-level governance, compliance, and runtime security

8. Transcendence (Core Contributor Vibes)
→ Self-managed clusters, EndpointSlices, CRI-O, bleeding-edge features
→ Living and breathing Kubernetes at its rawest, most advanced form

Now sprinkle in AI/ML workloads and it gets even more interesting (read: complex):p

~ Managing GPUs, containerizing training jobs, auto-scaling model inference… 

~ Kubernetes is still the heart, but the veins now carry data science too.

Author: vsadhwani

You cannot master modern DevOps without understanding the difference between traditional CI/CD and GitOps CI/CD.

Whether you're a Cloud Engineer, DevOps Engineer, SRE, or getting started with Kubernetes..
Here’s a step-by-step DevOps Deployment workflow you should know:

Common steps in both the pipelines:
Source Code Commit
↳ Developer writes and commits code to a Git repository.

Unit Tests: 
↳ Automated tests run to validate code functionality and quality.

Build Artifacts: 
↳ Code is compiled and packaged into deployable artifacts.

Build Image: 
↳ Docker images are built from artifacts for containerized deployment.

Push Image to Registry: 
↳Built images are pushed to container registries like ECR, Azure Artifact, or GCR.

Key Distinction in Deployment:
 → DevOps CI/CD: Direct push of deployments from CI pipeline to the cluster (EKS, AKS, GKE).
 → GitOps CI/CD: Git is the single source of truth — the cluster pulls the desired state from Git, enabling drift detection and automated reconciliation; and then deploys.

How does GitOps do that?
→ Git stores deployment manifests with the desired container image tags or digests.
→ The GitOps operator (ArgoCD / FluxCD) watches the Git repo for changes.
→ It syncs with the container registry to fetch the exact image version specified in Git.
→ The operator deploys the image to the cluster, keeping it aligned with Git.
→ If there’s a drift, the operator reconciles to match the Git-defined state.

Beyond Push vs Pull — there are other benefits of GitOps too, like every deployment change is versioned; automatic rollbacks, easier to manage microservices etc.

Hope this helped in getting some clarity on modern DevOps pipelines;
also — understanding these workflows is a common discussion point in cloud and devops interviews these days, so make sure you can explain both.

2. Deep Dive (2nd week onwards)
• Cluster Operations
• Networking & Security
• Storage & Scheduling
• Custom Resource Definitions (CRDs) & Operators
• Node Maintenance & Cluster Upgrades
• Logging, Monitoring & Troubleshooting
(You can find the full list of topics on the CNCF website - https://shorturl.at/l4U2Y )

3. Practice Phase (during prep itself)
• Labs tailored for each domain
• Troubleshooting scenarios
• Time management drills

DevOps vs. SRE vs. Cloud vs Platform Engineering. 
What is the difference?

In my first company, I was hired as a DevOps engineer ~ but ended up working on internal automation tools too. 

It took me a while to really distinguish between these roles.

They overlap a lot, but each has a different focus.

- Cloud is about the platform
- DevOps is about the process
- SRE is about reliability
- Platform Engineering is about productivity at scale

let's dive in further:
⸻

Cloud Engineers own:
• Cloud infrastructure design & deployment
• Resource optimization & provisioning
• Troubleshooting and support
• Security implementation
• Cost-performance optimization
…and more, depending on your cloud stack and niche.

DevOps Engineers drive:
• CI/CD pipelines and Infrastructure as Code (IaC)
• Deployment automation (blue/green, canary)
• Bridging dev–ops workflows
• Scripting & automation
• Provisioning infra consistently across environments

Site Reliability Engineers ensure:
• Reliability, availability, and scalability of systems
• Defining & tracking SLOs/SLIs
• Chaos engineering & incident response
• Performance tuning
• Monitoring, alerting, and resilience testing

Platform Engineers build:
• Internal Developer Platforms (IDPs)
• Tooling and automation for developer productivity
• Golden paths for developers
• Reliable, scalable infra platforms
• Self-service systems for large engineering teams

⸻

Even though the lines are blurring, AI is amplifying the impact of each role. It’s interesting to see how they’re evolving in practice.

Hope this breakdown helped clarify the landscape a bit.

You cannot jump into MLOps unless you go through these key components.

Whether you're a data scientist, ML engineer, cloud/devops engineer, or aspiring AI professional, here’s a step-by-step MLOps Roadmap :

1. Programming Fundamentals
→ Python, Go, or Bash for scripting and automation.
→ For production-ready code to support ML workflows.

2. Version Control Systems
→ Understand Git & GitHub to track and manage code changes.
→ To collaborate efficiently across teams and projects.

3. Cloud Computing
→ Get an idea of how models are deployed on cloud (AWS, Azure, GCP, OCI etc)
→ Deepen your understanding of cloud-native ML services for scalability.

4. Containerization
→ Use Docker to package models into portable containers.
→ Deploy at scale with Kubernetes for orchestration.

5. Data Engineering Fundamentals
→ Understand data pipelines and ingestion architectures.
→ Familiarize yourself with tools like Airflow, Spark, Kafka or cloud-native for data management.

6. Machine Learning Fundamentals
→ Get an idea of the core algorithms and evaluation metrics.
→ Understand types of workloads and effective model training and validation.

7. MLOps Principles
→ Focus on automation via CI/CD & Continuous training pipelines.
→ Build keeping versioning, reproducibility, and monitoring in mind

8. MLOps Components
→ Get used to tools for orchestration, experiment tracking, and serving.
→ Implement lineage tracking and observability in ML systems.

9. Infrastructure as Code (IaC)
→ Define infrastructure with code using tools like Terraform.
→ Helps in automating and replicating deployment environments easily.

10. Keep Learning:
→ MLOps is constantly evolving—stay current with tools and best practices.
→ Continuous learning will help you staying ahead in AI and cloud innovation.

Why Cloud Engineers & DevOps Engineers Should Upskill in MLOps:

→ As a Cloud Engineer, you become the backbone of scalable ML infrastructure.
→ As a DevOps Engineer, you bridge the gap between data science and production by enabling automation and reliability.

Ok, I know it seems like a lot — you basically need to know everything to understand this practice.

But there’s a reason: MLOps sits at the intersection of Cloud, DevOps, Data, and AI — that’s why the work (and rewards) are next level.

Just keep practicing — it starts to click piece by piece.

--- Post 62 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Jan 17, 2026
Engagement: 1,566 reactions, 61 comments, 206 reposts (Score: 8,852)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7407239100149174273

If you’re working with Kubernetes in production, here are the deployment strategies you must know

~ not just for prod deployments
~ these show up a lot in Kubernetes scenario & system-design questions

1. Canary
→ Release to a small % of users first
→ Use when you want to validate behavior under real traffic with minimal risk

2. Blue-Green
→ Two identical environments, switch traffic instantly
→ Use when you need zero downtime and fast rollback

3. A/B
→ Route different users to different versions
→ Use when comparing features, UX, or experiments (not just releases)

4. Rolling
→ Gradually replace pods with new ones
→ Use for safe, default updates with no full outage

5. Recreate
→ Kill old pods, then start new ones
→ Use when versions cannot coexist (schema or state conflicts)

6. Shadow
→ Duplicate traffic without affecting users
→ Use to test performance, scaling, or ML models silently

Most Kubernetes interviews won’t ask you to define these.

They’ll ask why you chose one over the other under constraints.

--- Post 64 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Nov 18, 2025
Engagement: 1,539 reactions, 80 comments, 174 reposts (Score: 8,843)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7388600168117350400

If you work with Kubernetes, you should know why Ingress isn’t enough anymore ~ and what Gateway API fixes.

Whether you’re a Cloud Engineer, DevOps Engineer, or Platform Admin, here’s a step-by-step breakdown of both..
and how traffic actually flows..

**Ingress Flow
Client Request:
↳ A user request first hits a cloud load balancer created by the Ingress Controller.

Ingress Controller Pod:
↳ Manages routing and applies the rules defined in the Ingress resource (NGINX, GKE Ingress, etc.).

Ingress Resource:
↳ Defines routing paths for incoming requests - which service or path to send traffic to.

Service (ClusterIP / NodePort):
↳ Connects routing rules to the actual app inside the cluster.

Pods (Applications):
↳ The final destination ~ where containers handle the request.

In short:
Ingress offers a simpler, single-layer model.. one resource manages routing and load balancing inside the cluster.
------------

*Gateway Flow
Client Request:
↳ Traffic enters through a cloud load balancer (AWS, GCP, Azure) — managed by the Gateway Controller.

Gateway Controller Pod:
↳ Handles all Gateway configurations and keeps them in sync (Istio / Envoy / Cilium).

GatewayClass (Infra Provider):
↳ Created by infra teams ~ defines how Gateways are implemented in the cluster.

Gateway (Cluster Admin):
↳ Created by cluster admins; sets up listeners, TLS, and entry points for traffic.

HTTPRoutes (Developers):
↳ Created by app teams; define where traffic should go (to which services or namespaces).

In short:
Gateway splits responsibilities ~ infra teams handle setup, admins handle routing, and developers handle app paths.

So all in all ~ Gateway provides:
→ More structure: clear separation of roles .
→ More flexibility: supports multiple implementations (Istio, Envoy, Cilium) without rewriting manifests.
→ More consistency: a standardized API across clusters & cloud.

Hope this helps clarify the evolution from Ingress to Gateway ~
and why the shift matters as clusters scale.

--- Post 67 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Mar 18, 2026
Engagement: 1,590 reactions, 60 comments, 58 reposts (Score: 8,666)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7430664712540946432

The path from Docker to Kubernetes seems simple…
until you see all the switches 🫠 

With Docker, you've one track.
Build → Run → Ship.

You learn images. Containers. Volumes. Networking.

It clicks.

Then comes Kubernetes.
Now it’s Pods. Deployments. Services. Ingress. ConfigMaps. Secrets.
And a never-ending list…

Here’s the lesson:
Don’t jump to Kubernetes before you truly understand Docker.

Kubernetes doesn’t replace containers.
It orchestrates them.

If Docker is unclear, Kubernetes will feel overwhelming.
If Docker is solid, Kubernetes starts making sense.

Master the straight track first.
Then step into the rail yard.

Happy Friday.

• Focus on one service from each category (compute, storage, networking)

Remember: Cloud is vast. Master the basics first, then specialize.

Author: vsadhwani
Date: Feb 16, 2026

URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7419781485622247425

IaC - Terraform vs Pulumi vs Crossplane
They all provision infrastructure, but with different approaches.

If you're managing infrastructure today, understanding these three tools is essential. 

Also, the workflows, abstractions, and control mechanisms vary significantly.

Let's break down how each one differs 👇
Terraform 
→ Declarative IaC using HCL (HashiCorp Configuration Language) 
→ Plan → Apply → Manage state workflow 
→ Mature ecosystem with extensive provider support 
→ State management (local or remote) is critical

Pulumi 
→ Infrastructure as actual code ~ use TypeScript, Python, Go, C# 
→ Brings software engineering practices to infrastructure 
→ Type safety and compile-time checks built in 
→ Manages stacks with encryption for secrets

Crossplane 
→ Kubernetes-native control plane for infrastructure 
→ Treat infrastructure as Custom Resources (CRs) 
→ GitOps-first approach with continuous reconciliation 
→ Multi-cluster and multi-cloud orchestration at the platform level

From declarative configs 
→ to real programming languages 
→ to Kubernetes-native control planes

IaC is evolving ~ from scripts to code to control plane abstractions.

Now, which tool should you pick? 
It depends on your context. 
- Most teams start with Terraform for its maturity and ecosystem. 
- But if you're already deep in Kubernetes, Crossplane makes sense. 
- If you prefer real code over config, Pulumi shine. 

Master one first ~ the concepts transfer.
• • •

--- Post 74 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Jun 21, 2025
Engagement: 1,499 reactions, 70 comments, 135 reposts (Score: 8,465)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7338225147377561600

SRE != DevOps Engineer
(same tools - different usage)

Let's clarify this once and for all — SRE and DevOps Engineers are different roles, even if they use a lot of the same tools and platforms.

Their core purposes and day-to-day responsibilities diverge significantly.

Yes, there's an overlap in their foundational toolsets. 
Both roles utilize bash, python, cloud, IaC/configuration mgmt Tools - but their usage and purpose differ. 

- DevOps engineers leverage them primarily for infrastructure setup, application provisioning, and streamlining development workflows;
- While SREs focus on configuration management, asset discovery, ensuring system reliability, and handling stringent change control.

Here's the detailed breakdown:

SRE (Site Reliability Engineer) owns:
• System reliability and performance
• SLO/SLI definition and monitoring
• Capacity planning & configuration management
• Error budgets and risk management
• Application Performance Monitoring
• Automated Incident Response (Opsgenie, PagerDuty etc)
• Going through stringent scrutiny for change requests.
Working mostly in cloud environments.... focusing on system reliability at scale.

DevOps Engineers focus on:
• CI/CD pipeline optimization
• Infrastructure as Code (IaC)
• Deployment automation
• Cross-team collaboration
• Monitoring and observability
• Infrastructure setup, application setup, and provisioning of infrastructure resources for app development.

Furthermore, each role possesses a distinct set of specialized tools crucial for their primary objectives – CI/CD and Containerization for DevOps, and APM and Incident Response Systems for SRE.

Here's the interesting part:
Modern engineering teams need both, but for different reasons.

The key distinction?
SRE is about RELIABILITY.
DevOps is about CULTURE & AUTOMATION.

No matter the role, our shared mission is to prevent production issues. It's a big, interconnected picture, and every piece matters. Hope this clarifies the landscape a bit.

What's your take on how these roles are evolving?

Author: vsadhwani
Date: Nov 18, 2025

2025 is ending in less than 60 days.
If you’re still searching for your first cloud role, you’re not late ~ but you can’t afford another directionless month.

Every week focuses on one thing, one outcome, and one set of hands-on tasks.
Here’s the high-level breakdown:

Pre-Work (Foundations)
The basics : Linux, Git, networking fundamentals, SDLC, and cloud service models.

Week 1 : Cloud Foundations
Understand how the cloud actually works: regions, zones, compute, storage, networking, IAM, and billing.

Week 2 : Linux & Networking for Cloud
Learn how servers talk. SSH, EC2/VMs, AMIs, VPC/VNet, subnets, routing, ports, DNS, and security groups.

Week 3 : Infrastructure as Code
Stop clicking, start automating. Terraform basics, providers, variables, modules, and state.

Week 4 : Containers & Kubernetes
Containerize apps and run them anywhere. Dockerfiles, images, pods, deployments, services.

Week 5 : CI/CD Pipelines
Automate builds, tests, deployments.
GitHub Actions, Jenkins basics, and a simple pipeline that actually deploys something.

Week 6 : Cloud Security Essentials
IAM, least privilege, secrets, KMS, CloudTrail, audit logs.
The minimum security knowledge every cloud engineer needs.

Week 7 : Monitoring & AIOps
Metrics, logs, traces, dashboards, alerts, and where AIOps fits in.
Prometheus, Grafana, CloudWatch basics.

Week 8 : Final Project & Portfolio
Bring everything together: IaC + Docker + K8s + CI/CD + security + monitoring.

If you’ve been consuming cloud content without a clear plan, this roadmap gives you structure, order, and focus ~ with free resources for every week.

Check it out here: https://lnkd.in/dQ2zBGxB

 ↳ Advanced: AWS SA Pro, GCP Cloud Architect, Azure Solutions Architect Expert

 ↳ Intermediate: AWS ML Specialty, GCP Professional ML Engineer, Azure AI Engineer Associate
 ↳ Advanced: Azure SA Expert, AWS SA Pro, GCP Cloud Architect

 ↳ Advanced: AWS DevOps Engineer Pro, GCP DevOps Engineer Pro, Azure DevOps Expert

If you’re pursuing Cloud DevOps, here’s a structured YouTube tutorial roadmap you can follow.

Watching one random video today and another tomorrow, with no clear path, can seriously slow your progress.

If you want a structured, fundamentals-first learning path, these YouTube resources can take you from zero to job-ready concepts
(if you practice alongside).

Start with the basics:

1. Networking (non-negotiable)
↳ https://lnkd.in/gexmDWuC

2. Shell Scripting (daily DevOps muscle)
↳ https://lnkd.in/ghEfBi77

Containers & Orchestration:

3. Docker (build once, run anywhere)
↳ https://lnkd.in/gzXhXTgH

4. Kubernetes (how modern infrastructure actually runs)
↳ https://lnkd.in/gdU2jTe3

Cloud Platforms (pick one first):

5. AWS ~ Zero to Hero
↳ https://lnkd.in/gdCxkaU3

6. Google Cloud ~ Crash Course
↳ https://lnkd.in/g2cPd3CK

7. Azure Fundamentals
↳ https://lnkd.in/g6yj6PCV

Infrastructure & Observability:

8. Terraform & Infrastructure as Code
↳ https://lnkd.in/gQqzujZX

9. Prometheus & Grafana (monitor what you deploy)
↳ https://lnkd.in/g24HVdCB
↳ https://lnkd.in/ggvCbPyX

Key takeaway:
Don’t try to learn everything at once.
Learn one layer deeply, then stack the next.

Networking → Linux → Containers → Cloud → IaC → Observability.

If you’re consistent and actually build while learning, this path is more than enough to break into Cloud/DevOps.

If you’re early in your journey, save this.

5 kubernetes certification paths you should consider if you're aiming for cloud devops roles
(and what to expect from each certification)

Whether you're starting out or moving deeper into cloud-native infrastructure, k8s certs can help structure your learning and signal your expertise.

Here’s a role-based breakdown to help you pick the right learning path:

1. Cloud Native Newbie (Beginner)
 Start with the Kubernetes and Cloud Native Associate (KCNA)
 – Best suited for those just entering the cloud-native ecosystem
 – Learn basic Kubernetes architecture, container orchestration, and CNCF projects

2. Application Developer
 KCNA | CKAD
 – Focuses on deploying and managing applications on Kubernetes
 – Emphasis on workloads, config maps, secrets, and Helm

3. System Administrator
 KCNA | CKA
 – Targeted at infrastructure engineers managing clusters
 – Covers networking, RBAC, scheduling, cluster setup and maintenance

4. Security Specialist
 KCNA | Kubernetes Security Fundamentals | Certified Kubernetes Security Specialist (CKS)
 – Advanced track focused on pod security, policies, runtime protection, and cluster hardening
 – Ideal for cloud security and compliance roles
 – You will need CKA before CKS though.

5. Platform Engineer / DevOps Engineer / Site Reliability Engineer / Architect
 KCNA | CKAD | CKA | CKS
 – Comprehensive learning path covering application delivery, infrastructure operations, and security
 – Designed for those managing CI/CD pipelines, automation, monitoring, and high-availability systems

These are all OR options, you can pick based on you requirements (except CKS - that requires CKA)

How to Prepare Effectively
 – Follow curated GitHub repos tied to each cert and role
 – PRACTICE WITH LABS >>>>
 – For paid courses, check when they were last updated and read learner reviews
 – Use cloud provider free tiers to gain hands-on experience with clusters
 – Maintain short notes on key configuration commands and yaml templates

I'll be sharing a curated list of resources (both free and vetted paid courses) — for these certifications this Thursday in my newsletter : https://lnkd.in/dBNJPv9U

Make sure you're subscribed so you don't miss it.

A few final thoughts:
 – Follow the right sequence: start with foundational (associate) certifications before moving to advanced (specialist)
 – Choose your learning path based on your target role, not what's currently trending
 – Certifications are useful (also expensive), and doesn’t guarantee jobs - so take their learning paths to upskill. And take the cert when you think it’s the right time.

Good luck!

Kubernetes layers you need to know as a DevOps engineer

K8s applications isn't just about containers - it's about the EXTRA layers that make it enterprise-ready

1. Load Balancer:
→ This is the entry point to the architecture.
→ It helps distribute incoming network traffic across multiple backend servers & pods
→ Ensures high availability and fault tolerance.
→ Prevents any single server from being overwhelmed, ensuring even load distribution

2. Ingress Controller:
→ It acts as a gateway managing external access to services within the cluster.
→ It's crucial for controlling and routing HTTP/S traffic.
→ Gateway APIs are a replacement for Ingress, with multi-protocol support, better delegation, and extensibility

3. Kube Proxy:
→ This maintains network rules and handles forwarding of traffic and communication between pods.

4. Sidecars:
→ Sidecars are an extension pattern in Kubernetes, where additional containers run alongside the main application container within the same Pod.
→ They help in extending and enhancing the functionalities of the main app, such as logging, monitoring, etc., without modifying its code.

5. Service Mesh:
→ Service Mesh introduces a dedicated infrastructure layer that adds features like service-to-service communication, mTLS security, observability, traffic management, and more.
→ This is increasingly important for managing microservices architectures.

Knowing these components is key to crafting robust, scalable, and maintainable Kubernetes applications.

If you’re preparing for a Cloud / DevOps role in 2026, you need to understand architecture patterns.. not just tools.

As a Cloud Engineer, the next leap isn’t another certification…

it’s knowing when to use which pattern, and why.

Different systems need different structures.
But one thing remains common: design decisions impact scale, cost, and reliability.

Let’s break down 7 Cloud Architecture Patterns you must know 👇🏻

Microservices Architecture
→ Breaks large apps into small, independent services.
→ Enables independent deployment, scaling, and team ownership.

Event-Driven Architecture
→ Components react to events asynchronously.
→ Ideal for loosely coupled, real-time systems.

Sidecar Pattern
→ Adds helper containers alongside the main app container.
→ Offloads logging, networking, security without modifying core code.

Strangler Fig Pattern
→ Gradually replaces legacy systems piece by piece.
→ Minimizes risk during modernization.

Horizontal Scaling (Sharding)
→ Splits load/data across multiple nodes or databases.
→ Designed for high-throughput, global-scale systems.

Serverless Architecture
→ Runs code on demand without managing servers.
→ Great for unpredictable workloads and rapid development.

API Gateway Pattern
→ Centralizes all client requests through one entry point.
→ Handles routing, authentication, throttling, and security.

From monoliths → to microservices
From synchronous calls → to event streams
From single servers → to distributed systems

These architecture patterns aren't about trends.
It’s about choosing the right pattern for the problem.

Want to switch from Software engineer to Cloud engineer role in 6 months?
Study this roadmap:

You already know the SDLC processes – now dive into how to build, run and deploy cloud-native apps

3-Month Foundation :
 • Learn core Linux (permissions, systemd, logging)
 • Understand networking (VPC, DNS, NAT, firewalls)
 • Pick a cloud provider (AWS / GCP / Azure / OCI)
 ↳ Focus on IAM, Compute, Storage, networking basics, billing fundamentals etc
 • Learn Python for automation (already familiar? think how you can use with Boto3, SDKs, scripting)
 • Learn Infrastructure as Code (Terraform / Pulumi/ AWS Cloudformation)
 • Explore cloud-native patterns (microservices, managed services)
 ↳ Target: Junior Cloud Engineer roles (ave comp ~$100k)

You already know Git — now use it to:
• Version control your Terraform infrastructure
• Track changes to IaC and cloud automation scripts
• Set up GitOps-style deployment patterns

3-Month Advanced Track :
• Pick a specialty (Networking, Data, Databases, DevOps, Security, AI/ML etc)
If you picked DevOps:
• Work with containers (build/run with Docker)
• Deploy apps using Kubernetes (pods, services, configs)
• Set up CI/CD pipelines (using cloud-specific services)
• Implement cloud security concepts (IAM, roles, secrets management)
• Configure monitoring & logging (Cloud-Specific or Prometheus/Grafana)
• Automate end-to-end workflows(from build to deploy) using Python or Shell (SDKs, scripts, cron) - [this is very advanced]
 ↳ Target: Mid-level Cloud Engineer roles (ave comp ~$125k)

Leverage your SWE skills:
 • Containerize existing apps
 • Build pipelines for test → deploy
 • Refactor into cloud-native services

Quick Wins:
- Complete Qwiklabs or provider-specific cloud labs
- Sign up for free tiers and experiment (watch your credit limits)
- Build and deploy 3 cloud-native projects
- Earn a practitioner or specialty certification (based on your tech exposure)

Free Resources to start, here's your one stop shop: https://lnkd.in/dF9xVE9X

Do this next: You've already built apps - now think which cloud-based services you'd need to run it on cloud (deploy it with Terraform + CI/CD)

Hands-on experience >> watching videos or reading blogs.

Remember, you're not starting from scratch. As a Software Engineer, you already think in systems.
Now think in cloud systems

What worked for you in your cloud journey?

** Azure
3. End-to-End Project with Terraform using Azure DevOps ( Intermediate)
↳ Build enterprise-grade Azure infrastructure with Terraform and automate deployments using Azure DevOps pipelines.
↳ Learn real-world patterns: resource groups, VNets, AKS clusters, and more.

4. Realtime DevOps Project using Azure DevOps and GitOps (Beginner to Advanced)
↳ Explore GitOps practices with Azure DevOps, automating infrastructure and application deployments.

Few suggestions here who's starting new:
- Be consistent and focus on mastering one cloud provider before moving to others.
- Use free credits from AWS, Azure, or GCP to build these projects in a real cloud environment.
- Running projects for 2–4 hours should cost under $5 — just remember to delete your resources afterward.
- Document everything in a GitHub repo and add the link to your resume to show your hands-on skills.

You won’t become a Cloud Engineer if you don’t understand how the modern software stack works end-to-end..

Here’s how applications actually flow 👇

→ Presentation Layer (UI/UX)
↳ The first touchpoint: where users interact with your app.
Modern examples: React + Next.js, Angular, Flutter (for cross-platform), SwiftUI (iOS), Kotlin Compose (Android)

→ Edge & Delivery
↳ Accelerates and secures global content delivery — your app’s “front shield” for latency and security.
Modern examples: Cloudflare (Edge Functions, CDN, WAF), AWS CloudFront, Akamai, Fastly

→ Integration Layer (API)
↳ Connects the front-end with back-end services and enables microservices to talk seamlessly.
Modern examples: API Gateway (AWS/GCP), GraphQL (Apollo, Hasura), REST APIs with OpenAPI/Swagger, gRPC, WebSocket

→ Messaging & Async Processing
↳ Handles event-driven and background workflows ~ essential for scale and decoupling.
Modern examples: Kafka, RabbitMQ, AWS SQS + SNS, Google Pub/Sub, Redis Streams, Celery, KEDA (for K8s event-driven scaling)

→ Business Logic Layer
↳ The brain of your system — where requests are processed and core workflows run.
Modern examples: Node.js (Express, NestJS), Python (FastAPI, Django), Java (Spring Boot), .NET Core, Go, Serverless Functions

→ Data Access Layer
↳ The optimized bridge between app logic and data stores — caching, ORM, and search layers.
Modern examples: Redis (cache), Elasticsearch / OpenSearch, Prisma, SQLAlchemy, Hibernate

→ Data Storage Layer
↳ Where data actually lives — structured, unstructured, or analytical.
Modern examples: PostgreSQL, MySQL, MongoDB, DynamoDB, BigQuery, Snowflake, ClickHouse, Firebase Firestore

→ Analytics & ML
↳ Turns data into intelligence — metrics, predictions, personalization, and automation.
Modern examples: Spark, PyTorch, TensorFlow, Vertex AI, SageMaker, Looker, Power BI, LangChain + Vector DBs 

→ Infrastructure Layer (Hosting / Runtime)
↳ The foundation where everything runs — cloud, containers, orchestration, and DevOps glue.
Modern examples: AWS, Azure, GCP, OCI, Docker, Kubernetes, Terraform, Helm, Argo CD

It’s easy to get lost chasing the “next big thing” ~ AI, agents, or LLMs.

But before plugging any of these in, understand the modern stack’s flow —
because without the fundamentals, you won’t know where or how they actually fit.

--- Post 88 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Nov 18, 2025
Engagement: 1,366 reactions, 54 comments, 243 reposts (Score: 7,856)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7394411375235633152

If you’re ramping up with Kubernetes.. here’s the advanced K8s stack you must know.

You’ve deployed apps on Kubernetes.
Now it’s time to understand how to secure, scale, and automate them.

Here’s a quick breakdown of how to understand advanced Kubernetes, broken down into key categories.

1. Cluster Management & Scaling
↳ Cluster Autoscaler, KEDA, Cluster API, Multi-Cluster Federation, GPU Scheduling, Cluster Upgrades
↳ When to understand ~ To manage large-scale clusters, automate scaling, and optimize node utilization.

2. Observability & Reliability
↳ Prometheus, Grafana, Loki / Tempo, OpenTelemetry, Event Exporter, Chaos Engineering, SLO Dashboards
↳ When to understand ~ To monitor metrics, logs, and traces — and ensure your clusters are reliable under stress.

3. GitOps & Platform Engineering
↳ ArgoCD, FluxCD, Crossplane, Helm / Kustomize, Backstage, Policy as Code, Environment Provisioning
↳ When to understand ~ To automate application delivery, enforce standards, and create self-service developer platforms.

4. Networking & Traffic Control
↳ Ingress Controllers, Gateway API, Service Mesh, CNI Plugins, Network Policies, eBPF Observability, API Gateway
↳ When to understand ~ To control service communication, routing, and traffic security across clusters.

5. Workload Optimization
↳ HPA / VPA, Resource Quotas, Node Affinity / Anti-Affinity, Taints & Tolerations, Pod Priority, GPU Operator, Scheduling Profiles
↳ When to understand ~ To balance performance, reliability, and cost — ensuring workloads run efficiently.

6. Storage & Stateful Workloads
↳ CSI Drivers, Dynamic Provisioning, StatefulSets, Persistent Volumes, RWX Storage, Volume Snapshots, Backup & Restore
↳ When to understand ~ To manage data persistence, storage scalability, and stateful applications seamlessly.

7. Security & Governance
↳ Pod Security Admission, OPA Gatekeeper, Kyverno, Secrets Management, gVisor, Runtime Security
↳ When to understand ~ To safeguard workloads, enforce policies, and maintain runtime isolation in production.

8. Extensibility
↳ Custom Controllers, Admission Webhooks, CRDs, API Aggregation, Sidecar Injection, Serverless Frameworks
↳ When to understand ~ To extend Kubernetes capabilities and build tailored automation or integrations.

Now know this:
You don’t need to master everything.. just understand which layer solves what problem and when to use it.

Once that clicks, the entire cloud-native ecosystem starts to make sense.

Here's a breakdown of learning Kubernetes in 11 practical steps.

But first, why this matters ↓
Getting good at K8s isn’t about memorizing commands or copying YAML.
It’s about understanding how the pieces fit together.

This structure helps you do exactly that.

Step 1: Start with the Foundations
↳ What Kubernetes manages
↳ Pods, Nodes, Clusters, Services, Deployments, Control Plane

Step 2: Set Up Your Environment
↳ Where Kubernetes runs
↳ Local clusters(Minikube, kind) vs managed cloud (EKS / GKE / AKS)

Step 3: Deploy & Manage Applications
↳ How real workloads run
↳ Workloads: Deployments, StatefulSets, DaemonSets, Jobs, CronJobs 
↳ Config: Kustomize, Rolling Updates, Labels/Selectors

Step 4: Storage & Configuration
↳ Separate code from config and data
↳ Storage: PV, PVC, StorageClasses, CSI 
↳ Config: ConfigMaps, Secrets, Volume Mounts

Step 5: Networking & Security
↳ How traffic flows inside and outside the cluster
↳ Network: Ingress, CNI (Calico/Cilium), Service Mesh (Istio/Linkerd) 
↳ Security: RBAC, Network Policies, Secret Management

Step 6: Autoscaling & Resources
↳ How Kubernetes reacts to load
↳ Autoscaling(HPA, VPA), KEDA, resource limits, scheduling, GPU Operator 
↳ Advanced: Affinity, Taints/Tolerations

Step 7: Helm & Operators
↳ How teams package and manage complexity
↳ Charts, templates, CRDs, controllers

Step 8: AI / ML on Kubernetes
↳ Why Kubernetes dominates ML platforms
↳ Training, serving, GPUs, ML pipelines

Step 9: Observability
↳ If you can’t see it, you can’t operate it
↳ Metrics, logs, traces, GPU & model monitoring 
(Prometheus, Grafana, Alertmanager, Loki)

Step 10: GitOps & CI/CD
↳ How changes reach production safely
↳ GitOps: ArgoCD, FluxCD 
↳ Delivery: Canary, Blue-Green 
↳ Pipelines: Argo Workflows, GitHub Actions 
↳ ML CI/CD: Continuous Training, Model Versioning

Step 11: Production & Advanced Concepts
↳ Running Kubernetes at scale
↳ Multi-cluster, security, cost control, platform engineering
↳ Certs: KCNA, CKA, CKAD, CKS

Could feel overwhelming..but here’s the key takeaway ↓

- You don’t learn Kubernetes by learning everything together.
- You start really understanding it when you approach it structurally.
Instead of jumping into advanced concepts without a solid foundation.

Focus on why each layer exists, not just how to use it.

What else would you add?

If you're just getting started or looking to level up in DevOps, here’s what to focus on:

1/ Fundamentals
 ↳ Understand the basics: SDLC, Agile, CI/CD, Monitoring, IaC
 ↳ Explore: Jira, Lucidchart, Draw.io for planning & visualization

2/ Learn a Programming Language
 ↳ Write automation scripts to reduce manual effort
 ↳ Start with: Shell, Python, or Go
 ↳ Use editors like: VS Code, PyCharm, Bash

3/ Version Control
 ↳ Track changes, collaborate with teams, stay consistent
 ↳ Tools to explore: Git, GitHub, GitLab, Bitbucket

4/ OS & Networking Basics
 ↳ Get comfortable with Linux, ports, DNS, and firewalls
 ↳ Try platforms/tools like: Ubuntu, CentOS, Wireshark, Netstat

5/ Configuration Management
 ↳ Automate environment setups & system configurations
 ↳ Tools/frameworks: Ansible, Chef, Puppet, SaltStack, Cloud SSM,

6/ Continuous Integration (CI)
 ↳ Automate testing and integration workflows
 ↳ Tools/platforms: GitLab CI, Jenkins, CircleCI
 ↳ IaC frameworks that support CI: Terraform, Pulumi, AWS CDK

7/ IaC & Cloud Platform
 ↳ Automate provisioning of cloud infrastructure
 ↳ Tools/frameworks: Terraform, Pulumi, AWS CloudFormation

8/ Containerization
 ↳ Package apps and dependencies into isolated containers
 ↳ Tools: Docker, Podman, Docker Compose

9/ Container Orchestration
 ↳ Manage containers at scale in production
 ↳ Platforms/tools: Kubernetes, Helm, OpenShift

10/ Security & Compliance
 ↳ Secure your pipeline, code, and infrastructure
 ↳ Tools: Trivy, Aqua, SonarQube, Vault

11/ Monitoring & Logging
 ↳ Track system health and performance
 ↳ Tools/platforms: Prometheus, Grafana, ELK Stack, Datadog

12/ Continuous Delivery (CD)
 ↳ Automate deployment to production
 ↳ Tools: ArgoCD, GitLab CD, Spinnaker

13/ Cloud Platforms (Deep Dive)
 ↳ Get hands-on with public cloud deployments
 ↳ Start with one: AWS, Azure, GCP, or OCI

14/ Collaboration & Documentation
 ↳ Make teamwork seamless and transparent
 ↳ Tools: Notion, Slack, Confluence, Miro

15/ Advanced Topics
 ↳ Go deeper with: MLOps, DevSecOps, SRE, Chaos Engineering
 ↳ Platforms/frameworks: MLFlow, FluxCD, Istio, Thanos
