Check out this practical cheatsheet to prep for the Cloud DevOps portion of your interviews
(with must-know concepts, tools, and use-cases)

1. Infrastructure as Code (IaC)
 → Tools: Terraform, CloudFormation, ARM/Bicep, Pulumi
 → Concepts: Declarative provisioning, version-controlled infra
 Use Case: Define and deploy all infrastructure (compute, network, IAM, storage) using code for consistency and automation.

2. CI/CD Pipelines
 → Tools: GitHub Actions, Jenkins, CodePipeline, Cloud Build
 → Concepts: Pipelines as Code, automated deployments
 Use Case: Automate building, testing, and deploying applications to cloud environments (VMs, containers, serverless).

3. Configuration & Patch Management
 → Tools: Cloud-Based SSMs, Ansible, Chef
 → Concepts: Desired state, golden images, automated patching
 Use Case: Manage and enforce configuration compliance across virtual machines and services at scale.

4. Cloud Container Orchestration
 → Services: EKS, GKE, AKS
 → Tools: Helm, Kustomize, App Mesh
 Use Case: Deploy, manage, and scale containerized applications on Kubernetes platforms with built-in monitoring and autoscaling.

5. Serverless DevOps (FaaS & BaaS)
 → Platforms: AWS Lambda, Firebase, AppSync
 → Concepts: Managed runtimes, event-driven compute
 Use Case: Build and deploy scalable APIs, functions, and backend services without managing servers.

6. Cloud Release Strategies
 → Strategies: Blue/Green, Canary, Feature Flags
 → Tools: Cloud Build, CodeDeploy, Spinnaker
 Use Case: Enable safe, progressive deployments using cloud-native traffic control methods for zero-downtime releases.

7. Cloud Observability
 → Tools: Cloud Monitoring, CloudWatch, Datadog etc
 → Concepts: Logs, metrics, traces, centralized telemetry
 Use Case: Monitor health and performance of cloud-native systems; troubleshoot failures and performance bottlenecks.

8. Cloud Cost Optimization (FinOps)
 → Tools: AWS Budgets, Azure Cost Management, Kubecost
 → Concepts: Cost tagging, rightsizing, auto-shutdown
 Use Case: Analyze and reduce cloud spend through visibility, optimization strategies, and policy-based cost controls.

9. DevSecOps for Cloud
 → Tools: OPA, Sentinel, AWS Config, Cloud WAF
 → Concepts: Secure IaC, policy enforcement, automated audits
 Use Case: Integrate security into every stage of the CI/CD pipeline and enforce compliance through code and automation.

Instead of memorizing tools — think in use cases.

My advice to study Cloud DevOps:
 → Understand the core building blocks (IaC, CI/CD, Containers, Serverless)
 → Learn how they integrate in real-world architectures - what used when?

I’ll be covering these Cloud DevOps topics in depth — with examples — in this week’s newsletter (dropping Thursday)

--- Post 98 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Feb 16, 2026
Engagement: 1,264 reactions, 42 comments, 222 reposts (Score: 7,184)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7422322752918847488

If you work with Kubernetes, here’s what you need to know about the shift from Ingress to Gateway API.

Whether you’re a Cloud Engineer, DevOps Engineer, or Platform Admin, here’s a step-by-step breakdown of both..
and how traffic actually flows..

**Ingress Flow
Client Request:
↳ enters through a cloud load balancer created by the Ingress Controller.

Ingress Controller Pod:
↳ manages routing and applies the rules defined in the Ingress resource.

Ingress Resource:
↳ defines routing paths for incoming requests - which service or path to send traffic to.

Service (ClusterIP / NodePort):
↳ connects routing rules to the actual app inside the cluster.

Pods (Applications):
↳ the final destination ~ where containers handle the request.

In short:
Ingress offers a simpler, single-layer model.. one resource manages routing and load balancing inside the cluster.
------------

*Gateway Flow
Client Request:
↳ traffic enters through a cloud load balancer (AWS, GCP, Azure) ~ managed by the Gateway Controller.

Gateway Controller Pod:
↳ handles all Gateway configurations and keeps them in sync

GatewayClass (Infra Provider):
↳ defines how Gateways are implemented in the cluster.

Gateway (Cluster Admin):
↳ sets up listeners, TLS, and entry points for traffic.

HTTPRoutes (Developers):
↳ define where traffic should go (to which services or namespaces).

So all in all ~ Gateway API provides:
→ More structure: clear separation of roles .
→ More flexibility: supports multiple implementations (Istio, Envoy, Cilium)
→ More consistency: a standardized API across clusters & cloud.

Hope this helps clarify the evolution from Ingress to Gateway ~
and why the shift matters as clusters scale.

If you're getting into cloud and want to map networking components across AWS, Azure, and GCP — this one’s for you.

Whether you're prepping for a certification, planning a cloud migration, or designing a multi-cloud architecture, knowing "how" each provider stacks up is essential.

Here’s what it covers:
→ Network Foundation
 From Amazon VPC to Azure VNet and GCP’s VPC, all three offer isolated virtual networks to host and protect your workloads.

→ Traffic Distribution
 Handle scale like a pro using ELB (AWS), Application Gateway (Azure), or GCP’s global/regional load balancers to keep apps smooth and responsive.

→ Threat Protection & Network Security
 DDoS? Malicious traffic? Compare AWS Shield, Azure Firewall, and Cloud Armor to see how each provider helps secure your perimeter.

→ Private Connectivity & VPNs
 Need secure hybrid connectivity? Think AWS Direct Connect, Azure ExpressRoute, or GCP Interconnect for private, low-latency links.

→ Monitoring & Logging
 Visibility matters. Use CloudWatch (AWS), Azure Monitor, or GCP Network Intelligence Center to keep an eye on your network.

This cheatsheet gives you a clear, side-by-side comparison of the key networking elements across the big three—making it easier to design, plan, or simply learn.

To understand these network components in detail, check this out : https://lnkd.in/dbyQ9uGE

Bookmark it if you're diving into cloud networking — this is one of those resources you’ll come back to.

If you’re preparing for Kubernetes certifications, be sure to check out this alias cheat sheet..

Why aliases matter in certification exams (especially K8s):

↳ Time is your biggest constraint in exams like the CKA, CKAD or CKS.
↳ Creating short aliases (e.g., alias k='kubectl', alias kgp='kubectl get pods') can save you hundreds of keystrokes.
↳ Many candidates who don't pass run out of time — not knowledge.
↳ You’re allowed to set up aliases in your shell configuration at the beginning of the exam — and it’s highly recommended.

While preparing:
- Practice with aliases daily to build muscle memory.
- Set up aliases in your .bashrc or .zshrc and use them exclusively during practice.
- Keep a personal cheat sheet of kubectl commands and common flags — memorize as much as possible.
- Streamline your workflow to stay focused on problem-solving, not TYPING.
- Memorize any pre-written helper functions you plan to use.

Any other tips you'd add?

If you’re starting in cloud, networking is both crucial and challenging — here’s what you need to know (with free resources)

Let’s dive into the main components:

→ VPC (Virtual Private Cloud)
↳ Provides an isolated environment in the cloud.
↳ The foundation where your applications and resources are hosted.

→ Subnets
↳ Break your VPC into smaller segments.
↳ Used to separate workloads — e.g., databases in private subnets, applications in public ones.

→ Routing
↳ Directs traffic between subnets, networks, and the internet.
↳ Ensures services can talk to each other efficiently.

→ Firewalls / Security Groups
↳ Control inbound and outbound traffic.
↳ Critical for securing applications and restricting unauthorized access.

→ Load Balancing
↳ Distributes traffic across multiple servers.
↳ Ensures high availability, fault tolerance, and performance at scale.

→ CDN (Content Delivery Network)
↳ Places content closer to users through edge servers.
↳ Reduces latency and improves global performance.

The bigger picture:
Each of these layers works together to make applications secure, reliable, and performant.

Youtube Videos to review:
1. Cloud Networking Basics: https://lnkd.in/dSVCUw2B
2. Google Cloud Networking Course : https://lnkd.in/dEkbayA2

Projects to practice:
↳ Deploy a web app inside a VPC with public + private subnets
↳ Add a load balancer and simulate traffic spikes
↳ Use a CDN to serve static assets faster

Note: This is not an exhaustive list. Other components — like VPNs, gateways, and interconnects — become important as you go deeper. 

Once you’re comfortable with the basics, that’s where you can specialize further.

Author: vsadhwani

Kubernetes layers you need to know as a Cloud DevOps Engineer

K8s applications aren’t just about containers ~ they’re about the EXTRA layers that make them enterprise-ready.

1. Load Balancer:
→ This is the entry point to your architecture.
→ It distributes incoming network traffic across backend servers & pods.
→ Ensures high availability and fault tolerance.
→ Prevents any single pod from being overwhelmed by load.

2. Ingress Controller & Gateway API:
→ Ingress acts as the HTTP/S gateway managing external access to services within the cluster.
→ The new Gateway API goes a step further — supporting multiple protocols (HTTP, TCP, gRPC), role-based delegation, and better extensibility.
→ Together, they help unify traffic control across internal and external systems - a must for modern, multi-tenant architectures.

3. Kube Proxy:
→ Maintains network rules and forwards traffic between pods and services.
→ Handles communication consistency even as pods scale up or down.

4. Sidecars:
→ Sidecars are helper containers running alongside your main app within the same Pod.
→ They extend app functionality ~ logging, monitoring, caching, etc.. without touching the core code.

5. Service Mesh:
→ Adds an infrastructure layer for managing service-to-service communication.
→ Enables traffic management, mTLS security, observability, and fault injection.
→ Critical for scaling microservices safely and predictably.

Knowing these layers helps you build resilient, scalable, and production-grade Kubernetes systems .. not just clusters that “work.”

If you're working in cloud, here are 7 architecture patterns you need to know..
(plus when each one makes sense to use)

Building in the cloud is all about designing systems that are scalable, resilient, and easy to evolve. These patterns help you do exactly that.

They’re battle-tested strategies used by modern teams to solve real-world challenges ~ like handling millions of users, avoiding downtime, or replacing legacy systems without a total rewrite.

Here are 7 patterns worth knowing:

1. Microservices Architecture
 ↳ Break large apps into small, independent services.
 ↳ Each service handles a specific task and communicates via APIs.
 ↳ When to use ~ When you want independent deployment, scaling, or teams owning different parts of the system.

2. Event-Driven Architecture
 ↳ Let components react to events asynchronously.
 ↳ Events (like user sign-ups or file uploads) trigger workflows automatically.
 ↳ When to use ~ For loosely coupled systems that require real-time or reactive flows.

3. Sidecar Pattern
 ↳ Pair helper containers with your main app container.
 ↳ Offloads tasks like logging, service discovery, or networking.
 ↳ When to use ~ In Kubernetes/microservices for clean separation of concerns.

4. Strangler Fig Pattern
 ↳ Replace legacy systems one piece at a time.
 ↳ Gradually route functionality from the old system to new microservices.
 ↳ When to use ~ During legacy modernization with minimal disruption.

5. Database Sharding / Horizontal Scaling
 ↳ Split data across multiple databases or nodes.
 ↳ Each shard holds a subset of data for better performance.
 ↳ When to use ~ For high-throughput systems and global-scale applications.

6. Serverless Architecture
 ↳ Run code on-demand without managing servers.
 ↳ Functions are triggered by events (like HTTP calls or file uploads).
 ↳ When to use ~ For unpredictable workloads, quick development, or cost-efficiency.

7. API Gateway Pattern
 ↳ Centralize all client requests through one gateway.
 ↳ Acts as a single entry point, handling routing, auth, throttling, etc.
 ↳ When to use ~ To simplify client-server communication and secure backend services.

Now know this ~ these patterns are the backbone of high-performing, cloud-native apps at top tech companies. Understanding their pros, cons, and use cases can give you a real edge in interviews.

If you’re looking to practice DevSecOps — here are 2 projects you should definitely check out..
(and the key processes you should know)

TL;DR : DevSecOps = DevOps + Security, built in from the start.

When I started exploring this practice, I realized I was already using parts of it in my day-to-day work. 
The security layer wasn’t just about adding tools — it was about thinking end-to-end across the whole DevOps workflow.

Here are the few key components:
→ Security Checks & Scans
Catch issues early with automated code and app security tests.

→ Vulnerability Management
Scan, prioritize, and patch vulnerabilities regularly.

→ Threat Modeling
Identify possible risks and plan mitigations before release.

→ Key Management
Keep secrets, API keys, and certificates secure.

→ CI/CD with Security
Automate builds and deployments with security gates built in.

→ Infrastructure as Code (IaC)
Define infra in code for consistency and secure provisioning.

→ Container Security
Scan images and protect containers during runtime.

→ Continuous Monitoring
Track logs, activity, and network traffic for anomalies.

→ QA Integration & Collaboration
Embed QA and make security part of team culture.

If you're aiming to become a Cloud Engineer — check out this roadmap!

This role goes beyond simply learning tools ~
It's about understanding how all the components work together to design, build, and deploy effective cloud systems.

Here’s a simple breakdown of the key areas you should focus on:

1/ Core Service Models
 IaaS, PaaS, SaaS
 ↳ Understand the foundational layers of the cloud stack.

2/ Compute & Storage
 VMs, Containers, Serverless + Storage Types
 ↳ Choose the right compute & storage for performance and cost.

3/ Networking & Delivery
 VPCs, Subnets, VPNs, API Gateway, CDN
 ↳ Build secure, performant, and scalable connections.

4/ Security & Compliance
 IAM, Encryption, Compliance (GDPR, HIPAA)
 ↳ Protect data and meet regulatory requirements.

5/ Architecture & Design
 Scalability, Resilience, Microservices
 ↳ Design systems that handle growth and failure gracefully.

6/ DevOps & Automation
 Terraform, Git, CI/CD, Infra as Code
 ↳ Automate infrastructure and streamline deployments.

7/ Cloud Observability
 Logging, Monitoring, Tracing, Analytics
 ↳ Detect, diagnose, and prevent issues early.

8/ Data & Analytics
 Pipelines, Warehousing, Kafka, BigQuery
 ↳ Move, store, and analyze data effectively.

9/ AI & ML in the Cloud
 SageMaker, Vertex AI, MLOps
 ↳ Integrate intelligent systems into your stack.

10/ Cost Optimization
 Auto-scaling, Right-sizing, Spot Instances
 ↳ Build smart to save money.

11/ Governance & Strategy
 Tagging, Policies, Multi-cloud Management
 ↳ Align cloud with business and maintain control at scale.

Beyond cloud services themselves - cloud engineers also focus on seamless integration with on-premise infrastructure, multi-cloud setups, various open-source tools, and other critical systems - this makes the field incredibly diverse.

I went through a recent DevOps job analysis report — and the hiring patterns were surprisingly clear.

→ Picking one cloud provider is more than enough to start ~ what truly matters is learning the underlying concepts.
↳ Once you understand IAM, networking, and infra automation at a foundational level, you can translate those skills across Azure, GCP, or any other platform without starting from scratch.

Other key observations:
→ Jenkins still dominates enterprise CI/CD — even with GitHub Actions and GitLab CI catching up fast.

→ Terraform and Ansible continue to lead the Infra-as-Code space, showing up in nearly every role.

→ Kubernetes and Docker are still non-negotiable not just for mid to senior DevOps positions but also for entry-level engineers.

→ Monitoring and security tools like Prometheus, Grafana, and Vault are now seen as core, not optional.

→ Early signs show AI/ML Ops familiarity starting to appear as a differentiator ~ still niche, but growing fast.

The pattern’s clear :
Don’t chase every new tool — chase understanding.
Because when you truly get how things work under the hood, you’ll never be “locked” to one provider, one stack, or one trend.

Start thinking in systems, not syntax..

At 24, I was moved from software dev to devops.

No warning. No choice.
(team priorities and somehow my cloud skills matched their needs)

I had barely learned enough coding to land my first job, and now they wanted me to do bash scripting, automation, and server troubleshooting?

Honestly, I was drowning.

Every morning, I'd stare at my laptop, overwhelmed by server logs and infrastructure provisioning scripts that were difficult to follow..

My mind kept telling me:
"Go back to coding. That's what you know. That's safe."
(also, confront your manager to move you back to dev-related tasks)

But then it struck me:
In life, we must choose our regrets: staying comfortable or taking the plunge into growth?
 
So I stayed.
Learned bash scripting line by line (yes line-by-line)
Debugged servers until midnight (took on-call every other weekend)
Built automation workflows from scratch..

Was it scary? Hell yes.
Was it worth it? ABSOLUTELY.

That adaptation became the best decision of my life.

Sometimes the path to growth lies exactly where your fear tells you not to go.. try it!

Hope this makes your week a bit better!:)

Author: vsadhwani
Date: Aug 20, 2025
Engagement: 1,115 reactions, 71 comments, 165 reposts (Score: 6,615)

URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7355616033405075457

One tool you'll see in almost every Cloud DevOps interview these days - Terraform.
Let's dive into how it really works at scale..

Whether you're deploying a single compute instance(any cloud provider) or scaling infrastructure across multiple regions - Terraform is now a core skill in the cloud and DevOps ecosystem.

Let's dive in:
1. Write
↳ Developers write declarative code using HCL (also supports JSON-compatible syntax)
↳ Use variables, modules, and outputs to keep it organized and reusable
↳ Leverage public modules from the Terraform Registry to avoid reinventing the wheel

2. Plan
↳ Terraform reads the configuration and validates it
↳ Integrates with CI/CD tools like GitHub Actions, Jenkins, and GitLab CI
↳ Generates an execution plan that shows exactly what will be created, changed, or destroyed
This step provides visibility—no changes are made yet.

3. Apply
↳ Terraform compares the current infrastructure with the desired state
↳ Executes only the required changes to align both states
↳ Ensures updates are safe, efficient, and idempotent

4. Infrastructure Ready
↳ Uses APIs to provision infrastructure via 1000+ supported providers: AWS, GCP, Azure, Kubernetes, and more
↳ Builds infrastructure that is consistent across environments
↳ Scales from individual projects to enterprise-grade production systems

Why teams rely on Terraform:
- Infrastructure is version-controlled and trackable
- Scales easily across teams and environments
- Reusable modules reduce redundancy and error
- Works seamlessly with DevOps pipelines

If you’re just getting started, keep going. These fundamentals compound fast.

Cloud / DevOps Engineer tech stack: Entry-level vs Mid vs Senior 
(and what you can expect during interviews)

How your Cloud & DevOps tools evolve through experience..

↳ Entry-Level (0 - 2 years)
• SDLC Concepts
• Linux & Shell Scripting 
• Docker & K8s (Intermediate level)
• Basic Git workflows
• Foundational DevOps concepts (CI/CD | Virtualization/ Containerization etc)
• Troubleshooting skills (how can you fix a broken pipeline)
• Jenkins / GitLab CI basics
• AWS /GCP/ Azure / OCI core services

↳ Mid-level Cloud/DevOps Engineer (3-6 yrs):
• Kubernetes for orchestration
• Terraform for infrastructure
• Ansible/Chef for configuration (in my previous life)
• Logging Stack (Datadog/ELK)
• Monitoring Stack (Datadog/Prometheus/Grafana)
• Python/Go automation (end-to-end workflow)
• AWS/GCP/Azure/OCI advanced services

↳ Senior Cloud/DevOps Engineer (7-10 yrs):
**Same tools but with architectural focus..
• Infrastructure for scalability
• Security by design 
• DevSecOps Implementation strategies
• Cloud Migrations ( understanding)
• Service mesh Implementation & Management
• Cost optimization patterns
• Cross-cloud solutions
• Platform engineering

The main takeaway..
Although preparation with in-demand tools is necessary, understanding system design patterns is more crucial than knowing every tool.

What does your DevOps toolkit look like? 🤔

Everyone keeps saying, “Learn Linux for DevOps” — here’s why you should.

Because in DevOps, it becomes part of nearly everything you do each day.

Here’s where you’ll see it most:

1. Running Containers & Orchestration
 ↳ Docker and Kubernetes both rely on Linux kernel features (cgroups, namespaces).
 ↳ Most container hosts and nodes you’ll manage are Linux-based.

2. Managing Cloud Servers
 ↳ EC2 instances, GCP VMs, Azure Linux VMs — all need Linux basics to configure and troubleshoot; (windows shows up too, but Linux runs most workloads.)
 ↳ Patching, monitoring, SSH access — all Linux workflows.

3. CI/CD Pipelines
 ↳ Build agents often run on Linux.
 ↳ Scripts, build tools, and package managers are designed to run in Linux shells.

4. Infrastructure as Code
 ↳ Tools like Terraform, Ansible, and Pulumi typically execute commands on Linux servers.
 ↳ Debugging failed playbooks or provisioning scripts = Linux command line.

5. Observability & Monitoring
 ↳ Checking logs, processes, and resource usage in real time often comes down to Linux commands.
 ↳ Even tools like Prometheus or Grafana agents run as Linux services.

6. Security & Permissions
 ↳ File permissions, user roles, SSH keys — all Linux-level basics.
 ↳ Critical when securing cloud servers or debugging failed pipeline deployments.
and many more instances where they are being used without your knowledge.

SO get comfortable with the commands you’ll actually use — and more importantly, the problems they help you solve

Remember:
When interviews test Linux, they’re not checking if you memorized commands.
They’re checking if you know where Linux fits in DevOps workflows — and if you can troubleshoot in those moments.

Just keep practicing — it starts to click piece by piece.

5 Case Studies You Must Read If You’re Preparing for Cloud Engineer Roles
(from classic cloud migrations to modern AI workloads)

1. Netflix’s Cloud Migration Journey
→ Deploy thousands of servers in minutes
→ Support 100M+ members on cloud-native infrastructure
Case Study Link: https://lnkd.in/dE9aKC6d

2. LinkedIn’s Infrastructure as Code at Scale
→ Standardize multi-cloud infrastructure with Terraform
→ Manage thousands of resources via IaC pipelines
Case Study Link: https://lnkd.in/dXpaAu9t

3. Spotify’s Gradual Cloud Migration
→ Transition massive infrastructure to GCP
→ Shift engineers’ time from infra maintenance to product development
Case Study Link: https://lnkd.in/dPqtQmNm

4. Uber’s End-to-End MLOps Pipeline 
→ Unified platform (Michelangelo) for training, deploying, and monitoring ML models
→ Automate feature engineering, versioning, and retraining
Case Study Link: https://lnkd.in/dJwys5uB

→ Distribute service costs via scalable microservices
→ Leverage cloud to off-load scaling and focus engineers on AI product development
Case Study Link: https://lnkd.in/dwAwX8gb

Here’s what you should focus on when reading them:

- Why the architecture was chosen
- How scalability and reliability were achieved
- Challenges with AI Workloads
- Cloud services and tooling used to solve those challenges

Don’t start with services.
Start with the problem, then understand why a certain service is used.

Most real-world systems win because of clear problem definition and thoughtful design.. not because of how many services they use.

5 kubernetes certification paths you should consider if you're aiming for cloud devops roles
(and what to expect from each certification)

Whether you're starting out or moving deeper into cloud-native infrastructure, k8s certs can help structure your learning and signal your expertise.

Here’s a role-based breakdown to help you pick the right learning path:

1. Cloud Native Newbie (Beginner)
 Start with the Kubernetes and Cloud Native Associate (KCNA)
 – Best suited for those just entering the cloud-native ecosystem
 – Learn basic Kubernetes architecture, container orchestration, and CNCF projects

2. Application Developer
 KCNA | CKAD
 – Focuses on deploying and managing applications on Kubernetes
 – Emphasis on workloads, config maps, secrets, and Helm

3. System Administrator
 KCNA | CKA
 – Targeted at infrastructure engineers managing clusters
 – Covers networking, RBAC, scheduling, cluster setup and maintenance

4. Security Specialist
 KCNA | Kubernetes Security Fundamentals | Certified Kubernetes Security Specialist (CKS)
 – Advanced track focused on pod security, policies, runtime protection, and cluster hardening
 – Ideal for cloud security and compliance roles
 – You will need CKA before CKS though.

5. Platform Engineer / DevOps Engineer / Site Reliability Engineer / Architect
 KCNA | CKAD | CKA | CKS
 – Comprehensive learning path covering application delivery, infrastructure operations, and security
 – Designed for those managing CI/CD pipelines, automation, monitoring, and high-availability systems

These are all OR options, you can pick based on you requirements (except CKS - that requires CKA)

How to Prepare Effectively
 – Follow curated GitHub repos tied to each cert and role
 – PRACTICE WITH LABS >>>>
 – For paid courses, check when they were last updated and read learner reviews
 – Use cloud provider free tiers to gain hands-on experience with clusters
 – Maintain short notes on key configuration commands and yaml templates

I have shared a curated list of resources (both free and vetted paid courses) — for these certifications in my newsletter : https://lnkd.in/dpb8Wg-6

Make sure you're subscribed so you don't miss it.

A few final thoughts:
 – Follow the right sequence: start with foundational (associate) certifications before moving to advanced (specialist)
 – Choose your learning path based on your target role, not what's currently trending
 – Certifications are useful (also expensive), and doesn’t guarantee jobs - so take their learning paths to upskill. And take the cert when you think it’s the right time.

Good luck!

If you’re looking to break into Cloud in 2026, here’s a practical cloud skills roadmap you can follow.

This is a high-level breakdown of the core areas worth focusing on:

1. Cloud Foundations
→ Understand how cloud providers think: regions, networking, IAM, pricing models.
→ This is about how cloud works, not which button to click.

2. DevOps & Cloud-Native
→ Learn how code moves from laptop → production reliably.
→ Containers, CI/CD, observability ~ the flow matters more than the platform.

3. Infrastructure as Code
→ Infra should be repeatable, reviewable, and versioned.
→ Think declarative systems, state, and lifecycle.. not manual setup.

4. Networking & Security
→ How traffic flows. How access is controlled. How things break.
→ This is where most real-world issues come from.. knowing how to troubleshoot distributed systems is critical..

5. AI / ML Infrastructure
→ Not just model training.. but how models run in real systems.
→ Serving, scaling, GPUs, monitoring, and cost awareness.

6. Platform Engineering
→ How teams build internal platforms that enable developers.
→ Focus on developer experience, self-service, and golden paths.

7. Cost & Operations (FinOps)
→ Everything you deploy has a cost.
→ Learn how usage, scale, and architecture impact spend.

Key takeaway:
Tools change. Core Buckets don’t.

Tools are just implementations.
What matters is understanding why a system exists, what problem it solves, and how the pieces fit together.

Don’t learn tools in isolation.
Learn systems thinking.. tools will follow.

If you’re early in your cloud journey, save this.
If you’re already in the field, which bucket would you double down on next?

If I were transitioning into MLOps as a DevOps engineer, this is the exact roadmap I’d follow.

You already know CI/CD, containers, and infrastructure.
Now it’s about learning how these apply to ML systems.

3-Month Foundation (Getting ML-aware)
• Use Programming for ML workflows
↳ Learn Python basics for scripts, automation, and simple ML tasks

• Strengthen Version Control for ML
↳ Use Git to track code, configs, and experiments

• Understand ML Basics
↳ Learn what training, evaluation, and deployment actually mean

• Learn Data Fundamentals
↳ Understand datasets, pipelines, and how data flows into models

• Run ML on the Cloud
↳ Understand IAM, compute, storage, and cloud managed ML services

• Containerize ML Applications
↳ Package training and inference using Docker
↳ Deploy workloads on Kubernetes

Target: Entry-level / Junior MLOps Engineer ($110k+)
-----
3-Month Advanced Track (Beginner → Practitioner)
• Familiarize yourself with MLOps Practices
↳ Understand CI/CD/CT concepts and why monitoring matters
↳ Explore: GitHub Actions (for end-to-end pipelines)

• Track ML Experiments & Data
↳ Version data and experiments (MLflow, DVC ~ concepts first)

• Provision a Simple ML Infrastructure
↳ Understand how Terraform is used for training and deployment setups (along with cloud-based compute)

• Learn how you can automate ML Pipelines
↳ Create simple training and deployment pipelines (Github Actions, Kubeflow)

• Serve Models Reliably
↳ Deploy models and understand inference workflows 
(FastAPI, Docker-based model serving, GPU deployments)

• Monitor Models in Production
↳ Learn model drift, data drift, and performance monitoring fundamentals
(DCGM, Prometheus/Grafana)

Target: Mid-level MLOps Engineer ($130k+)

If you want a guided learning path that explains these concepts from scratch and includes an end-to-end project ~ Abhishek's MLOps Zero to Hero course is an excellent resource.

It’s curated to help you build a solid, step-by-step foundation.

Do check it out here: https://lnkd.in/gJHbDFz6

--- Post 132 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Feb 16, 2026
Engagement: 1,082 reactions, 50 comments, 107 reposts (Score: 6,124)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7427752931439050752

If you want to move from DevOps into MLOps in the next 6 months,
here’s the roadmap you can follow.

1. Build Strong Programming Foundations (0–3 Months)
↳ Familiarize yourself Python basics for ML workflows.
↳ Run training scripts, preprocess data, and call ML libraries.
↳ Think of Python like Bash/Go ~ but for ML systems.

2. Strengthen Version Control for ML
↳ Practice Git to track code, configs, and experiments.
↳ Understand which change impacted model output.
↳ Treat experiments like production artifacts.

3. Understand ML Basics
↳ Learn how models are trained using data.
↳ Understand how models are tested before release.
↳ Know how trained models are used in real applications.

4. Learn Data Fundamentals
↳ Understand how data flows from source → pipeline → model.
↳ Learn how datasets are versioned and managed.
↳ See how data quality directly affects model performance.

→ At this point, you’re ML-aware.
You can qualify for junior MLOps roles.

5. Run ML on the Cloud
↳ Learn how cloud resources support training & inference.
↳ Understand IAM, compute, and storage for ML workloads.
↳ Explore managed ML services.

6. Containerize ML Applications
↳ Package training and inference workflows into containers.
↳ Run ML jobs consistently across environments.
↳ Deploy and scale ML workloads using Kubernetes.
→ Now you’re transitioning from beginner → practitioner.

7. Apply MLOps Practices (3–6 Months)
↳ Bring CI/CD thinking into ML systems.
↳ Automate retraining and validation workflows.
↳ Understand why monitoring is critical for ML reliability.

8. Track Experiments & Data Properly
↳ Compare results across multiple training runs.
↳ Version data and model outputs.
↳ Identify which combination of code + data produced the best outcome.

9. Provision ML Infrastructure
↳ Set up repeatable training & deployment environments.
↳ Use Terraform for scalable, cost-aware infrastructure.
↳ Optimize compute for training vs inference.

10. Automate ML Pipelines
↳ Build pipelines that train, validate, and deploy models.
↳ Orchestrate workflows with GitHub Actions or Kubeflow.
↳ Reduce manual effort across the entire ML lifecycle.

11. Serve Models Reliably
↳ Expose models via APIs for real-time & batch inference.
↳ Handle traffic using container-based serving.
↳ Support GPU-backed deployments when needed.

12. Monitor Models in Production
↳ Track model performance and prediction quality.
↳ Detect data drift and model drift early.
↳ Build dashboards for ML metrics.

That’s the shift:

DevOps focuses on shipping software.
MLOps focuses on shipping models ~ reliably.

Infra + Data + Models + Monitoring.

If you’re already strong in DevOps,
you’re halfway there.

The difference is learning how ML systems behave in production.

============================================================

Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7391511294220177408

If you’re diving into Kubernetes and unsure where to even start ~
don’t learn everything. 

First understand what belongs where.

Kubernetes is just the control plane ~
the ecosystem around it gives it life: networking, automation, observability, and security.

Here’s how it breaks down:

1. Cluster Management
→ EKS, AKS, GKE ~ managed clusters
→ kubeadm, Rancher, OpenShift ~ for DIY setups
→ Helm, Tanzu, Minikube ~ packaging, local dev & testing

2. Networking
→ Calico, Cilium, Istio, Linkerd ~ CNI plugins & service mesh layers
→ Traefik, MetalLB, Kube-Router ~ traffic routing & load balancing

3. Infra Automation
→ Terraform, Ansible, Pulumi ~ automate provisioning
→ ArgoCD, Flux ~ GitOps for continuous delivery
→ CloudFormation, Kubermatic ~ hybrid & enterprise automation

4. Monitoring & Observability
→ Prometheus, Grafana ~ metrics & visualization
→ ELK, Jaeger, Datadog, Splunk ~ logs & tracing
→ Thanos, New Relic, Dynatrace ~ enterprise-grade monitoring

5. Security & Compliance
→ Falco, Trivy, Kyverno ~ runtime & policy security
→ OPA, Vault ~ access & secret management
→ Kube-bench, Clair ~ vulnerability scanning

6. Container Runtimes
→ Docker, containerd, CRI-O ~ standard runtimes
→ Firecracker, gVisor, Kata ~ lightweight, secure sandboxes

Indeed, an intimidating stack of OSS tools and platforms.
but what helps is this:

→ Start small ~ pick one or two tools from each block and really understand their use case.
→ Then “apply” it ~ build something, break it, troubleshoot it.

That’s how you actually learn Kubernetes.. the right way.

If I were looking to enter MLOps right now as a DevOps engineer, here’s exactly how I’d approach it.

MLOps isn’t a completely new world 
It’s DevOps principles ~ just adapted for the ML lifecycle.

Here’s a step-by-step MLOps Roadmap showing what you already know (and what to add next):

1. Programming Fundamentals
→ Learn Python, Go, or Bash for scripting and automation
→ (DevOps folks: You already know scripting - just focus more on Python for data workflows)

2. Version Control Systems
→ Use Git & GitHub to manage and track code changes
→ (Already core to DevOps — just extend versioning to datasets and models)

3. Cloud Computing
→ Understand how ML models are deployed on AWS, Azure, GCP, or OCI
→ (DevOps experience with compute, networking, and IAM directly translates here — now explore managed ML services like Vertex AI or SageMaker)

4. Containerization
→ Use Docker to package ML environments and models
→ (Already familiar ground — the new part is packaging dependencies like models, data preprocessors, and serving APIs)

5. Data Engineering Fundamentals
→ Learn how data pipelines and ingestion architectures work
→ (Likely new — focus on tools like Airflow, Spark, or cloud-native dataflow systems)

6. Machine Learning Fundamentals
→ Get a grasp of core ML algorithms and evaluation metrics
→ (New domain — understand how data and code interact during model training)

7. MLOps Principles
→ Automate with CI/CD and Continuous Training (CT) pipelines
→ (DevOps principles apply — but now you integrate data validation, model retraining, and experiment tracking)

8. MLOps Components
→ Explore orchestration and tracking tools like Kubeflow, MLflow, or TFX
→ (Analogous to your CI/CD stack — but designed for model lifecycle management and metadata tracking)

9. Infrastructure as Code (IaC)
→ Use Terraform or CloudFormation to define reproducible ML environments
→ (You already do this — just adapt for ML infra like GPU clusters, data stores, and pipelines)

10. Keep Learning
→ Stay current with MLOps tools and evolving practices
→ (DevOps mindset already gives you an edge — apply the same continuous learning habit here)

The bridge is smaller than you think.
Your DevOps foundation — CI/CD, monitoring, automation, cloud infra — already covers half of MLOps.

You just need to extend it into data and model workflows.

Piece by piece, it starts to make sense.

If you’re aiming to grow in Cloud or DevOps, Terraform is one skill that continues to stay in-demand.

And across different roles, it’s used in different ways 👇

→ Automating infrastructure provisioning with Terraform scripts (spin up servers and networks fast)

→ Building reusable IaC templates for consistent environments across dev, stage, prod (avoid manual setup errors)

→ Managing state files and remote backends for drift detection (track infra changes over time)

→ Optimizing Terraform workflows with CI/CD integrations ( enable automated transitions across different stages)

Each use case adds depth — and helps you understand why it’s used in the first place.

Currently, Terraform remains one of the strongest IaC skills to have in your toolkit.

Found a solid list of 200+ Terraform Interview Questions — real-world use cases, CI/CD, state management, and updated for the latest version.

Check it out.

Before Cloud, before DevOps, and before becoming a Solutions Architect…

I was an Electronics & Telecom graduate trying to build a career in hardware.

And it wasn’t working.

I came to the US for a master’s degree, chasing a career in hardware.
Loans building up. Adjusting to a new education system.

About a year in, I realized hardware just wasn’t for me. 
I was struggling with my courses..

And then I started questioning all my life decisions..

Personally embarrassed to even tell my parents that their daughter might have made a mistake moving across the world without a clear path.

So I started over.

I picked cloud and figured things out as I went ~ no roadmap, no connections, just the drive to survive here.

What followed definitely wasn’t a straight line.

I made plenty of learning mistakes.
Got into roles that broke my confidence… but also rebuilt it. 

My path looked something like this:

HW → SW → DevOps → Cloud Support → Cloud Architect → Solutions Architect

Every single step refined me, taught me something I still use today.

So I broke down how I navigated each phase:
→ Finding the entry point to Big Tech
→ Building credibility through DevOps specialization
→ Getting clarity on what I actually wanted to do (Business or hands-on tech work)
→ Accelerating into opportunities I couldn’t have imagined when I started
→ And finally, the impact piece (showing up!!)

Watch the full 8-year journey here → https://lnkd.in/gApubaEp

Because I wanted to talk about the messy middle ~ the part before the shiny LinkedIn title.

The wrong degree.
The self-doubt.
The relatives asking questions you don’t have answers to yet.
(well there's never a good answer there)

So if you’re somewhere in the middle of that journey, feeling stuck or behind.. maybe this gives you the push to keep going.

If you’re preparing for Cloud DevOps interviews, here’s a practical cheatsheet that can help.

Instead of just understanding how to handle tools, think in use cases.

Here’s the breakdown:

1. Infrastructure as Code (IaC)
• Define cloud resources using code for repeatability and consistency
• Think Terraform, Cloudformation, Pulumi, ~ infra should be versioned like software; also look in Crossplane for managing cloud infra using K8s-style APIs. 

2. CI/CD Pipelines
• Automate build, test, and deployment workflows
• Pipelines as code, not click-ops

3. Configuration & Patch Management
• Enforce desired state across VMs and services
• Automated patching beats manual fixes at scale

4. Containers & Kubernetes
• Run and scale containerized apps reliably
• Managed K8s + Helm/Kustomize = real-world DevOps

5. Serverless DevOps
• Event-driven compute without server management
• Great for APIs, background jobs, and async workflows

6. Cloud Release Strategies
• Blue/Green, Canary, Feature Flags
• Ship safely without downtime

7. Observability
• Logs, metrics, alerts, traces ~ centralized visibility
• You can’t fix what you can’t see

8. Cost Optimization (FinOps) - crucial to understand with AI workloads
• Track, tag, rightsize, and control spend
• Cost is an architecture decision

9. DevSecOps
• Security and policy as code
• Guardrails built into pipelines, not added later

How to study Cloud DevOps (my advice):
→ Understand the core building blocks
→ Learn how they connect in real architectures
→ Always ask why this tool is used here

This isn’t an exhaustive list ~ but you should also look into topics like Networking for DevOps, GitOps & Platform Engineering, Disaster Recovery & Reliability

It will help you move beyond “I’ve used the tool” answers.

What else would you add that should be covered?

Also, I’ve shared the newsletter link with scenario-based questions for this role in the comments below ~ do check it out.

If you’re building a career around AI and Cloud infrastructure ~ this roadmap will help map the journey.

It breaks down the Cloud AI Engineer role into 12 focused stages:

– Build a strong foundation in cloud platforms and Linux (it’s everywhere), and understand networking, storage, and core infrastructure concepts

– Practice containerization and orchestration with Docker and Kubernetes to run scalable AI workloads

– Provision infrastructure using Infrastructure as Code (Terraform, Ansible, cloud-native tools) and CI/CD pipelines

– Understand AI/ML fundamentals including model architectures, training vs inference workflows, and distributed training concepts

– Get familiar with GPU computing, CUDA, and NVIDIA GPU architectures used for AI workloads

– Know how high-performance networking works for AI clusters using RDMA, GPUDirect, and optimized network fabrics

– Know how to manage AI storage systems including object storage, NVMe, and parallel file systems for large datasets (and why storage can become a bottleneck)

– Understand how to run AI workloads on Kubernetes with GPU scheduling, Kubeflow, and ML job orchestration

– Learn how to optimize and deploy AI inference pipelines using TensorRT, Triton, batching, and model optimization techniques

– Know how to build distributed training infrastructure for large models using NCCL, NVLink, and multi-node GPU clusters

– Implement monitoring and observability for AI systems with GPU metrics, tracing, and performance profiling

– Operate production AI systems with multi-cluster architectures, disaster recovery, and enterprise-scale AI infrastructure

So if you’re building AI models but don’t understand the infrastructure behind them ~ this roadmap helps connect the dots.
Resources in the comments below 👇 

Hope this helps clarify the systems and skills behind the role.

Cloud / DevOps Engineer Tech Stack: Entry-Level vs Mid vs Senior
(and what you can expect during interviews)

This is how your Cloud, DevOps & AI toolset evolves as you gain experience…

↳ Entry-Level (0–2 years)
• SDLC Concepts
• Linux & Shell Scripting
• Docker & K8s (Intermediate level)
• Basic Git workflows
• Foundational DevOps concepts (CI/CD | Virtualization | Containerization)
• Troubleshooting skills (fixing broken pipelines, reading logs)
• Jenkins / GitLab CI basics
• AWS / GCP / Azure / OCI core services
• Intro to AI workloads on cloud (deploying simple ML models, using prebuilt APIs)

↳ Mid-Level Cloud/DevOps Engineer (3–6 years)
• Kubernetes for orchestration
• Terraform for infrastructure automation
• Ansible/Chef for configuration management
• Logging Stack (Datadog / ELK)
• Monitoring Stack (Datadog / Prometheus / Grafana)
• Python/Go automation for end-to-end workflows
• AWS/GCP/Azure/OCI advanced services
• AI Infrastructure basics: GPUs/accelerators, setting up inference endpoints, model-serving tools
• Working with vector databases / feature stores in ML pipelines

↳ Senior Cloud/DevOps Engineer (7–10 years)
Same tools ~ but with an architectural and cross-functional focus:
• Infrastructure for scalability & reliability
• Security by design
• DevSecOps Implementation strategies
• Cloud migration planning & architecture
• Service Mesh implementation & management
• Cost optimization at scale
• Cross-cloud / hybrid patterns
• Platform engineering
• Designing infra for AI systems: distributed training, inference scaling, latency & performance tuning
• AI Observability: tracing model performance, drift, and reliability inside production systems

The main takeaway:
Tools matter ~ but understanding system design patterns and how modern AI systems run in production matters even more.

What does your Cloud, DevOps & AI toolkit look like today?

Author: vsadhwani

Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7309967277737566208

If you're searching for a good DevOps read, you should see this.

before diving in, ask yourself:

1. Are you interested in the principles or the practical aspects?

• foundations and principles → "The Goal" or "Lean Software Development"
• application → "The DevOps Handbook" or "Continuous Delivery"

2. Which is your primary focus: culture or implementation?

• cultural → "Team Topologies" or "The Five Dysfunctions of a Team"
• for implementation → "Site Reliability Engineering" or "Accelerate"

3. How do you learn best?

• prefer story-based learning → "The Phoenix Project" or "The Unicorn Project"
• prefer structured approaches → "Domain-Driven Design: Distilled" or "Database Reliability Engineering"

Don't try to read them all. 
Pick ONE that aligns with your current needs and actually finish it.

Also, this is not an exhaustive list of titles - there are more great reads in the market!
(happy to share a few more that I've read)

Here's what you can do ~ my 2 cents:

Start with "The Phoenix Project" for the WHY
Move to "The DevOps Handbook" for the HOW
Then begin "Site Reliability Engineering" for the next-level

Remember: Reading should complement your DevOps practice, not substitute it.

For a detailed rundown of each book, check out this blog by Octopus Deploy -
https://lnkd.in/dNS_PmXf

What books have been most impactful in your DevOps journey? 
I'd love to hear your recommendations.

Author: vsadhwani

You cannot master modern cloud-native devops without understanding how code moves from a developer’s workstation to production

Whether you're a DevOps Engineer, Cloud Developer, or SRE, here’s a visual step-by-step DevOps Delivery Workflow with Google Cloud that can help:

1. Source Code Management
 ↳ Developers write and push code to version control platforms like GitHub Enterprise, Bitbucket, or Google Cloud Source Repositories.

2. Build
 ↳ CI/CD tools such as GitHub Actions, GitLab CI, Jenkins, CircleCI, or Google Cloud Build compile the code and run automated tests.

3. Store Artifacts
 ↳ Once the build passes, artifacts (packages, images, etc.) are stored in registries like Artifact Registry, Cloud Storage, Docker Hub etc

4. Deploy Infrastructure
 ↳ Infrastructure as Code tools like Terraform, Pulumi to provision and configure cloud infrastructure.

5. Test & Monitor
 ↳ Tools like Prometheus, Datadog, Splunk, and Google Cloud Operations suite to monitor app health, performance, and availability.

6. Deploy to Cloud
 ↳ The final application is deployed to Google Cloud services — Compute Engine, Kubernetes Engine (GKE), App Engine, or Cloud Run — depending on the use case.

This is the full journey of delivering software in a cloud-native, scalable, and secure way — from local code to live product on the cloud.

If you're aiming for a role in the cloud devops domain, being able to explain this flow — especially with real-world tools — is essential.

Author: vsadhwani

If you’re learning containers and asking Docker vs Kubernetes ~
you’re mixing layers.

They solve different stages of the same problem.

Let me break down what’s actually happening:

Docker → Container Runtime
↳ Builds container images
↳ Runs containers on a single host
↳ Provides basic networking
↳ Manages volumes and storage

Kubernetes → Container Orchestrator
↳ Uses Docker (or containerd) under the hood
↳ Manages containers across multiple hosts
↳ Handles scheduling, scaling, service discovery, and load balancing

Key point → They’re not alternatives.
Kubernetes needs a container runtime.

Real-World Scenarios

Scenario 1 → Blog + Database (100 users/day)
❌ Kubernetes → Overkill. Managing 20+ K8s objects for 2 containers.
✅ Docker Compose → ~20 lines of YAML. Done in 10 minutes.

Scenario 2 → E-commerce (10M users, 50 microservices)
❌ Docker Compose → No autoscaling, no self-healing, no multi-region support.
✅ Kubernetes → Built for exactly this level of complexity.

Scenario 3 → AI Model Testing (Single Model, Low Traffic)
❌ Kubernetes → Overkill. GPU scheduling and autoscaling for a single model.
✅ Docker → Run the model in a container on one machine or GPU VM.

Scenario 4 → Production AI System (Multiple Models, High Traffic)
❌ Docker Compose → No model scaling, no rollout strategy, no fault isolation.
✅ Kubernetes → Manages GPU workloads, rolling model updates, and reliability.

Key Takeaway:
Docker answers “how do I run a container?”
Kubernetes answers “how do I run systems at scale?”

The real skill is understanding why each is used, and applying it when needed.

Author: vsadhwani

If you’re preparing for Kubernetes certifications without knowing the right order, you’re making it harder than it needs to be.

Note: these are advanced certs, so proper planning is required!

K8s certifications actually follow a pretty logical path.

Here’s the simple way to think about it:

1. KCNA ~ Start here (Foundations)
This is the fundamentals layer.

You learn things like:
• What “cloud native” actually means
• Container basics
• Kubernetes architecture
• Container runtimes
• Core objects like Pods

Good if you’re new to Kubernetes or cloud-native systems. 
But if you're already in cloud, you can proceed to the next one!

2. CKAD ~ If you like building things
This certification focuses on application workloads.

You work with:
• Pods, Deployments, ReplicaSets
• ConfigMaps & Secrets
• Jobs & CronJobs
• Resource limits
• Debugging workloads

Think of this as Kubernetes from the developer/application side.

3. CKA ~ The core certification
This is where most platform engineers / DevOps engineers focus.

It covers cluster-level operations:
• Kubernetes architecture
• etcd and control plane components
• Scheduling
• Networking
• Kubelet & kube-proxy
• Cluster maintenance
• Troubleshooting

4. CKS ~ Security specialization
This is the advanced path after CKA.

You dive into:
• Kubernetes attack surfaces
• CIS benchmarks
• Pod security
• Authentication & authorization
• Network policies
• Runtime and image security

This is where you start thinking like a platform security engineer.

The simple roadmap most engineers follow:

KCNA → CKA → CKS
(or)
KCNA → CKAD → CKA

And the interesting part:
A lot of the concepts overlap across these certifications.. things like Kubernetes architecture, networking, and core objects keep showing up everywhere.

Once those fundamentals click, the rest becomes much easier.

5 Database Types Every Engineer Should Know in 2026

Different workloads demand different database architectures

Modern applications rely on multiple database types, from real-time analytics to vector search for AI. 

Knowing which type to use can make or break your system's performance.

→ Relational / SQL Database: Traditional row-and-column databases with strong ACID properties, great for structured data and transactional systems.

→ Time-Series Database: Optimized for time-stamped data like metrics, logs, and IoT sensors. Built for high-write throughput and fast time-based queries.

→ NoSQL Database: Flexible schema databases that come in multiple flavors ~ document-based, key-value, column-based, and graph. 
Perfect for semi-structured data and horizontal scaling.

→ Vector Database: Specialized for AI/ML applications, storing embeddings and enabling ultra-fast similarity search for semantic search, recommendations, and RAG pipelines.

→ NewSQL Database: Combines the ACID guarantees of SQL with the distributed scalability of NoSQL. Built for modern high-scale enterprise applications that need both consistency and performance.

Over to you: Which database type do you think will grow fastest in the next 5 years?

Cloud migration strategies explained in 7 simple points!
(common scenario-based question for interviews) 

Don't skip these if you're gearing up for a cloud interview

1. Rehost (lift and shift) : Simply move applications to the cloud as they are.
↳ Quick wins with minimal changes
↳ Good for tight deadlines (if compatible)
↳ Lower initial cloud costs

2. Replatform : Adapt applications slightly to leverage cloud benefits
↳ Optimize without full rewrites
↳ Cloud-native service adoption
↳ Balance speed with optimization

3. Repurchase : Replace existing systems with cloud-based alternatives.
↳ Replace with SaaS solutions
↳ Reduce maintenance overhead
↳ Focus on core business needs

4. Refactor (re-architect) : Redesign applications for optimal cloud performance.
↳ Full cloud-native transformation
↳ Maximum cloud benefits
↳ Higher initial investment

5. Relocate: Move workloads between cloud locations or providers.
↳ Strategic region/provider moves
↳ Compliance & performance focus
↳ Multi-cloud flexibility

6. Retire (decommission): Eliminate unnecessary systems.
↳ Remove technical debt
↳ Cost optimization
↳ Simplify landscape

7. Retain : Keep systems on-premises for now.
↳ Strategic postponement
↳ Risk management

For Interview Takers:
→ Most migrations use a mix of these strategies.
→ Know when to pitch each one
→ Be ready to explain the trade-offs of each strategy (cost, time, risk, long-term benefits).
→ Discuss real-world scenarios.
→ Show cloud-native awareness.
→ Emphasize assessment and planning.
→ Address security considerations.

This is not an exhaustive set of considerations

Migration questions can delve into strategy and business impact, especially for higher-level roles.

Having key points readily available is essential!!

If you're working in cloud, here are 7 architecture patterns you need to know..
(plus when each one makes sense to use)

Before we start ~ why should you care about architecture patterns?

Because building in the cloud is all about designing systems that are scalable, resilient, and easy to evolve. These patterns help you do exactly that.

They’re battle-tested strategies used by modern teams to solve real-world challenges.. like handling millions of users, avoiding downtime, or replacing legacy systems without a total rewrite.

Here are 7 patterns worth knowing:

1. Microservices Architecture
 ↳ Break large apps into small, independent services.
 ↳ Each service handles a specific task and communicates via APIs.
 ↳ When to use ~ When you want independent deployment, scaling, or teams owning different parts of the system.

2. Event-Driven Architecture
 ↳ Let components react to events asynchronously.
 ↳ Events (like user sign-ups or file uploads) trigger workflows automatically.
 ↳ When to use ~ For loosely coupled systems that require real-time or reactive flows.

3. Sidecar Pattern
 ↳ Pair helper containers with your main app container.
 ↳ Offloads tasks like logging, service discovery, or networking.
 ↳ When to use ~ In Kubernetes/microservices for clean separation of concerns.

4. Strangler Fig Pattern
 ↳ Replace legacy systems one piece at a time.
 ↳ Gradually route functionality from the old system to new microservices.
 ↳ When to use ~ During legacy modernization with minimal disruption.

5. Database Sharding / Horizontal Scaling
 ↳ Split data across multiple databases or nodes.
 ↳ Each shard holds a subset of data for better performance.
 ↳ When to use ~ For high-throughput systems and global-scale applications.

6. Serverless Architecture
 ↳ Run code on-demand without managing servers.
 ↳ Functions are triggered by events (like HTTP calls or file uploads).
 ↳ When to use ~ For unpredictable workloads, quick development, or cost-efficiency.

7. API Gateway Pattern
 ↳ Centralize all client requests through one gateway.
 ↳ Acts as a single entry point, handling routing, auth, throttling, etc.
 ↳ When to use ~ To simplify client-server communication and secure backend services.

Now know this.. these patterns are the backbone of high-performing, cloud-native apps at top tech companies. 

Understanding their pros, cons, and use cases can give you a real edge in interviews.

If you’re working in Cloud DevOps, Docker is one of the tools you’ll keep coming back to, even in the AI era.

Before Kubernetes, Helm, or Terraform
you need Docker.

You use it to:
- package applications consistently
- speed up local development and CI pipelines
- prepare workloads for Kubernetes and production
- package models for AI inference and serving

Understanding Docker goes beyond writing a Dockerfile.

It’s about knowing how images are built, how containers run, and how small choices impact performance, security, and cost.

It’s also about understanding runtimes.
Docker is the interface you use, but underneath it’s containerd, runc, and the Linux kernel doing the real work.

The Docker CLI becomes your control surface
building images, running containers, inspecting layers, streaming logs, and debugging issues locally before they ever reach production.

Sharing a Docker cheatsheet that covers the commands you actually use day to day
from building and tagging images to debugging running containers.

Author: vsadhwani

URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7356346807288774689

If you can’t clearly explain Containers vs. Serverless, you’re not ready for the cloud interview.

Here’s a quick 2-minute overview of the key differences you need to know:

1. Control:
 → Containers give you full control (OS, runtime, dependencies).
 → Serverless abstracts all that — just write code and deploy.

2. Runtime:
 → Containers run continuously or as needed.
 → Serverless functions spin up on demand and shut down after execution.

3. Use Cases:
 → Containers = long-running services, microservices, custom environments.
 → Serverless = event-driven tasks, APIs, cron jobs.

4. Scaling:
 → Containers scale with orchestrators (Managed/self-hosted K8s, ECS).
 → Serverless auto-scales instantly per request.

5. Confused Terms:
→ FaaS ≠ Full Serverless platform ( FaaS is one part of serverless — typically for running functions or small tasks)
→ Serverless ≠ No server (servers are managed for you)

6. Architecture Mindset:
 → Containers = manage infra, patch OS, monitor runtime.
 → Serverless = focus on business logic, let the platform handle ops.

In a nutshell:
- Containers give you flexibility and control.
- Serverless gives you simplicity and automation.

Both are modern deployment models - so the idea is to present the answer or strategy based on the specific scenario or use case.

Most Cloud & DevOps engineers assume these new AI roles don’t concern them - but that’s where they’re wrong..
here’s how the role is evolving:

→ Model Development
Data scientists, ML engineers, and prompt engineers design the models.
↳ But those models still need cloud infra — GPUs, storage, networking, scaling. That’s us.

→ Model Validation
Model validators & ethicists check fairness and accuracy.
↳ DevOps ensures this happens in CI/CD pipelines with automated tests and gated deployments.

→ Deployment & Integration
This is where Cloud/DevOps overlaps the most with AI roles:
AI Architect, MLOps Engineer, Software Engineer
↳ We’re the ones putting models into production with Kubernetes, containers, APIs, and load balancers.

→ Monitoring & Governance
Models need more than uptime checks — drift, bias, cost, compliance.
↳ Cloud/DevOps extends into AIOps: metrics, alerts, governance at scale.

⸻

Thanks to the growth of open-source AI tools, agent development is becoming more accessible.

So here we are:
Cloud & DevOps aren’t being replaced — they’re growing too.
We make that infrastructure run securely.

If you’re a DevOps engineer or someone building a career in DevOps, there are 4 stages to it:

Stage 1: “Learn the Core” DevOps Hat
These are the skills you’re expected to come with:
→ Linux + Shell
→ Git & GitHub
→ CI/CD basics
→ Containers (Docker)

This is the one hat every DevOps engineer wears, no matter the company.

Stage 2: The Cloud & Infra Hat
Here your basics are solid, and you add the cloud layer:
→ AWS / Azure / GCP / OCI stacks
→ IAM practices
→ Networking (VPC, subnets, routing)
→ Infrastructure as Code (Terraform / CloudFormation)

This is when DevOps starts feeling real ~ and you are the “responsible one.”

Stage 3: The Platform & Kubernetes Hat
This is where multiple hats start stacking:
→ Kubernetes fundamentals
→ Helm, Ingress, basic Service Mesh concepts
→ Observability (logs, metrics, traces)
→ Scaling, reliability, SRE basics
→ AI workload readiness (scheduling, autoscaling)
→ Model deployment basics (inference optimization, batching, caching)

This is the stage where you shift from “running pipelines” to running full platforms ~ including AI workloads that need real performance.

Stage 4: The Everything-Else Hat 
All the invisible work DevOps quietly owns:
→ Security fundamentals
→ Cloud cost management
→ Incident response + on-call
→ Capacity planning
→ AI cost governance 
→ Model observability (latency, drift, failure modes)
→ Documentation and process hygiene

This is the stage where the hats pile up :)

No DevOps Engineer should have to do it all ~ but most of us end up wearing a few hats anyway.
That’s the job.
And personally, I love every chaotic bit of it.

Image Credits: TechOps Examples

============================================================

The first time I ever interviewed was on-campus, with TCS - I didn’t even make it past the written assessment round.

The results came out in the evening. Everyone was on campus, and most of my friends had their names on the list.
I didn’t.

I went back to my hostel, skipped dinner, and went to sleep. I didn’t know how to process that kind of disappointment.

The second company that came to campus was Capgemini. I didn’t even show up for the written assessment round.
Not because I wasn’t eligible — but because I was scared (and was trying to avoid that same feeling again)...

At the time, I was preparing for the GRE. It felt like the only door still open — the only way to escape the disappointment I had in myself.

So I gave the test, got a decent score, and applied to universities.
No Plan B.

Growing up in Raipur (central India), we didn’t have many people going abroad for studies.
Limited guidance, endless uncertainties.

Every application cost a fortune. I’d regularly ask my dad to transfer money to my account, watching their savings fly out with no guarantees.

Then came the leap of faith → moving to the US.

When luck showed up, I was ready:
→ I was ready to do an $8/hr waiter job during college to make ends meet.
→ I was ready to turn that first on-campus rejection — and secure 3 offers before graduation.
→ I was ready to leave a stable, higher-paying job to follow the role I truly wanted.
→ I was ready to face those H1B lottery rejections — and conquer it with EB1.
→ I was ready to start putting my thoughts out here, on LinkedIn.

Yes, you might be lucky to be in the right place at the right time —
but you spend years facing disappointments, fighting your fears, overcoming struggles, and building the skills, experience, and judgment to make the most of that luck.

Today, I’m still ready — ready to find strength in new challenges and opportunities.

Because that’s how I have been training myself. 
You thrive because you refuse to just survive.

TL;DR – Train yourself to face disappointments head-on (there'll be many, trust me). 

Your strength often lies buried under the weaknesses you try to ignore.
Face them.

============================================================

When I joined AWS, I had zero certifications.

Certifications weren’t the reason I got in — but they did help me:
~ shape the way I learned
~ focus my energy, especially given how vast the cloud landscape is
~ structure my thinking around how different platforms actually work

They weren’t just a checkbox — they gave me a framework.

At AWS, I was thrown into deep-end learning:
figuring out cloud architecture, debugging deployment issues, and troubleshooting app failures..

No exam could’ve prepared me for that, but it made me realize how wide the cloud ecosystem really is.

That’s when I started preparing for certs — not to check a box, but to build structure in the chaos.

They gave me a roadmap. A way to identify gaps.

And most importantly ~ confidence.

By the time I transitioned to google, I had:
- a clearer technical foundation
- a stronger portfolio
- and language to explain the “why” behind my architecture decisions

If you’re debating whether certifications matter — here’s my take:

Certs don’t guarantee a job.

But they can guide your learning, polish your fundamentals, and give you momentum — especially if you’re starting out or switching roles.

My path to becoming a Solutions Architect in Silicon Valley
- failed to thrive in pure development or competitive coding

- couldn’t decide whether to go deep into Bash, scripting, or “proper” backend development

- failed coding interviews (more than once)..

- landed my first tech role as a cloud migration intern

- worked closely with developers, infra teams, and legacy systems

- realized I enjoyed how systems fit together more than writing isolated features

- tried focusing on development again but something still felt off

- somehow (very unintentionally) moved deeper into DevOps and cloud work

- learned CI/CD, infrastructure, containerization, and on-call realities the hard way

- started understanding trade-offs: speed vs stability, cost vs scale, managed vs unmanaged environments

- moved from how do I code this? to how should this be built in the cloud?

- began thinking in architectures instead of just implementing tickets

- realized whiteboarding helped me learn and reason faster

- slowly grew into a Cloud Architect role

- started sharing cloud and AI architecture lessons publicly

- changed lanes toward AI infrastructure and accelerated computing

- failed interviews again as AI concepts felt dense being an infra person

- took the feedback, and built a structured way to study and think

- cracked solutions architect role at NVIDIA

- still learning ~ but finally feels like the right lane (maybe)

In summary:

What we can do vs. how tech will change is mostly out of anyone’s control.

But as a tech professional, you do have multiple trial-and-error paths you can take until you find the lane that fits.

So take the risk of failing at something 
and come out with clarity (if not stronger)..

You got this!

--- Post 177 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Feb 16, 2026
Engagement: 825 reactions, 34 comments, 140 reposts (Score: 4,745)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7424488944785993728

You cannot master MLOps without understanding how it differs from traditional DevOps pipelines.

Whether you're a DevOps engineer moving into ML or an MLOps engineer designing production systems, this is the core difference you need to understand.

What DevOps and MLOps share
Both rely on the same foundation: 
↳ Source control (Git) 
↳ CI pipelines for testing and artifacts 
↳ Container registries (ECR/ACR/GCR)
↳ GitOps tools like ArgoCD/Flux 
↳ Kubernetes-based deployments with monitoring

In other words ~ the infrastructure layer looks very similar.

Where MLOps is different: the CT loop
Traditional DevOps follows a linear path: Code → Build → Deploy → Monitor
MLOps is circular: Code → Build → Train → Deploy → Monitor → Retrain

This extra Continuous Training (CT) loop is what changes everything.

Here's what makes MLOps different: 
→ You version data in addition to code (DVC, MLflow, etc.) 
→ You introduce a new stage between CI and CD: model training, validation, and registry management 
→ Production monitoring now includes model performance, data drift, latency, and feature shifts 
→ When drift occurs, the system must automatically retrain and redeploy

The CT loop isn't optional; it's the core of MLOps.

If you already understand CI/CD, you're halfway there.

The tools may look the same ~ but the workflow logic is fundamentally different.

• • •
If this helped clarify DevOps vs. MLOps, I regularly share practical breakdowns of Cloud, DevOps, MLOps, and AIOps.

--- Post 179 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Oct 19, 2025
Engagement: 818 reactions, 31 comments, 122 reposts (Score: 4,644)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7376636930718846976

Load balancing is a key networking concept
here are 8 algorithms with use cases you should know:

1. Round Robin (most-used)
↳ Sends requests one after the other.
↳ Use case: Static websites with identical VMs or containers

2. Least Connections 
↳ Chooses the server with the fewest active connections.
↳ Use case: Long-lived connections like video streaming or chat apps.

3. Weighted Round Robin 
↳ Assigns more requests to stronger servers.
↳ Use case: Hybrid cluster with large and small VMs.

4. Weighted Least Connections 
↳ Considers both capacity and live connections.
↳ Use case: Enterprise apps where some servers handle heavy batch jobs while others stay lighter.

5. IP Hash 
↳ Routes the same client IP to the same server.
↳ Use case: Applications(eg. e-commerce) that need session stickiness without external session stores.
 
6. Least Response Time 
↳ Directs traffic to the server with the fastest response.
↳ Use case: Latency-sensitive APIs like stock trading or payments.

7. Random 
↳ Sends requests randomly.
↳ Use case: Simple test environments or when traffic is very light.
 
8. Least Bandwidth 
↳ Routes traffic where bandwidth usage is lowest.
↳ Use case: Media delivery platforms where servers stream large files/video.

How can you configure it?
→ All Cloud Providers supports load balancers with options for custom balancing policies
(the default as round-robin)
→ In Kubernetes - you'd define these policies via annotations or ingress controller configs

So next time you create a cloud load balancer, remember—
the algorithm you choose isn’t just about distributing load.

It directly impacts performance, cost, and the experience your users will have.

- understand the API layer behind cloud CLI commands ~ the docs really help.

- maintain a personal Linux command doc for those heavy searches, log parsing, and debugging.

Once you start connecting commands to APIs and automation,
the terminal screen stops being intimidating ~ and starts becoming the comfort zone you can rely on.

Author: vsadhwani
Date: Mar 18, 2026
Engagement: 806 reactions, 29 comments, 133 reposts (Score: 4,586)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7429922828281397248

If you’re working with Kubernetes, you need to clearly understand Static vs Runtime security.

Static Security (Before the Pod Runs)

↳ Cluster & Control Plane (API server access, etcd encryption, TLS)
↳ Authentication & Authorization (RBAC, service accounts, least privilege)
↳ Image & Supply Chain (image scanning, signing, SBOM)
↳ Policy & Configuration (OPA/Kyverno, admission controllers, Pod Security Standards)
↳ Secrets Management (external secret managers, encryption at rest, rotation)
↳ Network (design-time policies, default deny, namespace isolation)
↳ Node Hardening (minimal OS, secure kubelet, patching)

This is your prevention layer.
——

Runtime Security (When the Pod Runs)

↳ Threat Detection (suspicious processes, privilege escalation, container escape)
↳ Process & Syscalls monitoring (exec/fork, crypto-mining detection)
↳ Runtime Network (outbound traffic monitoring, east-west anomalies)
↳ Identity at Runtime (token misuse, pod identity abuse)
↳ Filesystem Protection (sensitive path access, binary tampering)
↳ Incident Response (kill pod, quarantine namespace, auto rollback)
↳ Audit & Observability (audit logs, security events, SIEM integration)

Static = prevention
Runtime = detection & response

Production-grade security = both.

Use this as a reference before your next interview.

If you’re leveling up Python for a DevOps role in 2026, here’s what you should know 👇 

DevOps Python ≠ LeetCode.
And with AI-assisted coding, the real skill isn’t writing clever algorithms..
it’s knowing what to automate and where Python fits in real systems.

- You start by replacing messy Bash scripts with clean, readable Python scripts.
What used to take hours of manual setup becomes a few minutes of automated provisioning.

- Next, Python shows up inside your CI/CD pipelines.
You use it to customize Jenkins workflows, trigger deployments through Git hooks, and build scripts that adapt across environments instead of breaking.

- As systems move to the cloud, Python becomes your control plane.
You manage aws resources with Boto3, provision infrastructure with azure SDKs, and define cloud resources using Pulumi ~ all through code you can version and review.

- When containers enter the picture, Python helps you operate at scale.
You automate Docker image builds and interact with Kubernetes clusters using the Python client instead of clicking through dashboards.

- Finally, Python keeps systems reliable and secure.
You parse logs at scale, integrate with Prometheus and Grafana APIs, and automate security checks so issues are caught before they reach production.

That’s what Python looks like in DevOps.

It’s automation, cloud APIs, pipelines, observability, and security.

Learn Python where it actually runs ~ in production.

A lot of people think MLOps is just ML workloads + DevOps.
But is it really?

Let’s break down both pipelines and see where they converge — and where they don’t.

DevOps Pipeline
→ Plan & Code – You use Git or a source repo to manage your codebase
→ Build & Test – Then you build the code from the previous stage and run automated tests
→ Release – The build artifacts are pushed to an artifact registry
→ Deploy – Containers with those app artifacts are deployed via Kubernetes or similar orchestration
→ Operate & Monitor – Logs, metrics, and alerts are set up to ensure system reliability

MLOps Pipeline
→ Data Engineering – Here you ingest raw data and prepare features for model training
→ Model Training – This stage focuses on training models using code, data, and experiments
→ Model Versioning – Trained models are now stored and tracked in a registry with metadata
→ CI/CD for ML – Testing and validation of ML pipelines are automated
→ Deploy – This is when the model goes into production (available for users) with inference endpoints
→ Monitor & Retrain – Detect data drift and retrain models for consistent performance

So yes, MLOps can be seen as DevOps for ML, but it goes beyond — combining DevOps principles with data pipelines, model lifecycle management, and continuous retraining to form an extended, adaptive ecosystem.

In short:
DevOps streamlines code to production.
MLOps extends that journey to include data, models, and continuous training.

I decided not to sit on the sidelines, waiting to feel "ready" for this cloud role — because honestly, you're never truly ready.

So, I set a goal to apply to AWS in five months. 
Even if I wasn't perfect. Even if I got rejected.

→ Learned cloud fundamentals (core services) from every free resource I could find. 
→ Got comfortable with the CLI and console, breaking things unintentionally to end up diving into messy logs (totally worth it!). 
→ Picked some projects from GitHub repos and tried them out. 
→ Documented everything in my Word doc folder (rough notes) — thinking I'd push it to Git someday.

When I uploaded my resume, I wasn't the most qualified.

And sometimes? That’s the most powerful thing you can bring to the table.

Core cloud skills, coding, analyzing logs — everything clicked!

If you’re working with Kubernetes, here are 6 scaling strategies you should know — and when to use each one.

Before we start — why should you care about scaling strategies?

Because when Kubernetes apps face unpredictable demand, you need scaling mechanisms in place to keep them running smoothly and cost-effectively.

Here are 6 strategies worth knowing:

1. Human Scaling
 ↳ Manually adjust pod counts using kubectl scale.
 ↳ Direct but not automated.
 When to use ~ For debugging, testing, or small workloads where automation isn’t worth it.

2. Horizontal Pod Autoscaling (HPA)
 ↳ Changes pod count based on CPU/memory usage.
 ↳ Adds/removes pods as workload fluctuates.
 When to use ~ For stateless apps with variable load (e.g., web apps, APIs).

3. Vertical Pod Autoscaling (VPA)
 ↳ Adjusts CPU/memory requests for existing pods.
 ↳ Ensures each pod gets the right resources.
 When to use ~ For steady workloads where pod count is fixed, but resource needs vary.

4. Cluster Autoscaling
 ↳ Adds/removes nodes based on pending pods.
 ↳ Ensures pods always have capacity to run.
 When to use ~ For dynamic environments where pod scheduling fails due to lack of nodes.

5. Custom Metrics Based Scaling
 ↳ Scale pods using application-specific metrics (e.g., queue length, request latency).
 ↳ Goes beyond CPU/memory.
 When to use ~ For workloads with unique performance signals not tied to infrastructure metrics.

6. Predictive Scaling
 ↳ Uses ML/forecasting to scale in advance of demand.
 ↳ Tries to prevent traffic spikes before they happen.
 When to use ~ For workloads with predictable traffic patterns (e.g., sales events, daily peaks).

Now know this — scaling isn’t one-size-fits-all.
The best teams often combine multiple strategies (for example, HPA + Cluster Autoscaling) for resilience and cost efficiency.

What did I miss?
