DevOps Engineer != SRE Engineer.
Let's clarify the differences once and for all.

↳ while SREs focus on system reliability metrics, DevOps engineers optimize deployment pipelines.

↳ while SREs live and die by SLOs/SLIs, DevOps engineers obsess over CI/CD success rates.

↳ while SREs are deep in incident management, DevOps engineers are knee-deep in automation scripts.

↳ while SREs need to master monitoring at scale, DevOps engineers need to master infrastructure as code.

↳ while SREs focus on reducing toil, DevOps engineers focus on reducing deployment friction.

Distinctions in initial qualifications for DevOps and SRE career paths ~

- DevOps welcomes beginners who understand the basics of automation and collaboration. 

- SRE positions usually demand seasoned engineers who've already dealt with complex system reliability challenges.

If you’re preparing for DevOps / Cloud interviews, you need to stop mixing up Docker and Kubernetes.

They are NOT competitors.

They solve different stages of the same problem.

Let me break down what’s actually happening:
Docker → Container Runtime
↳ Builds container images
↳ Runs containers on a single host
↳ Provides basic networking
↳ Manages volumes and storage

Kubernetes → Container Orchestrator
↳ Uses Docker (or containerd) under the hood
↳ Manages containers across multiple hosts
↳ Handles scheduling, scaling, service discovery, and load balancing

Key point → They’re not alternatives.
Kubernetes needs a container runtime.

You must see how it is implemented for different scenarios:

Scenario 1 → Blog + Database (100 users/day)
❌ Kubernetes → Overkill. Managing 20+ K8s objects for 2 containers.
✅ Docker Compose → ~20 lines of YAML. Done in 10 minutes.

Scenario 2 → E-commerce (10M users, 50 microservices)
❌ Docker Compose → No autoscaling, no self-healing, no multi-region support.
✅ Kubernetes → Built for exactly this level of complexity.

Scenario 3 → AI Model Testing (Single Model, Low Traffic)
❌ Kubernetes → Overkill. GPU scheduling and autoscaling for a single model.
✅ Docker → Run the model in a container on one machine or GPU VM.

Scenario 4 → Production AI System (Multiple Models, High Traffic)
❌ Docker Compose → No model scaling, no rollout strategy, no fault isolation.
✅ Kubernetes → Manages GPU workloads, rolling model updates, and reliability.

The real skill is understanding why each is used, and applying it when needed.

Application Load Balancer vs Network Load Balancer
What's the difference?

If you’re designing anything cloud-native; Kubernetes, microservices, or AI inference endpoints..
you cannot treat ALB and NLB as interchangeable.

They solve completely different engineering problems.

The real difference is in how they behave:

ALB (L7)
→ Inspects the request: path, headers, host, cookies
→ Applies routing rules & authentication
→ Works best for APIs, web apps, multi-tenant routing
→ Gives you observability at the application layer
Key Differentiator: makes traffic smart by understanding the application-level intent

NLB (L4)
→ Moves packets at extreme speed
→ Handles TCP/UDP without overhead
→ Perfect for gRPC, streaming, IoT, and high-throughput AI workloads
→ Can proxy… or fully pass traffic through to the backend
Key Differentiator: makes traffic fast by providing raw, low-latency transport and forwarding packets at L4 without inspecting application data.

In short:
If your system needs smart decisions with path based routing, ALB is preferred.
If it needs raw performance, choose NLB.

Modern architectures usually need both.. but knowing where to use each one is what separates a working system from a resilient one.

Hope this visual flow helps clarify the difference a bit.

Anything else you think should be called out?

If you’re looking to build a career in Cloud — here are the top career paths you should know.

Let’s break down 8 key career paths in cloud:

1. Cloud Developer – Build and deploy cloud-native applications
 ↳ Skillset: Proficiency in programming (Python, Java, Go), containers (Docker/Kubernetes), and serverless.

2. Cloud Network Engineer – Design and manage cloud networking infrastructure
 ↳ Skillset: VPCs, subnets, firewalls, load balancers, hybrid connectivity (VPN, Direct Connect).

3. Cloud Security Engineer – Protect and secure cloud environments
 ↳ Skillset: IAM, encryption, threat detection, compliance frameworks (SOC2, GDPR, HIPAA).

4. Cloud Database Engineer – Manage and optimize cloud databases
 ↳ Skillset: SQL & NoSQL systems, replication, performance tuning, backups, and monitoring.

5. Cloud DevOps Engineer – Manage build, deployment, and operations
 ↳ Skillset: CI/CD, Infrastructure as Code (Terraform/Pulumi), observability, automation.

6. Cloud Data Engineer – Develop and maintain data pipelines
 ↳ Skillset: ETL, big data tools (Spark, Hadoop), cloud warehouses (BigQuery, Redshift, Snowflake).

7. Cloud AI/ML Engineer – Deploy and scale machine learning models in the cloud
 ↳ Skillset: ML frameworks (TensorFlow, PyTorch), model deployment (SageMaker, Vertex AI), data pipelines.

8. Cloud Solutions Architect – Design cloud systems for business needs
 ↳ Skillset: System design, multi-cloud strategies, cost optimization, stakeholder communication.

The takeaway:
You don’t need to know everything in cloud.
You just need to identify the role that fits your interest — and go deep into it.

So, get a practitioner-level understanding of cloud first — then pick a track and dive in.

What roles would you add to this list?

CKS is known to be the toughest of all the K8s certs, but with the right prep - it's completely manageable.
Here's a roadmap with vetted free and paid resources..

Also, TL;DR on how CKS differs from CKA:
- With CKA, you learn how to build, run, and operate Kubernetes clusters.
- With CKS, you learn how to secure and protect them.

Key Exam Details:
• 2 hours long
• ~15-20 performance-based tasks
• $445 USD (includes 1 free retake)
• Valid for 2 years
• Passing score: 67%
• Pre-requisite: CKA Required

1. Shift Your Mindset (Week 1)
• Understand what CKS expects: not operations, but secure configurations
• Review security contexts, PodSecurityStandards (PSS), and Kubernetes attack surfaces
(Make sure your K8s basics are fresh ~ RBAC, network policies, admission controller etc)

2. Security Fundamentals (Week 2)
Dive into:
 • AppArmor / seccomp
 • TLS basics & Kubernetes certificates
 • Secrets management
 • Sysdig and Falco
Learn how Linux tools tie into K8s workloads

3. Cluster Hardening (Week 3)
Practice:
 • Securing API server access
 • Limiting kubelet exposure
 • Restricting access via RBAC
 • Configuring audit logs
Use CIS Benchmarks to evaluate your setup

4. Runtime Security & Supply Chain (Week 4)
• Tools like Trivy, OPA/Gatekeeper, Kyverno
• Image scanning, signing, and verification
• Enforcing policies at runtime
• Practice pod security with real-world misconfigurations

Some Free Resources:
• Kubernetes Security Docs: https://lnkd.in/dnvFCqUH
• killer.sh simulator(included with exam)
• KodeKloud Playgrounds (free tier) - https://lnkd.in/dinUBK3P
• Kubernetes Security Youtube: https://lnkd.in/ds6QxXE4

Recommended Paid Resources:
• ACloudGuru/PluralSight - https://lnkd.in/d8yatGs3 (I took this!)
• CKS course on Udemy - https://lnkd.in/dirQdhns
• KodeKloud's CKS course - https://lnkd.in/duGKCAXg
(please make sure the course reflects the most up-to-date curriculum before signing up)

GitHub Repos:
• Certified-Kubernetes-Security-Specialist - https://lnkd.in/drUYpiw4
• Kodekloud - https://lnkd.in/dV3-SRGq

By now, you're already comfortable with yaml indentation and K8s CLI 

So here's what you can do next to save time:
• Use kubectl to create Role/RoleBinding etc objects directly from the CLI 
(instead of copy-pasting YAML from the docs)
• Practice using tools/modules like trivy, apparmor etc
• Get comfortable with audit logs
• Learn to spot misconfigured RBAC

I started learning to code when I was 22.
And no, I wasn’t a computer science student; so the transition was anything but smooth.

I struggled with syntax. I struggled with logic.
I struggled with simply believing I could write a clean function and call it… without seeing those warnings and errors.

And today, it’s even more confusing because people say coding isn’t “necessary” anymore.
But it is ~ and you know why?

A data scientist still writes.
An SDE still builds.
A DevOps engineer still automates.
A cloud engineer still scripts and deploys.

Every role touches code in some form.
They all require coding.

And yes, “vibe coding” makes things look easy.
AI fills half the gaps. Fixing issues in code feels magical.
But you still need to understand the logic behind why the model is suggesting a certain block, what it’s doing behind the curtains.

So if I were starting to learn coding today, I’d follow one framework: CLIMB.

C → Choose your path
Pick your direction first: Web, Data, AI, Cloud, DevOps, Systems.
Your language depends on where you’re going ~ not the other way around.

L → Look at real-world use cases
See what developers actually build:
APIs, cloud automations, dashboards, AI models, pipelines.
It shows you the purpose behind every concept.

I → Identify the fundamentals
Syntax. Variables. Loops. Functions. Data structures.
Don’t skip this. These basics stay with you for life.

M → Master the small stuff
Mini labs. LeetCode. HackerRank. Playgrounds.
Small repetitions build big confidence.

B → Build real projects
Start with scripts and tiny apps (automation, apis).
Then move to full-stack projects, cloud infra, AI workflows, data pipelines.
Projects validate your skills more than anything else.

Because learning to code isn’t hard… it’s just overwhelming.
Structure makes it doable.

If you’re starting today…
CLIMB.
One step at a time.

============================================================

Here's a breakdown of 8 important communication patterns and infrastructure layers used in modern cloud-native systems 
(plus when each one is used):

First — why should you care about communication patterns?

Because the way your services communicate directly shapes your system's :
- scalability (how well it handles growth) 
- reliability (how dependable it is)
- and performance (how fast and efficient it runs)
Choose the wrong pattern, and you’ll hit bottlenecks fast.

Let’s break them down:

1. REST (HTTP/HTTPS APIs)
 ↳ Classic request-response using HTTP methods like GET, POST, PUT, DELETE.
 ↳ Simple, widely adopted, and great for CRUD operations.
Use Case ~ Standard web APIs or when interoperability is key.

2. gRPC
 ↳ High-performance RPC over HTTP/2 with strong typing and streaming support.
 ↳ Enables low-latency, efficient communication between microservices.
Use Case ~ For internal microservices needing speed and tight contracts.

3. Event-Driven Messaging
 ↳ Services emit and consume events asynchronously via queues or brokers.
 ↳ Promotes decoupling and scalability.
Use Case ~ For workflows where services shouldn’t wait on each other.

4. Publish-Subscribe (Pub/Sub)
 ↳ One message, multiple subscribers — no direct link between sender & receiver.
 ↳ Supports real-time updates to many consumers.
Use Case ~ For fan-out messaging like notifications or live feeds.

5. Streaming Data (not a pattern, but an infrastructure layer)
 ↳ Handles continuous data flow (think logs, metrics, IoT).
 ↳ Powers real-time analytics and monitoring.
 Use Case ~ For pipelines that need constant data ingestion and processing.

6. Service Mesh Communication (not a pattern, but an infrastructure layer for inter-service comms)
 ↳ Uses sidecar proxies to manage service-to-service traffic securely.
 ↳ Adds observability, traffic control, and fault tolerance without app code changes.
Use Case ~ In complex microservice systems needing resilience and traceability.

7. WebSockets
 ↳ Keeps a persistent, full-duplex connection open.
 ↳ Enables real-time bidirectional data.
Use Case ~ For live features like chat, gaming, or real-time dashboards.

8. GraphQL APIs
 ↳ Clients request exactly the data they need — nothing more, nothing less.
 ↳ Minimizes over-fetching/under-fetching issues.
Use Case ~ For flexible APIs in modern frontend or microservice architectures.

Start by understanding the basics of REST and event-driven systems — then gradually explore others as your projects grow more complex.

With each new system you build, you'll get a clearer sense of when and why to use each approach.

If you're aiming for Cloud engineer roles, here's how to prep for scenario-based interviews using cloud architecture principles..

Let’s align your interview prep with cloud architecture fundamentals:

1. Scalable Systems
 ↳ Know how to design for traffic surges using load balancers and autoscaling
 ↳ Example Prep: Describe a time you handled unexpected load increases?

2. Highly available, Reliable & Resilient Systems 
 ↳ Understand fault tolerance, multi-AZ/multi-region designs, Disaster Recovery & Backup Strategies
 ↳ Example Prep: Explain how you achieved high availability in your solution.

3. Secure Architecture
 ↳ Be ready to talk IAM, VPC security, encryption etc
 ↳ Example Prep: How did you manage data in your last project? (see the hints for security)

4. Cost-Effective Design
 ↳ Show awareness of billing models, reserved instances, discounts and right-sizing
 ↳ Example Prep: What decisions helped reduce your cloud costs?

5. Automated Deployments
 ↳ Speak to IaC, CI/CD pipelines, and provisioning tools
 ↳ Example Prep: How did automation improve your team’s velocity?

6. Manageable Environments
 ↳ Bring up centralized monitoring, alerting, and logging
 ↳ Example Prep: How did you improve observability in your infrastructure?

7. Interoperability
 ↳Touch on hybrid/multi-cloud setups, API integrations, and vendor-agnostic tooling
 ↳ Example Prep: How did you connect cloud with legacy/on-prem systems?

8. Multi-Tenant Architectures
 ↳ Show understanding of data isolation, access controls, and shared resources
 ↳ Example Prep: How did you design for multiple clients in a shared environment?

9. Flexibility
 ↳ Talk about supporting multiple runtimes, deployment models, or frameworks
 ↳ Example Prep: What did you do to keep your architecture adaptable?

How to prepare?
 → Review your project work through the lens of these principles
 → Practice “challenge → solution → impact” storytelling
 → Use whiteboard tools or mock interviews to walk through system designs
 → Keep metrics handy: latency cut, cost saved, uptime achieved

A few final points:
 → Try connecting principles to real outcomes (for projects as well)
 → Build a bank of 2–3 stories per principle
 → Be ready to explain your design choices and trade-offs
 → You don’t need a fancy title to talk like an architect—just clarity and logic

Author: vsadhwani
Date: Jul 21, 2025

I stepped into the US in Aug 2016 with two things: a student visa and an education loan I had no clue how I’d repay..

I was pursuing a degree I wasn’t even sure I wanted..

My parents were supportive, but there were still questions around:
 - why this field?
 - what’s your plan after graduation?
 - how will you pay back the loan?

To be honest, I didn’t have an answer to any of those.

I was sharing a packed 2-bedroom apartment with 5 other people. Every morning, the only thought on my mind was:
 “What can I do today to start paying that loan back?”

Not - which field will I specialize in? Hardware or Software
Not - what career do I want?
Not - will I get a $70k or $100k job?

It was just: earn something. Anything. The hunger was that real.

I got an assistantship on campus — $8 an hour. 
It wasn’t glamorous, but that first paycheck made me feel a little better about myself. Like I was finally moving.

Then came an internship: $21 an hour. 
That number felt big at the time, and with it came a little more dignity. I finally bought myself a laptop with my own money. I even managed to pay for my last semester’s tuition on my own.

Eventually, I landed my first full-time job — a proud moment. A relief. 
It wasn’t just about the number; it was the fact that I could finally see the debt reducing. 
The loan was always on my mind ~ like a countdown clock ticking away.

Initially, that loan fueled my hunger. 
Not for titles, not for fancy careers, but for solving a very real problem that was breathing down my neck: financial pressure.

But when I look back now — I realize that clock, that pressure, was actually a blessing in disguise. 
If that clock wasn’t ticking, would I have hustled this hard? 
Would I have pushed myself to learn, adapt, and grow so quickly?
I doubt it.

That loan, that struggle — it taught me more than any classroom could:
- how to work harder and smarter
- how to navigate uncertainty
- how to create my own opportunities

Today, I don’t carry the same fears or doubts I once did. But I carry the lessons from that phase with me every single day.

Whenever I talk about career choices, growth, or pivots, I always remind people -
"Sometimes it’s the urgency of your problems that forces you to discover the best version of yourself"

If you’re facing struggles — job hunting, financial pressure, uncertainty; I hope they lead you to the best version of yourself.
Because that’s where real growth begins.

If you’re in cloud, these 4 levels of expertise directly impact your compensation.

1. Cloud Enthusiasts
→ Tinker with free tiers, spin up EC2s/VMs, deploy static websites, basic terraform. 
→ Know the basics but limited production exposure.
Compensation: Entry-level salaries or internships ($60,000 – $90,000).

2. Cloud Engineers
→ Confident with Infra as Code, CI/CD, Kubernetes intermediate, monitoring, and security. 
→ Can run production workloads, but mostly follow existing patterns.
Compensation: $95,000 – $140,000.

3. Senior/Platform Engineers
→ Architect scalable systems across multiple services (load balancing, networking, observability, automation, AI Infrastructure). 
→ Bridge DevOps + Security. Own reliability at scale.
Compensation: $140,000 – $200,000+.

4. Cloud Architects / Principal Engineers
→ Design multi-region, hybrid, or AI-native architectures. 
→ Optimize cost, governance, and compliance. Mentor teams and set org-wide cloud standards.
Compensation: $200,000 – $350,000+ (with bonuses/equity).
⸻

Cloud is vast.
But if you understand this hierarchy, you’ll know exactly where to double down and increase your chances.

This isn’t an exhaustive list ~ there are many roles and paths in cloud; but it should give you direction on what depth looks like at each stage.

Take a strategic approach. Pick a level. Deep dive.
And position yourself as an expert there.

We need networking for cloud and application connectivity -- that's the focus here.

And understanding networking will make troubleshooting,
And designing architectures a whole lot easier.

Here's the guide I've put together!

Core Topics You Need:
• Basic Networking Concepts (IP, DNS, Protocols)
• Network Infrastructure and Topologies
• Cloud Networking (VPC, Subnets, Route Tables)

• Service Discovery and Distributed Systems
• Container Networking (Docker/K8s)
• Monitoring, Logging and Tracing
• Network Security & Firewalls
• Performance and Optimization
• Software Defined Networking (SDN)

This is the practical networking you'll use everyday or at least need to know.

Real-world applications to be familiar with:
↳ Setting up multi-tier applications
↳ Troubleshooting connectivity issues
↳ Implementing zero-trust security
↳ Automating network configurations

Don't let networking basics hold you back.
You got this!

So many DevOps tools, so little time. Sound familiar?
(This DaaB roadmap can help)

The DevOps landscape is massive.
But you need a systematic approach, not more tools. 

And DaaB (DevOps as a Burger) provides a framework for this!

Here's a layer-by-layer breakdown :
↳ Learn a programming language (Python, Go, ...) to write automation scripts

↳ Master one operating system (Linux, Unix, ...) and its command-line interface (CLI)

↳ Brush up your Networking basics: TCP/IP Fundamentals, DNS, HTTP/s, FTP, SSL...

↳ Master server management, web servers including fundamentals of proxies 

↳ Study caching solutions (Redis, Memcached) and get those database basics right (SQL, NoSQL).

↳ Explore infrastructure as code in detail along with container management 

↳ Study CI/CD practices for automating app delivery and deployment stages 

↳ Learn about different monitoring techniques for real-time oversight of applications, services, and infrastructure 

↳ Gain hands-on experience with Cloud providers like AWS, Azure, GCP,..

You'll find tons of DevOps tools out there
But you only need a handful to get the job done! 

Think of it like this burger – it's the combination of all the layers working together that makes it great, not just one ingredient.

Want a detailed breakdown of each layer? Drop a comment or dm me, and I'll share some free github resources to get started.

If I had to restart my tech career today,
these are the 9 soft skills I’d learn first:

1. Master the 4-Line Update
↳ What you’re doing, what’s done, what’s blocked, what’s next.
↳ Clear updates make you look organized and reliable.

2. Understand the 7-38-55 Rule
↳ Words matter less than tone and presence.
↳ How you say it often matters more than what you say.

3. Record yourself in meetings
↳ You don’t sound how you think you sound.
↳ Sharpen clarity. Cut filler words. Improve delivery.

4. Send Weekly Updates
↳ Friday recap: wins, risks, next steps.
↳ Visibility compounds — especially during review season.

5. Say Thank You Specifically
↳ “Thanks for fixing that prod issue last night” > “Good job.”
↳ Specific recognition builds real trust.

6. Be the Person Who Unblocks the Team
↳ See friction? Fix it or escalate it.
↳ Initiative gets remembered.

7. Leave Things 1% Better
↳ Improve a doc. Clean a script. Simplify a process.
↳ Small upgrades compound over time.

8. Review Yourself Monthly
↳ What did you learn? What would you do differently?
↳ Growth doesn’t happen accidentally.

9. Eliminate Complaining & Gossip
↳ Your words shape your reputation.
↳ Propose solutions instead.

The AI era is accelerating.

Hard skills evolve fast.
Tools change.
Frameworks get replaced.

What doesn’t change?

Your reputation.
Your initiative.
The trust you build.

Soft skills aren’t optional in 2026.
They’re leverage.

If this resonates, share it with your team.

I still remember my first paycheck as a software engineer.
And this time, my paycheck was not only going for rent.

The first thing I wanted to do was send some money home to my parents - it felt like a pat on the back, that I could finally start giving back for everything they carried me through.

I kept the second bi-weekly check for my loan, but this one was about me ~ and how I wanted to spend it.

So I sent $1,000 out of the $2,700 I received.
Didn’t even tell them. Used the economy option :) it took 3–5 days.

When the money arrived, my dad called - surprised and grateful, but also asked out of curiosity: “How much did you actually send?”

That’s when I learned the hidden side of sending money across borders:
→ Transfer fees you see upfront
→ Exchange rate markups you don’t notice until it’s too late
→ And the waiting game before it shows up in their account

It hit me, because the money I lost in the process might feel small here, but back home it could cover groceries, electricity, or even part of my sister’s tuition.

No one tells you this when you move abroad — how much quietly leaks away every time you hit send.

So I moved over to Aspora, and for once, the numbers actually matched what I expected:

→ Exchange rates - EXACTLY what you see on Google(no markup)
→ Instant transfers, 24/7 with a lower fee ( to all the others )
→ First 3 transfers free
→ Full transparency on what your family will actually receive

Now, when I send money, my parents get an amount comparable to the Google rate. No guessing. No leaks.

Because every dollar I earn isn’t just mine - it’s meant for the people who made it possible.

--- Post 215 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Sep 19, 2025

Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7369386932939726850

If you’re aiming to build expertise in Kubernetes — here's why you should start with Linux.

Every K8s feature maps back to a Linux capability; here's how it's linked 👇 

→ containerd
↳ The container runtime that actually runs containers.
↳ Linux link: Uses namespaces (isolation), cgroups (resource limits), OverlayFS (image layers), and seccomp (security).

→ kubelet
↳ The agent on every node that manages pods and talks to the API server.
↳ Linux link: Relies on systemd/init to run services and cgroups to enforce pod resource usage.

→ kube-proxy
↳ Handles service networking and routes traffic between pods.
↳ Linux link: Uses iptables rules to manage and route cluster networking.

→ Pods (runtime)
↳ The smallest deployable unit in Kubernetes, made of one or more containers.
↳ Linux link: Pods exist because of namespaces and cgroups (for isolation and resources), with OverlayFS and seccomp layered in for filesystem and security.

→ Network Policies
↳ Define how pods can communicate with each other.
↳ Linux link: Enforced by iptables through CNI plugins, which apply traffic filtering rules.

→ Persistent Volume Mounts
↳ Attach storage to pods so data isn’t lost when containers restart.
↳ Linux link: Uses mount and filesystem namespaces to manage storage at the OS level.

→ Security Context
↳ Lets you control pod/container permissions (e.g., run as non-root).
↳ Linux link: Uses user namespaces, seccomp, AppArmor, and SELinux for isolation and enforcement.

→ Pod Resource Limits
↳ Define how much CPU/memory a pod can use.
↳ Linux link: Enforced directly via cgroups.

→ Image Management
↳ How Kubernetes pulls, stores, and runs container images.
↳ Linux link: Relies on OverlayFS to manage image layers efficiently.

⸻

The takeaway:
Every major Kubernetes component depends on Linux kernel features.
So if you want to master Kubernetes, you MUST understand Linux first.

Here’s a quick breakdown of 7 DevOps Workload Patterns — and why they matter.

If you’re working in DevOps or cloud-native environments, understanding these patterns isn’t just nice to have. It’s essential. 

Each pattern solves a specific class of problems in the software delivery lifecycle — from code commits to production monitoring; and knowing them helps you distinguish tasks, architect smarter pipelines, and optimize for actual results.

Here are the 7 patterns you should know:
1. Continuous Integration (CI) Workloads
 ↳ Build and test code automatically on every commit
 ↳ Generate artifacts like Docker images
Use Case ~ To catch bugs early, maintain code quality, and speed up integration across teams.

2. Continuous Deployment (CD) Workloads
 ↳ Automate deployment after passing CI
 ↳ Support blue-green and canary releases
Use Case ~ To push reliable code to production frequently and safely with minimal downtime.

3. Infrastructure Provisioning Workloads
 ↳ Define infrastructure as code
 ↳ Manage infrastructure consistently
Use Case ~ To automate cloud resource creation and keep infrastructure reproducible and version-controlled.

4. Containerization Workloads
 ↳ Build and package applications into containers
 ↳ Optimize images for microservices and CI/CD
Use Case ~ To standardize deployment environments and improve portability across dev, test, and production.

5. Serverless Workloads
 ↳ Develop and deploy functions without managing servers
 ↳ Automate scaling based on demand
Use Case ~ To reduce operational overhead and only pay for compute when code is running.

6. Monitoring & Observability Workloads
 ↳ Collect metrics, logs, and traces
 ↳ Alert teams on anomalies or breaches
Use Case ~ To maintain system health, troubleshoot issues quickly, and ensure performance benchmarks.

7. Security & Compliance Workloads (DevSecOps)
 ↳ Integrate security scanning into CI/CD
 ↳ Automate compliance and secret management
Use Case ~ To embed security early in the development process and stay compliant with industry standards.

You have probably heard a lot about different CI/CD, Containerization Tools & Practices - but knowing the specific use case they solve is just as important; and these patterns guides you through that.

Author: vsadhwani

Here’s a quick breakdown of 7 DevOps Workload Patterns — and why they matter.

If you’re working in DevOps or cloud-native environments, understanding these patterns isn’t just nice to have. It’s essential. 

Each pattern solves a specific class of problems in the software delivery lifecycle — from code commits to production monitoring; and knowing them helps you distinguish tasks, architect smarter pipelines, and optimize for actual results.

Here are the 7 patterns you should know:
1. Continuous Integration (CI) Workloads
 ↳ Build and test code automatically on every commit
 ↳ Generate artifacts like Docker images
Use Case ~ To catch bugs early, maintain code quality, and speed up integration across teams.

2. Continuous Deployment (CD) Workloads
 ↳ Automate deployment after passing CI
 ↳ Support blue-green and canary releases
Use Case ~ To push reliable code to production frequently and safely with minimal downtime.

3. Infrastructure Provisioning Workloads
 ↳ Define infrastructure as code
 ↳ Manage infrastructure consistently
Use Case ~ To automate cloud resource creation and keep infrastructure reproducible and version-controlled.

4. Containerization Workloads
 ↳ Build and package applications into containers
 ↳ Optimize images for microservices and CI/CD
Use Case ~ To standardize deployment environments and improve portability across dev, test, and production.

5. Serverless Workloads
 ↳ Develop and deploy functions without managing servers
 ↳ Automate scaling based on demand
Use Case ~ To reduce operational overhead and only pay for compute when code is running.

6. Monitoring & Observability Workloads
 ↳ Collect metrics, logs, and traces
 ↳ Alert teams on anomalies or breaches
Use Case ~ To maintain system health, troubleshoot issues quickly, and ensure performance benchmarks.

7. Security & Compliance Workloads (DevSecOps)
 ↳ Integrate security scanning into CI/CD
 ↳ Automate compliance and secret management
Use Case ~ To embed security early in the development process and stay compliant with industry standards.

You have probably heard a lot about different CI/CD, Containerization Tools & Practices - but knowing the specific use case they solve is just as important; and these patterns guides you through that.

DevOps ≠ SRE ≠ Cloud

They overlap.
They share tools.
But the focus is different.

In my first company, I was hired as a DevOps Engineer ~
but I also worked on internal automation tools.

It took me time to clearly distinguish between these roles.

• Cloud is about the platform
• DevOps is about the process
• SRE is about reliability

And Platform Engineering is about building self-service internal developer platforms (IDPs)… often sitting across all three.

There’s heavy overlap in the tech stack, but responsibilities differ.

Let’s break it down:

⸻
Cloud Engineers own:

• Cloud infrastructure design & deployment
• Resource provisioning & optimization
• Security implementation
• Cost-performance management
• Troubleshooting cloud-native systems
• Networking, IAM, and scaling strategies
⸻
DevOps Engineers drive:

• CI/CD pipelines
• Infrastructure as Code (Terraform, CloudFormation, etc.)
• Deployment strategies (blue/green, canary)
• Automation & scripting
• Environment consistency
⸻
Site Reliability Engineers (SREs) ensure:

• System reliability & availability
• Defining SLOs / SLIs / error budgets
• Incident response & postmortems
• Monitoring & alerting strategy
• Performance tuning
• Chaos engineering
⸻

Even though the lines are blurring, AI is amplifying the impact of each role.
Automation is smarter. Reliability is measurable. Platform abstraction is accelerating.

If you’re confused between Cloud, DevOps, and SRE brackets and wondering what other roles fit into each ~

check out the full breakdown here: https://lnkd.in/gkydiDET

Author: vsadhwani

URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7418698408561016832

Kubernetes layers you need to know as a DevOps engineer in 2026

K8s applications isn't just about containers - it's about the EXTRA layers that make it enterprise-ready

1. Load Balancer:
→ This is the entry point to the architecture.
→ It helps distribute incoming network traffic across multiple backend servers & pods
→ Ensures high availability and fault tolerance.
→ Prevents any single server from being overwhelmed, ensuring even load distribution

2. Ingress Controller:
→ It acts as a gateway managing external access to services within the cluster.
→ It's crucial for controlling and routing HTTP/S traffic.
→ [IMPORTANT] Gateway APIs are a replacement for Ingress, with multi-protocol support, better delegation, and extensibility ~ also more extensible and expressive than traditional Ingress

3. Kube Proxy:
→ This maintains network rules and handles forwarding of traffic and communication between pods.

4. Sidecars:
→ Sidecars are an extension pattern in Kubernetes, where additional containers run alongside the main application container within the same Pod.
→ They help in extending and enhancing the functionalities of the main app, such as logging, monitoring, etc., without modifying its code.

5. Service Mesh:
→ Service Mesh introduces a dedicated infrastructure layer that adds features like service-to-service communication, mTLS security, observability, traffic management, and more.
→ This is increasingly important for managing microservices architectures.

Knowing these components is key to crafting robust, scalable, and maintainable Kubernetes applications.

Author: vsadhwani
Date: May 17, 2025

DevOps Engineer vs Platform Engineer vs SRE – how their tech stacks & responsibilities differ (and what to expect in interviews)

In the world of cloud and infrastructure roles, titles can feel interchangeable. 
But in reality — each has a unique focus, and your toolset evolves depending on the role and level.

Here’s a breakdown of the typical stacks and core responsibilities 
(Entry to Mid-Level Roles)

1. DevOps Engineer
 • Infrastructure as Code (Terraform, Cloudformation, Pulumi)
 • CI/CD pipelines (Jenkins, GitLab CI, ArgoCD)
 • Automation & Scripting (Python, Bash)
 • Virtualization & Containerization
 • Cloud-native deployments (AWS/GCP/Azure)
 • Cross-team collaboration with devs & QA

Shared Responsibilities:
→ Monitoring (Prometheus, Datadog etc)
→ Continuous reliability improvements
→ Deployment automation
→ Scalable cloud infrastructure

Interview focus:
→ CI/CD pipeline design
→ IaC practices and tools
→ Troubleshooting broken builds
→ Networking, Security & Cloud Cost Optimization
-------

2. Site Reliability Engineer (SRE)
 • Defining & tracking SLOs, SLIs
 • Ensuring uptime and high availability
 • Incident response (on-call rotations, root cause analysis)
 • Observability (Grafana, ELK, etc)

Shared Responsibilities:
 → Real-time monitoring and alert systems
 → Process improvement and automation
 → Capacity planning
 → Performance optimization

Interview focus:
→ Reliability design patterns
→ Incident handling scenarios
→ Scaling systems under load
→ Writing postmortems

-------

3. Platform Engineer
 • Building internal developer platforms
 • Cloud infrastructure (including Kubernetes)
 • Container orchestration & scalability
 • Service mesh (Istio, Linkerd)
 • Application Lifecycle Management

Shared Responsibilities
 → Deployment pipelines
 → Cloud infra management (multi-account, multi-region setups)
 → System performance tuning
 → Scaling and provisioning

Interview focus:
→ Designing internal platforms or PaaS
→ Advanced Kubernetes architecture
→ Developer experience tooling
→ System design with scalability in mind

-------

The main takeaway
While the tools/concepts may overlap, the "focus" and "mindset" differ. So create a roadmap that aligns with the role. 

If you're preparing for interviews: a solid grasp of infrastructure design, reliability patterns, and automation strategy will take you further.

Author: vsadhwani

If you’re starting your Cloud / DevOps journey in 2025 — make sure Prometheus and Grafana are on your roadmap..

Here’s a quick overview:

Both Prometheus and Grafana are observability tools focused on monitoring —
→ Prometheus collects and stores metrics (plus alerting).
→ Grafana visualizes those metrics through rich dashboards.

Companies can:
- self-host them on VMs or Kubernetes, 
- or choose managed offerings from major cloud providers (AWS, Azure, GCP) + grafana cloud

Let’s dive in further:

Prometheus handles:
↳ Collecting and storing metrics (from servers, containers, apps)
↳ Alerting when thresholds are breached (via Alertmanager)
↳ Infrastructure monitoring with exporters
↳ SLA/SLO tracking

Grafana shines at:
↳ Building rich, interactive dashboards for metrics
↳ Business KPI monitoring alongside system data
↳ Log & metric correlation for root cause analysis
↳ Visualizing trends for capacity planning

Why are they preferred for monitoring:
• Real-time visibility across systems
• Faster incident response with both historical + live data
• Simplified application performance tracking (latency, error rates, throughput)
• Deep insights into container & Kubernetes health (pods, resources, clusters)
• Open-source and cloud-agnostic → no vendor lock-in

That’s why they’ve become the go-to choice for modern observability.

⸻

Monitoring isn’t just about collecting data — it’s about using the right tools to see the bigger picture and act before things break.

Question for you:
Have you used Prometheus & Grafana mainly for infra monitoring, or also for business KPIs?

4 years at Google, 4 key things I learned as a Cloud Architect

When I joined, I wasn’t very familiar with pre-sales roles.

 I went from debugging sessions, failed pipelines, and K8s troubleshooting moments…

 …to sitting with customers, scoping business needs, and translating them into technical requirements and solutions.

Here are 4 things that started making sense along the way:

1. You won’t know how to architect apps until you’ve been hands-on
- Tools, configs, troubleshooting — it’s the doing that validates your judgement.

2. People teach you more than documents
- A 20-minute chat with a teammate can save you hours of figuring things out 'alone' and weeks of feeling stuck..

3. Adaptability beats expertise
- There’s no playbook and no fixed niche — when the business, scale, or technology changes, you adapt (hello AI)

4. Meetings where you don’t speak up still teach you something
- Listening to how others frame problems and defend decisions has been a masterclass on its own — especially in moments when I was too nervous to speak.

Reflecting on it now, my growth had little to do with knowing it all. 

It came from staying open to learning, even when I thought I should already know (a classy case of imposter syndrome)

And I wasn’t wrong. Those hands-on experiences shaped my design decisions far more than any theory ever could.

Real growth comes from showing up, doing the work, and letting each project leave you a little sharper than before.

Btw, if you want to learn 5 minutes of cloud every week, I share cloud insights and lessons from my own journey through my newsletter. Do check it out.

If you’re in the Cloud + DevOps ecosystem, this one’s for you.
80+ tools. 10 domains. 1 evolving ecosystem.

Here’s what it teaches us to take forward into 2026:

• Infrastructure ≠ Servers anymore → IaC is now the standard. Terraform, Pulumi, and Crossplane are defining modern cloud workflows.

• Automation is the new deployment pipeline → GitHub Actions, ArgoCD, and JenkinsX are bridging CI/CD with GitOps.

• Security is shifting left → Secrets management (Vault, SOPS) and policy enforcement (OPA, Kyverno) are built into pipelines — not bolted on later.

• Networking is cloud-native → From Cloudflare to open-source api management platforms, the modern stack is API-first and mesh-driven.

• AI meets CloudOps → Agents can now auto-tune configs, monitor drift, and suggest scaling actions before issues arise.

• Observability runs on intelligence → OpenTelemetry, Loki, and Prometheus are merging with AIOps for predictive insights
(there's a great project for this - comment if you want to check that out).

TL;DR:
Don’t see this periodic table as just a list of tools ~
look at how they connect, and you’ll notice how Cloud, DevOps, and AI are finally converging into one ecosystem.

--- Post 232 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Dec 18, 2025

URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7396753154517676032

If you work with Kubernetes, Helm is the tool you must know for managing an application’s entire lifecycle.

To make it simpler, here is a visual cheatsheet that breaks Helm into the eight categories you actually use in real environments.

→ Plugin Management
Install, update, list, or remove Helm plugins that extend capabilities.

→ Repos Management
Add, remove, or search chart repositories.
This is where most engineers start.

→ Install and Uninstall Apps
The core workflow: install charts, pass values files, debug installations, and uninstall cleanly.

→ Chart Management
Create your own charts, lint them, package them, and inspect what is inside.

→ Release Monitoring
List all releases, check statuses, filter by namespaces, and inspect history.

→ Upgrade and Rollback
Upgrade apps safely, handle atomic rollouts, or rollback to a known-good version.

→ Release Information
Fetch manifests, hooks, notes, or all resources linked to a release.

→ Help and Inspection
Check versions, get command help, and inspect environment details.

Helm simplifies Kubernetes complexity, but only when you understand how the commands fit together.

If you’re deep into Kubernetes prep or handling day-to-day platform tasks, this cheatsheet will make Helm feel a lot less heavy.

Which Helm command group do you use the most?

--- Post 233 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Dec 18, 2025
Engagement: 592 reactions, 27 comments, 69 reposts (Score: 3,368)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7396581728867438592

If you work with Kubernetes, Helm is the tool you must know for managing an application’s entire lifecycle.

To make it simpler, here is a visual cheatsheet that breaks Helm into the eight categories you actually use in real environments.

→ Plugin Management
Install, update, list, or remove Helm plugins that extend capabilities.

→ Repos Management
Add, remove, or search chart repositories.
This is where most engineers start.

→ Install and Uninstall Apps
The core workflow: install charts, pass values files, debug installations, and uninstall cleanly.

→ Chart Management
Create your own charts, lint them, package them, and inspect what is inside.

→ Release Monitoring
List all releases, check statuses, filter by namespaces, and inspect history.

→ Upgrade and Rollback
Upgrade apps safely, handle atomic rollouts, or rollback to a known-good version.

→ Release Information
Fetch manifests, hooks, notes, or all resources linked to a release.

→ Help and Inspection
Check versions, get command help, and inspect environment details.

Helm simplifies Kubernetes complexity, but only when you understand how the commands fit together.

If you’re deep into Kubernetes prep or handling day-to-day platform tasks, this cheatsheet will make Helm feel a lot less heavy.

Which Helm command group do you use the most?

--- Post 234 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Sep 19, 2025
Engagement: 587 reactions, 27 comments, 64 reposts (Score: 3,333)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7371560868318187521

Kubernetes is powerful, but let’s be honest: the ecosystem can feel endless.
(And no, this isn’t a detailed tool breakdown post 😅)

The fix is to break things into focus areas. 

Instead of trying to learn every logo in the stack, think of Kubernetes tools in buckets — each solving a specific problem:

→ Monitoring & Observability
Keep track of cluster health, logs, metrics, and performance.

→ Infrastructure Orchestration
Manage infra as code, provision resources, and automate deployments. This is how teams scale reliably.

→ Networking
Ensure pods, services, and clusters can talk to each other securely and efficiently.

→ Security
Scan for vulnerabilities, manage secrets, enforce policies. Security is never “add-on” in Kubernetes.

→ Container Runtimes
The engines that run your containers — from Docker to CRI-O to containerd.

→ Cluster Management
The tools to actually create, upgrade, and maintain Kubernetes clusters across cloud and on-prem.

⸻

Each of these focus areas has plenty of managed and open-source tool options.
Pick a stack that fits your current environment, niche, and even interests.

Skim through this wheel — notice what feels familiar, and see what else is out there to stay relevant.

TL;DR:
You don’t need to learn every tool at once.
Start with the bucket that aligns with your role or project — and dive in.

Which Kubernetes “bucket” do you work with the most today?

• AI for DevOps (ChatOps bots, AI-driven pipeline optimization)
• Monitoring with AIOps (detect drift, auto-heal incidents)
• Security automation with AI (policy as code, threat detection)
• MLOPs basics - build one project if you can!
↳ Target: Cloud Engineer + AI Specialist (avg base comp ~$140K+)

--- Post 236 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Mar 18, 2026
Engagement: 587 reactions, 38 comments (Score: 3,315)

I realized 6 months into my master’s in the US that I had chosen the wrong career path..

and going back wasn’t an option.

Loans. Expectations back home. A plan everyone believed in.

And deep down, I knew I've messed it up.

I came to the US with a draft version of a plan.

Electrical & Computer Engineering.
Hardware design focus.

It sounded ambitious
It looked impressive on research papers.
It felt like the “right” choice.

But somewhere between lectures and lab sessions, I felt it.

Confusion. Pressure. Misalignment.

I was studying hard.
Keeping my grades just high enough to stay safe.
But I couldn’t see myself surviving, let alone thriving.. on that path.

And that’s a dangerous realization when you’ve:
→ Moved across the world
→ Taken loans
→ Built some expectations back home

For almost a year, I wrestled with it.
Is it just difficult?
Am I giving up too soon?
Should I push one more year and try working it out?

Switching wasn’t simple.

In the US system, you don’t just change interests.
You change degree requirements.
You extend timelines.
You increase costs.

So I navigated carefully.

I spoke with my advisor.
I enrolled in software courses.

It was a completely different world.

With no real coding background.
No DSA foundations.
A lot of fear.

But I had to try...

Those courses changed everything.

Software clicked (coding was still a weak spot) ~ but seeing something I built come alive after hours of debugging (and living on Stack Overflow) felt different.

I landed an internship with whatever little I could comprehend from the software world at that time.

Then transitioned into cloud through a migration project.

Hardware → Software → Cloud → AWS → Google → Nvidia.

Looking back, choosing the “wrong” path
is what helped me discover the right one.

Not going to lie.. it takes a lot of courage to pivot.

But admitting something isn’t working
is the first step toward building something that will.

Author: vsadhwani
Date: May 17, 2025

Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7293327292619976706

15 Linux commands every DevOps engineer should know
(Part 1)

As an SDE turned DevOps in my first job,
One thing I learned in my on-call weeks:

📌 You better have your commands ready (non-negotiable)!

Either practice until it becomes muscle memory, or keep a cheat sheet handy. 

Ideally, you can do both 👀

Here's my DevOps cheatsheet of Linux commands:

System & Process Management:
• ps aux | grep process_name
• top / htop
• systemctl status service_name

File Operations:
• find / -name filename
• tar -czvf archive.tar.gz /path
• scp source destination

Network Tools:
• curl -I url
• ping hostname
• traceroute hostname

Log Analysis:
• tail -f logfile
• grep -r "error" /var/log
• journalctl -u service

Performance Analysis:
• vmstat 1
• iostat
• sar -u

These are your best friend commands when you are:
• running a resource check and optimizing on it
• handling files and archive operations and transfers
• checking network operations and performances
• analyzing logs and monitoring apps
• identifying resource performance of your app.

Remember: DevOps is all about automation,
But it's about being able to support when things break too.

P.S. Which command do you use most often at work?

♻️ Repost to help someone. 
🔔 Follow Vishakha Sadhwani for more.

The first time I applied for AWS, I had a referral — and I was rejected..
The second time, I didn’t even apply

But little did I know, this one tech workshop - would lead me straight to an onsite.

--

Here’s how it happened:

I came across this AWS Engineer Learning Series happening nearby.
So I took a day off, hopped on a train, and spent the day learning directly from AWS engineers.

The session was focused on AWS developer and mobile services — walking us through the workflows to build a real-time serverless AI app.

At the end, there was a challenge:
 → Build a serverless AI chatbot using what you just learned.

Now, I already had some hands-on experience with AWS DevOps services.
But I wasn’t familiar with the developer side of the house.
Many of the services were new to me.

That’s where the foundation helped — and I could connect some dots.
That flipped a switch for me.

A week later, instead of a “thanks for attending” email, I had an onsite AWS interview invite.

It hit me then: the best opportunities aren’t always on careers pages or through referrals.

They’re also in places where you show up, learn, and prove yourself.

If you’re trying to break into Cloud, here are paths you should explore (beyond job portals):
- Hackathons (tech companies scout talent here)
- Technical challenges & capture-the-flag events
- Webinars and learning series with live problem-solving
- Student/community ambassador programs
- Certification bootcamps with real-world labs

Sometimes, those side doors open faster than the front door.

If you’re exploring serverless and want to see how AWS, Azure, and GCP stack up side by side — this cheat sheet is for you.

Whether you’re building a new serverless app, designing a multi-cloud architecture, or just trying to wrap your head around the landscape, having a clear comparison of key services can save hours of confusion.

Here’s what it covers:
→ Compute
 Go serverless without managing servers using Lambda (AWS), Azure Functions, or Cloud Functions (GCP) to build scalable apps with ease.

→ Containers & App Hosting
 Deploy containers or apps without worrying about infrastructure with AWS Fargate, Azure App Service, and Cloud Run on GCP.

→ Security & Access Control
 Secure your serverless apps using identity management tools like Amazon Cognito, Azure Active Directory, and Google IAM.

→ Cloud Messaging & Workflows
 Coordinate services and events with messaging tools like SNS (AWS), Azure Event Grid, or Cloud Pub/Sub, plus orchestration solutions like Step Functions (AWS) or Logic Apps.

→ Analytics & Developer Tools
 Harness data and streamline dev workflows using tools like Kinesis & Cloud9 (AWS), BigQuery, or Azure Stream Analytics & Visual Studio.
and more.. 

I’ll be diving into serverless in my newsletter this coming Monday — subscribe here if you’re looking for a step-by-step learning roadmap: https://lnkd.in/dBNJPv9U

My friend Jason Smith also shares some fantastic serverless use cases in his newsletter — check it out here: https://lnkd.in/dh7pwZRs

Hope this helps you get a clear, side-by-side view of the serverless ecosystem across the three major clouds.

If you’re pursuing DevOps in 2025 — here are the skills and tools showing up in job postings right now.

Two years ago, DevOps was just another high-demand role in tech.
Today, it’s supercharged by cloud, automation, and now, AI.

Top skills in current job postings:
 1 – Terraform 88% (+9%)
 2 – Python 80% (+8%)
 3 – Kubernetes 76% (+6%)
 4 – Cloud 
 …and many more.

The pattern:
- Infrastructure as Code remains consistently in demand.
- Container orchestration has become standard in most environments.

Why DevOps talent is in demand:
 ↳ Cloud-native expertise is essential — 83% of organizations now use multi-cloud approaches. If you can work across Cloud(AWS, GCP, Azure) and Kubernetes, you have a clear advantage.

 ↳ Architects who combine DevOps, cloud, and AI skills are building scalable, secure, AI-ready infrastructure.

 ↳ Platform engineering adoption is rising as companies prioritize developer productivity.

AI isn’t replacing DevOps — it’s making it more effective.
- Predictive analytics for incidents
- Self-healing infrastructure
- Smarter pipelines

But none of this works without strong fundamentals: cloud architecture, CI/CD, automation, and scripting.

some final tips:
→ Learn at least two automation tools (Terraform + Kubernetes is a good combination)
→ Go deep with one cloud, but stay familiar with the others
→ Build strong scripting skills in Python and bash
→ Explore AI integrations, but master CI/CD and infrastructure practices first

Source : Nana Janashia

If you’re a Cloud Engineer, here are the 5 skills that will actually move the needle for you in 2026.

Not for the hype ~ but AI and DevOps are no longer separate worlds.

Here’s what will truly give you an edge:
1) GitOps & Platform Engineering
↳ Cloud teams are moving from “manually setting up pipelines” to “building internal platforms devs can self-serve.”
↳ Knowing GitOps workflows and tools like ArgoCD/FluxCD puts you right in the center of that shift.

2) Infrastructure as APIs (Crossplane, Terraform)
↳ Infra isn’t just YAML + CLI anymore. Crossplane turns infra into APIs with k8s; Terraform still leads IaC.
↳ If you can abstract infrastructure for your org, you immediately stand out.

3) Observability & AIOps
↳ Metrics, traces, logs… and now AI-driven correlation and anomaly detection.
↳ Companies want engineers who can deploy systems and explain what’s happening under the hood.

4) AI Infrastructure & Model Deployment
↳ GPU provisioning, autoscaling, model serving, inference optimization: this is becoming core cloud engineering.
↳ LLMs are fun, but productionizing them is where real engineering begins.

5) Event-Driven Architecture & API Intelligence
↳ Modern systems run on events: streaming, async flows, pub/sub, real-time pipelines.
↳ Combine that with strong API intelligence (rate limits, routing, auth, caching), and you can build systems that scale without breaking.

When I was preparing for Google’s Role-Related Knowledge (RRK) round, I had a gap — I didn’t have deep Google Cloud expertise. But I had a strategy.

First, I did a self-assessment of my skills — here’s where I stood:
✔️ Strong cloud fundamentals (thanks to AWS experience)
 ✔️ Pipeline automation
 ✔️ System design principles 
 ✔️ Observability tools & services
 ❗ App orchestration — still made me nervous

The job description heavily hinted at Kubernetes, so I made a targeted move: diving into the CKA (Certified Kubernetes Administrator) cert prep.

Why certification prep?
Because it gave me a clear learning path — before that, I was all over the place with my Kubernetes understanding.

No, I didn’t take the actual exam (I only had a week!), but the prep alone set me up with the structure and confidence I needed.

Those hands-on labs didn’t just teach me Kubernetes — they prepped me with technical + soft skills.

During the whiteboarding session, I intentionally included Kubernetes in my architecture diagram.
When the interviewer probed deeper, I could explain my choices with real examples from the labs I’d completed.

Not all of us will have the same story — but doing a skills audit helped me build a focused strategy.
So if you’re preparing for an interview, start there! It really does give you a confidence boost.

And remember — you don’t need to be an expert in everything.
Focus on understanding enough to connect the dots and show that you’re someone who can learn and adapt.

What was your cloud interview journey like? Share below!

--- Post 250 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Dec 18, 2025
Engagement: 550 reactions, 32 comments, 58 reposts (Score: 3,186)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7401662936449986560

If you’re just starting out or leveling up with K8s ~ here’s a quick breakdown of the four main certifications.

Also what they focus on, and how difficult they are.

Here’s a simple breakdown to help you choose where to start and understand the overlaps:

1. KCNA – Kubernetes & Cloud Native Associate
Cost: $250
Questions: ~60
Difficulty: Beginner
Focus: Fundamentals
→ What is Cloud Native
→ Container basics
→ Kubernetes architecture
→ Container runtime (CRI)
→ Docker vs containerd
→ Core Kubernetes objects

Best for beginners or anyone new to cloud-native concepts.
---
CKAD : Certified Kubernetes Application Developer
Cost: $445
Questions: ~15–20 hands-on tasks
Difficulty: Intermediate
Focus: Application Workloads
→ Pods, Deployments, ReplicaSets
→ ConfigMaps & Secrets
→ Resource limits
→ Jobs & CronJobs
→ Debugging workloads

Great for developers, DevOps engineers, and anyone building applications on Kubernetes.
---

CKA – Certified Kubernetes Administrator

Cost: $445
Questions: ~15–20 hands-on tasks
Difficulty: Intermediate → Advanced
Focus: Cluster Operations
→ Kubernetes architecture
→ etcd
→ Control plane components
→ Scheduling
→ Networking
→ Kubelet & Kube-proxy
→ Cluster maintenance & troubleshooting
The core certification most professionals aim for ~ overlaps heavily with KCNA and CKAD.
---

CKS – Certified Kubernetes Security Specialist
Cost: $445
Questions: ~15–20 hands-on tasks
Difficulty: Advanced
(Requires CKA first)
Focus: Security
→ Kubernetes attack surface
→ CIS benchmarks
→ Pod security
→ Authentication & authorization
→ Network policies
→ Image & runtime security
→ Secrets & encryption

The most advanced and security-focused exam.

There are common topics across all four certifications, and together they give you a strong, practical understanding of Kubernetes.

And as always ~ no certification guarantees a job, but K8s certs are an excellent way to level up your skills..
-- especially when paired with real troubleshooting and production-like scenarios.

Author: vsadhwani
Date: Oct 19, 2025
Engagement: 517 reactions, 38 comments, 77 reposts (Score: 3,119)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7378805958346514432

Here are 6 deployment strategies you must know when scaling traditional apps and ML workloads — and when to use each..

So deployments aren’t just about “pushing code.”

They decide → uptime, user experience, rollback safety, and scaling efficiency.

Here’s a quick breakdown:

1. Blue/Green
→ Spin up two environments, switch traffic instantly. 
→ Fast rollback, zero downtime.
(less common for ML — duplicating GPU infra is expensive).

2. Canary
→ Ship new versions to a small % of users. 
→ Rollout confidence without risking the whole system.
(widely used for ML inference endpoints — test with 5–10% traffic).

3. A/B Testing
→ Test multiple versions simultaneously. 
→ Perfect when data-driven decisions matter.
(ideal for ML models — compare accuracy, latency, and user outcomes live).

4. Feature Flags
→ Control features at runtime. 
→ Toggle on/off for specific users without redeploying.
(great for shadow testing or gradual rollout of AI-powered features).

5. Rolling
→ Gradually replace instances. 
→ Smooth balance of risk + stability.
(better for app services than ML workloads).

6. Live A/B Testing (ML focus)
→ Run different model versions in production and staging 
→ You can compare performance before committing

⸻

In the AI era, deployment ≠ delivery.

Scaling GPUs and experimenting with models need controlled rollouts, not blind pushes.

Which strategy do you use most often in your projects?

• Network Infrastructure and Topologies

• Container Networking (Docker/K8s)
• Monitoring, Logging and Tracing
• Network Security & Firewalls
• Performance and Optimization
• Software Defined Networking (SDN)

This is the practical networking you'll use everyday or at least need to know.

↳ Setting up multi-tier applications
↳ Troubleshooting connectivity issues
↳ Implementing zero-trust security
↳ Automating network configurations

Author: vsadhwani
Date: Jan 17, 2026

₹45 lakhs sitting on my parents’ property as collateral.

That was my education loan situation in 2018.

Like most international students, I didn’t question it.

I signed the papers in India, moved to the U.S., and assumed:
This is the deal. 
I’ll live with it for the next few years, paying heavy interest until I’m financially stable enough to pay it off.

I didn’t know there were other options.

It wasn’t until someone casually mentioned refinancing that I looked into it; skeptical at first.. and realized something important:

Your Indian education loan doesn’t have to stay an Indian loan.

I ended up refinancing with MPOWER Financing, and it genuinely changed my financial situation:

→ My family’s property was released from collateral ~ my parents were finally off the hook
→ I switched to a U.S. lender
→ I started paying in the same currency I was earning (USD)
→ I became eligible for U.S. tax benefits
→ Because of fixed interest rates, I was fully aware of the exact repayment amount. No hidden charges!

The entire process was online, and I had an advisor guiding me through each step.

Fast forward to last week.
I was talking to a friend working in the U.S., still stressed about collateral back home. They had no idea refinancing was even possible.. which is why I felt compelled to share this here.

So if you’re in a similar spot, here’s what you should know:
Refinancing isn’t just about getting a lower interest rate (though that helps).

It’s about freeing up your family’s assets, simplifying your finances, and unlocking benefits many of us aren’t even aware of.

And it’s far less complicated than it sounds.

A few takeaways from my experience:
MPOWER charges a one-time origination fee. 
For me, paying that fee made sense because of currency fluctuation.. but do your own math based on your situation.

It may not be right for everyone, but if you’re earning in USD and repaying an INR loan from India, it’s absolutely worth exploring.

You can check your eligibility here: https://lnkd.in/ghYdTAz6

Have you looked into refinancing, or are you still figuring out your options?

Would love to hear your experience.

Author: vsadhwani
Date: May 17, 2025

Cloud Devops interviews have 4-5 components to it.

So, first up, let's talk Infrastructure as code

Basically, it's a strategy behind building infrastructure that scales:

1. Containerization 
↳ Package applications with all dependencies
↳ Ensure consistent behavior across environments
↳ Docker simplifies build, ship, and run workflows
↳ Enables microservices architecture
↳ Perfect for DevOps practices

2. Container Orchestration 
↳ Kubernetes leads the way here
↳ Automates container deployment & scaling
↳ Handles load balancing
↳ Manages container health
↳ Enables zero-downtime deployments
↳ Supports multi-cloud deployments

3. Infrastructure as Code (IaC) 
↳ Terraform for multi-cloud provisioning
↳ CloudFormation for AWS-specific workloads
↳ Ansible for configuration management
↳ Version control your infrastructure
↳ Test before deployment
↳ Implement infrastructure CI/CD
↳ Maintain consistent environments

4. GitOps Workflow (not in this sheet)
↳ Git as single source of truth
↳ Automated infrastructure updates
↳ Pull-based deployments
↳ Built-in audit trail
↳ Easy rollbacks when needed
↳ Improved security & compliance

Why this matters?
→ Improved availability and scalability
→ Consistent deployments
→ Optimized costs
→ Minimal human error

The key thing is, you treat infrastructure like code, and I mean literally - 
Making it versionable, testable, and reusable!

Have you implemented IaC in your projects? 
What tools worked best for you?

--- Post 265 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Jun 21, 2025
Engagement: 501 reactions, 38 comments, 27 reposts (Score: 2,939)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7340482106495537152

DevOps ≠ DevOps Engineer
Let’s clear this up once and for all.

DevOps is a culture. A philosophy. A mindset.
DevOps Engineer is a role — one of many that help bring that philosophy to life.

They are not interchangeable terms, and treating them as such only dilutes what devops truly stands for.

This culture is enabled through a combination of practices and roles, including but not limited to:

1/ Core DevOps enablers
DevOps Engineer
↳ Automates CI/CD, config mgmt, builds pipelines

SRE (Site Reliability Engineer)
↳ Focuses on reliability, observability, and ops at scale

Platform Engineer
↳ Builds internal developer platforms and abstractions

Automation Engineer
↳ Enables automated testing, deployment, infrastructure

Cloud Engineer
↳ Implements cloud-native practices that support DevOps

Infrastructure Engineer 
↳ Builds scalable systems that support DevOps automation.

Service Manager 
↳ Owns SLAs, service lifecycles — operational maturity.

-----
2/ Production-focused roles:

Delivery / Production Engineer 
↳Automate & Orchestrate workflows from code to deployment

Sysadmin / Application Support
↳ Adapt monitoring, logging, and automation for modern stacks.

Cloud Ops / Resilience Engineer / Chaos Engineer – 
↳ Focus on reliability, failover, and incident readiness.

-----
3/ AI/ML Focussed Roles:

MLOps 
↳ Applies CI/CD, versioning, and monitoring to ML pipelines.

AIOps
↳Uses ML/AI to enhance ops (e.g., anomaly detection, predictive alerting).

DataOps 
↳Brings DevOps discipline to data pipelines: versioning, CI/CD, governance.

And many more..

This culture shows up differently depending on your domain — software, infrastructure, cloud, and even AI/ML.

Reasons to pick them:
- These roles fuel cloud-native adoption
- They connect product thinking with production readiness
- They own the impact from code to customer

Today, titles may differ—but the mission is shared: ship faster, safer, smarter.

Hope it helps give you an idea of the DevOps landscape!

• • •

If you found this useful..
🔔 Follow me (Vishakha) for more Cloud & DevOps insights
♻️ Share so others can learn as well

↳Brings DevOps discipline to data pipelines: versioning, CI/CD, governance.

And many more..

This culture shows up differently depending on your domain — software, infrastructure, cloud, and even AI/ML.

Reasons to pick them:
- These roles fuel cloud-native adoption
- They connect product thinking with production readiness
- They own the impact from code to customer

Today, titles may differ—but the mission is shared: ship faster, safer, smarter.

Hope it helps give you an idea of the DevOps landscape!

There’s a hidden hierarchy in Cloud Engineering that nobody talks about..
Here are 4 levels of cloud expertise that directly impact your compensation.

1. Cloud Enthusiasts
→ Tinker with free tiers, spin up EC2s/VMs, deploy static websites. 
→ Know the basics but limited production exposure.
Compensation: Entry-level salaries or internships ($60,000 – $90,000).

2. Cloud Engineers
→ Confident with Infra as Code, CI/CD, Kubernetes basics, monitoring, and security. 
→ Can run production workloads, but mostly follow existing patterns.
Compensation: $95,000 – $140,000.

3. Senior/Platform Engineers
→ Architect scalable systems across multiple services (load balancing, networking, observability, automation). 
→ Bridge DevOps + Security. Own reliability at scale.
Compensation: $140,000 – $200,000+.

4. Cloud Architects / Principal Engineers
→ Design multi-region, hybrid, or AI-native architectures. 
→ Optimize cost, governance, and compliance. Mentor teams and set org-wide cloud standards.
Compensation: $200,000 – $350,000+ (with bonuses/equity).
⸻

Cloud is vast..
But if you understand this hierarchy, you’ll know where to double down your chances.
So take a strategic approach and focus one level & position yourself as an expert.

If I were to transition from Software Engineering to DevOps in 2025, here is what I'd learn first:

learn Infrastructure as Code (IaC) 
– master Terraform and/or CloudFormation 
– understand idempotency and state management 
– master Python or Bash for automation tasks
– learn infrastructure testing methodologies 
– practice modularizing and organizing IaC code

learn Containerization and Orchestration 
– master Docker for building and managing images 
– understand container networking and storage 
– master Kubernetes core concepts (Pods, Deployments, Services, Namespaces..) 
– learn Helm for packaging and deploying applications on Kubernetes

learn CI/CD Pipelines 
– master a CI/CD tool (e.g., Jenkins, GitLab CI, GitHub Actions..) 
– implement automated testing (unit, integration, end-to-end) 
– learn artifact management and versioning 
– implement various deployment strategies (Canary, Blue/Green)

learn Cloud Platforms (Operations Focus) 
– master core services on (your favorite) cloud relevant to operations (Compute, Networking, Storage, IAM) 
– understand managed Kubernetes services (pick one cloud provider) 
– learn cloud monitoring and logging solutions 
– implement infrastructure cost management

learn Monitoring, Logging, and Alerting 
– master a monitoring tool (e.g., Prometheus, Grafana, Datadog, cloud-based) 
– implement centralized logging (e.g., ELK stack) 
– learn to write effective alerts and set up notifications 
– understand on-call and incident management processes

learn Security in DevOps (DevSecOps) 
– implement security scanning in the CI/CD pipeline
– understand cloud security best practices and compliance 
– learn secrets management tools (e.g., HashiCorp Vault) 
– implement infrastructure security scanning

learn Networking Fundamentals (Cloud & Containers) 
– understand TCP/IP and DNS 
– learn about load balancing and firewalls in the cloud 
– understand container networking concepts (CNI) 
– grasp the basics of service meshes

learn MLOps Fundamentals (for future integration) 
– understand the ML lifecycle and deployment challenges 
– learn about model versioning and registries 
– familiarize yourself with tools for model monitoring 
– understand basic concepts of deploying ML models

Aim to be an engineer who:

1. Can bridge development & operations
2. Are poised to integrate their expertise with AI/ML capabilities

It's time to level up!

Scenario-based questions in cloud interviews can be tricky, particularly the migration ones. 
(this checklist can help)

You may encounter these questions as scenarios or when discussing your project --

Either way, frame your experience around key domains:

1. Application Discovery & Compute Requirements
→ Start with the right questions about current workloads
→ Migration patterns (when to lift-and-shift vs. re-architect)
→ Understand performance baselines and scaling needs

2. DevOps, CI/CD & Infrastructure as Code
→ Current workflows, tools and testing strategies
→ Downtime minimization techniques 
→ Choosing the right deployment service (Serverless vs Containerized apps)

3. Networking & Connectivity
→ Data Migration strategies
→ Key Networking considerations
→ Hybrid and multi-cloud scenarions

These three cover 80% of initial scenarios. 
And yes, there's more (data, security, etc.), but start with these first!

Common gotchas to watch for:
• Handling legacy applications
• Database migration challenges
• Dealing with stateful applications
• Cost optimization strategies

Think in scenarios while preparing.
As, these interviews push you to reason through problems.

Did your interview include any of these questions? 

Share your experience in the comments!

--- Post 274 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Sep 19, 2025
Engagement: 460 reactions, 44 comments, 62 reposts (Score: 2,864)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7371204306382909440

If you’re in cloud and not looking at optimization end-to-end, you’re missing out — here are the key strategies you should know..

→ Compute
↳ Right-size instances, use auto-scaling/serverless, and leverage spot/preemptible VMs
↳ Consolidate workloads with Kubernetes/Fargate/Cloud Run

→ Storage
↳ Use lifecycle policies to move infrequently used data to cheaper tiers
↳ Deduplication, compression, and smart replication strategies reduce costs

→ Networking
↳ CDN for static content, private networking to cut egress, and traffic shaping with load balancers
↳ Always optimize data transfer (avoid unnecessary cross-region costs)

→ Databases
↳ Use managed services, read replicas, and caching
↳ Shard/partition for scale, and pick the right DB for the workload

→ Big Data
↳ Spot clusters for jobs, serverless analytics, and data partitioning
↳ Stream only what’s critical, batch the rest

→ Security
↳ Enforce least privilege IAM, encrypt in transit/at rest
↳ Automate threat detection and centralize secrets with KMS/Vault

→ AI/ML
↳ Track experiments, use AutoML/pre-trained APIs
↳ Share GPUs, and clean/optimize data before training

Essential Note:
Cloud optimization isn’t a one-time exercise.
You have to keep at it — especially now, with AI workloads driving cloud costs to new highs.

Start with one area → measure impact → repeat. 

What other strategies would you add?

Here's how I created these 12 system design visuals for cloud engineers in a fraction of the time:

("Load balancer distributing traffic to three backend servers, clean technical diagram style")

3. Iterate in seconds → Adjust the prompt until the visual matches what's in my head

4. Export and share → Drop it straight into my posts, newsletter or carousel slides

--- Post 277 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Sep 19, 2025
Engagement: 446 reactions, 48 comments, 71 reposts (Score: 2,852)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7373372778533707776

If you’re aiming to grow in Cloud Security, here’s how to approach it step by step..

In any field, the right order of learning matters. Cloud Security is no different.
Check this out:

Step 1: Identity Security
→ Start with IAM, RBAC, MFA, and SSO.
Identity is the first line of defense in the cloud. If access isn’t right, nothing else matters.

Step 2: Infrastructure Security
→ Next, protect your network, APIs, and endpoints.
Think WAFs, firewalls, DDoS protection, secrets management — the foundation of secure workloads.

Step 3: Data Security
→ Once infra is safe, secure the data itself.
Encryption (at rest/in transit), tokenization, masking, and DLP — these prevent leaks from turning into disasters.

Step 4: IaC & Code Security
→ Shift security left.
Scan Terraform/CloudFormation for misconfigs, catch secrets in code, manage dependencies. Stop issues before they hit production.

Step 5: Container & Kubernetes Security
→ Modern apps = containers.
Secure them with runtime threat detection, image scanning, RBAC. Add service mesh (mTLS, Istio, Linkerd) for secure pod-to-pod comms.

Step 6: AI Model Security
→ As AI adoption grows, so do risks.
Guardrails, bias detection, API threat protection, and secure model deployment are the new essentials.

Step 7: Business Security
→ Finally, scale security across the org.
CSPM, SIEM, threat monitoring, compliance, and audits ensure governance at scale.

⸻

Security is already vast — adding cloud makes it even more complex.
That’s why structure matters.

This Thursday, I’ll be sharing a detailed newsletter with resources to start → tech5ense.com

Author: vsadhwani
Date: Jun 21, 2025
Engagement: 431 reactions, 60 comments, 29 reposts (Score: 2,813)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7329961027528232961

If I were to transition from Software Engineering to DevOps Engineering all over again in 2025, here is what I'd learn first: 

learn infrastructure as code (IaC)
- get hands-on with YAML/JSON and Terraform for cloud resource provisioning
- understand modular design, state management, and idempotency
- version and automate infra changes like code
- explore Pulumi for Python-based IaC
- learn CloudFormation for AWS-specific provisioning

learn containerization and orchestration
- master Docker (images, volumes, networks, multi-stage builds)
- understand Kubernetes architecture deeply (deployments, services, Helm)
- implement container security and health checks
- practice with cloud environments (EKS/AKS/GKE)
- understand container networking and storage patterns (VERY IMPORTANT)

study CI/CD pipelines
- learn about different stages of pipelines (test, build, and deploy)
- understand how to build automated pipelines (along with managed cloud services)
- explore tools and processes (GitHub Actions, Jenkins, etc)
- learn deployment strategies like rolling, blue-green, and canary
- learn pipeline security and compliance

learn cloud platforms and services
- get hands-on with one cloud provider (AWS/GCP/Azure)
- focus on core services (compute, storage, IAM, VPC etc)
- understand networking (subnets, firewalls, DNS, load balancers)
- learn about cost monitoring, logging and resource tagging
- manage resources programmatically via SDKs and CLI tools
- explore serverless, container orchestration, and managed services

<Advanced Level>
study observability and reliability
- how to set up centralized logging across services and environments
- understand distributed tracing and debugging
- study concepts like SLOs, SLAs, and SLIs
- explore alerting systems (PagerDuty, Opsgenie, SNS)

learn security best practices
- understand zero trust architecture
- Learn container scanning - why we do it & how (Trivy, Snyk)
- What are automated compliance checks 

If you need a detailed study guide — subscribe here: https://lnkd.in/dBNJPv9U

I’ll leave you with some final tips:
→ You don’t need to master everything at once — start small, stay consistent.
→ Be more than a tool user — become a systems thinker who understands why things work.
→ Join DevOps communities — ask questions, share learnings, and grow in public.
→ Pick 1–2 real projects — applying what you learn beats passive study every time.

Keep building, keep shipping — and keep the momentum going.
