Here’s a quick cheat sheet on GCP DevOps Services — plus 6 resources (labs, trainings, projects) to help you practice

Let’s dive into the buckets first:

→ Source Code & Artifacts
↳ Git repos and artifact registries to manage code and container images.
Examples: Cloud Source Repositories, Artifact Registry

→ Provisioning & Infrastructure Management
↳ Infra as Code, config management, GitOps.
Examples: Terraform on Google Cloud, Config Connector, Config Sync

→ CI/CD (Build & Deploy)
↳ Automate builds and continuous delivery.
Examples: Cloud Build, Cloud Deploy, Spinnaker on GCP

→ Compute & Hosting
↳ Where apps actually run — VMs, Kubernetes, serverless.
Examples: Compute Engine, GKE, Cloud Run Functions(correction from the image), Cloud Run

→ Security & Identity
↳ Manage secrets, IAM, workload identity, and container policy enforcement.
Examples: Secret Manager, Cloud IAM, Binary Authorization

→ Automation & Orchestration
↳ Event-driven pipelines and serverless orchestration for DevOps workflows.
Examples: Cloud Scheduler, Cloud Tasks, Workflows, Pub/Sub

→ Monitoring & Logging
↳ Full visibility into apps, infra, and performance.
Examples: Cloud Monitoring, Cloud Logging, Cloud Trace, Cloud Profiler

⸻
Now that you know the buckets → practice labs & projects using the resources below: 

1. cloud.google.com/training → Free courses to get you started
2. cloudskillsboost.google → Hands-on labs (with free options)
3. Google Cloud Platform Samples → https://lnkd.in/gEChjt68
4. Code Labs → https://lnkd.in/g_gRFhN7
5. Cloud Architecture Center → https://lnkd.in/gYYR-3kN
6. Cloud Computing Fundamentals (Classroom-based Training) → https://lnkd.in/d-VEb8gn

-- focus on consistent, incremental progress.

--- Post 282 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Jul 21, 2025
Engagement: 451 reactions, 47 comments, 30 reposts (Score: 2,785)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7346197294909657088

If you want to build cloud-native systems 
here are the core pillars you must look into:

1. Stateless Design 
↳ Avoid local state storage; store data in distributed databases or caches; design for crash tolerance and fast startup.

2. Containerization 
↳ Use Docker or OCI-compliant containers; keep containers lightweight; isolate dependencies and runtime.

3. CI/CD Pipelines 
↳ Automate testing & builds; use GitOps for versioned infra; utilize cloud-native services for scalable & resilient pipeline execution; implement rollback & approval gates.

4. Infrastructure as Code (IaC) 
↳ Use Terraform, Pulumi, CloudFormation; keep infra modular and reusable; store IaC in version control.

5. Service Mesh 
↳ Use Istio, Linkerd, or Consul; enable traffic routing, retries, & TLS; monitor and trace traffic flows.

6. Auto-scaling 
↳ Use HPA (Horizontal Pod Autoscaler), Keda/Deeper, CPU/memory thresholds; monitor scaling triggers continuously.

7. Observability 
↳ Collect logs, metrics, and traces; use tools like Prometheus, Grafana, OpenTelemetry; implement alerting for critical failures.

8. Secrets Management 
↳ Store secrets securely (e.g., HashiCorp Vault, Cloud-based Secrets Manager); never hardcode secrets; enable automatic secret rotation.

9. Multi-cloud Readiness 
↳ Avoid vendor lock-in and improve resilience; abstract cloud services where possible; use open standards and cloud-agnostic tools; ensure data portability and cross-cloud sync.

10. Fault Tolerance 
↳ Maintain uptime during failures; use retries, timeouts, and circuit breakers; distribute workloads across zones/regions; design for graceful degradation.

I’d heard of cloud-native systems for years - but I only started truly understanding the pillars once I worked with them hands-on - so learn & apply!

Remember, each pillar comes with its own trade-offs and complexities.

What else do you think should make this list?

One tool you'll see in almost every Cloud DevOps interview these days - Terraform.
Let's dive into how it really works at scale..

Whether you're deploying a single compute instance(any cloud provider) or scaling infrastructure across multiple regions - Terraform is now a core skill in the cloud and DevOps ecosystem.

Let's dive in:
1. Write
↳ Developers write declarative code using HCL (also supports JSON-compatible syntax)
↳ Use variables, modules, and outputs to keep it organized and reusable
↳ Leverage public modules from the Terraform Registry to avoid reinventing the wheel

2. Plan
↳ Terraform reads the configuration and validates it
↳ Integrates with CI/CD tools like GitHub Actions, Jenkins, and GitLab CI
↳ Generates an execution plan that shows exactly what will be created, changed, or destroyed
This step provides visibility—no changes are made yet.

3. Apply
↳ Terraform compares the current infrastructure with the desired state
↳ Executes only the required changes to align both states
↳ Ensures updates are safe, efficient, and idempotent

4. Infrastructure Ready
↳ Uses APIs to provision infrastructure via 1000+ supported providers: AWS, GCP, Azure, Kubernetes, and more
↳ Builds infrastructure that is consistent across environments
↳ Scales from individual projects to enterprise-grade production systems

Why teams rely on Terraform:
- Infrastructure is version-controlled and trackable
- Scales easily across teams and environments
- Reusable modules reduce redundancy and error
- Works seamlessly with DevOps pipelines

If you’re just getting started, keep going. These fundamentals compound fast.

Author: vsadhwani

If I were advancing my DevOps skills in this AI-driven era, understanding the MLOps process would be my starting point 
(also knowing the DevOps role in each stage)

Let's break down what you need to know:

1. Data Strategy: Define goals and data needs for the ML project.
↳ DevOps Role: Provides infrastructure and tools for collaboration and documentation.

2. Data Collection: Acquire data from diverse sources, ensuring compliance.
↳ DevOps Role: Sets up and manages data pipelines, storage, and access controls.

3. Data Validation: Check quality and integrity of collected data.
↳ DevOps Role: Automates validation processes and integrates them into data pipelines.

4. Data Preprocessing: Clean, normalize, and transform data for training.
↳ DevOps Role: Provides scalable compute resources and infrastructure for preprocessing.

5. Feature Engineering: Create meaningful inputs from raw data.
↳ DevOps Role: Supports feature stores and automates feature pipeline deployment.

6. Version Control: Manage changes in data, code, and model setups.
↳DevOps Role: Implements and manages version control systems (Git) for code, data, and models.

7. Model Training: Develop models with curated data sets.
↳DevOps Role: Manages compute resources (CPU/GPU), automates training pipelines, and handles experiments (MLflow, etc.).

8. Model Evaluation: Analyze perf metrics.
↳DevOps Role: Integrates evaluation metrics into CI/CD pipelines and builds monitoring dashboards.

9. Model Registry: Log and store trained models with versions.
↳DevOps Role: Sets up and manages the model registry as a central artifact store.

10. Model Packaging: Bundle models and dependencies for deployment.
↳DevOps Role: Automates the containerization of models and their dependencies.

11. Deployment Strategy: Outline roll-out processes and fallback plans.
↳DevOps Role: Leads the design and implementation of deployment strategies (Canary, Blue/Green, etc.).

12. Infrastructure Setup: Arrange compute resources and scaling guidelines.
↳DevOps Role: Provisions and manages the underlying infrastructure (cloud resources, Kubernetes, etc.).

13. Model Deployment: Move models into the production environment.
↳DevOps Role: Automates the deployment process using CI/CD pipelines.

14. Model Serving: Activate model endpoints for application use.
↳ DevOps Role: Manages the serving infrastructure, scaling, and API endpoints.

15. Resource Optimization: Ensure compute efficiency and cost-effectiveness.
↳ DevOps Role: Implements auto-scaling, cost management strategies, and infrastructure optimization.

16. Model Updates: Organize re-training and version advancements.
↳DevOps Role: Automates the retraining and redeployment processes through CI/CD pipelines.

It's a steep learning curve, but actively working on MLOps projects and understanding these stages is absolutely vital today..

If you’re in Cloud, DevOps, or SRE — this one AIOps project should definitely be in your resume..
(YouTube + GitHub links included)

Modern systems generate millions of logs daily. 
Tools like ELK or Loki help, but they’re often reactive.

This project shows how AI makes log analysis proactive ~

→ Anomaly Detection : spot unusual spikes & patterns in logs
→ Root Cause Analysis : trace issues back to their source
→ Predictive Maintenance : forecast failures before they happen
→ Alert Prioritization : cut through noise with severity-based alerts
→ Automated Remediation (Future scope) : trigger fixes when anomalies hit

TL;DR :
Parse millions of logs → detect anomalies → trace root causes → predict failures → prioritize alerts → (future) auto-remediate.

Why it matters?

Logs are the heartbeat of cloud systems. AIOps helps detect unknown issues early, handle massive log volumes (yes, huge GBs of data), and uncover hidden patterns that traditional tools miss.

Tech stack: Python, pandas, scikit-learn
ML Model: Isolation Forest — unsupervised, scalable, works without labeled data

YouTube Walkthrough: https://lnkd.in/dkwaYyEh
GitHub Repo: https://lnkd.in/dwatbcX3

Big thanks to Abhishek + Aditya for breaking it down step by step.

Here are 12 essential security practices you need to know for cloud roles
(crucial concepts for interviews)

1. Shared Responsibility Model:
Know what your cloud provider secures vs. what you must secure.
→ provider vs. customer responsibilities.

2. Multi-Factor Authentication (MFA):
Add an extra layer beyond passwords for access.
→ time-based tokens, authenticator apps, biometrics.

3. Identity & Access Management (IAM):
Control who can access what and enforce strict permissions.
→ roles, policies, least privilege.

4. Secure Cloud Storage Permissions:
Avoid public buckets and overly broad access.
→ ACLs, IAM policies, bucket-level security.

5. Encrypt Data at Rest and in Transit:
Use encryption to protect stored and moving data.
→ TLS, AES-256, envelope encryption.

6. Network Segmentation:
Limit breach impact by isolating workloads.
→ VPCs, subnets, firewalls.

7. Update and Patch Systems:
Fix known vulnerabilities in all components.
→ OS, applications, containers.

8. Enable DDoS Protection:
Prevent service disruption from traffic floods.
→ AWS Shield, Cloud Armor, rate limiting.

9. Backup Data Regularly:
Protect against data loss with frequent, tested backups.
→ snapshot automation, recovery drills.

10. Monitor and Log Activities:
Track events across your cloud infrastructure.
→ audit logs, CloudTrail, SIEM tools.

11. Set Resource Usage Alerts:
Catch anomalies early through alerts.
→ billing thresholds, abnormal activity triggers.

12. Use Cloud Security Posture Management (CSPM):
Continuously detect and fix cloud misconfigurations.
→ real-time scanning, policy enforcement.

As cloud environments get more complex, organizations really need people who get cloud security — because it’s not just about tech, it’s about protecting what matters most.

If you want to stand out, focus on learning these core security concepts and how to apply them in real cloud environments — that’s what companies really value.

How many of these cloud security practices do you actually follow?

Author: vsadhwani

URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7350913295421181952

Platform Engineer != Cloud Engineer != DevOps Engineer

Time to clear this up — once and for all.

Job posts might blur them together, but if you want clarity, here it is.

Cloud Engineers:
 → Build, manage, and optimize cloud infrastructure
 → Handle networking, security, resource provisioning within a cloud/multi-cloud/hybrid setup
 → Focus on platform scalability and cost-efficiency

DevOps Engineers:
 → Streamline development pipelines via CI/CD
 → Automate deployments and manage IaC
 → Own monitoring, logging, and feedback loops
 → Bridge the dev–ops collaboration gap

Platform Engineers:
This is where things get interesting. 

Platform Engineers build Internal Developer Platforms (IDPs) — custom-built platforms (or you can call it internal tools) designed to abstract infrastructure and streamline developer workflows.

Their role?
 → Provide golden paths for developers (secure, standardized workflows)
 → Build self-service capabilities (deployments, environments, observability)
 → Standardize tools and workflows across teams
 → Improve developer experience (DX) and velocity

Platform Engineering sits on top of Cloud and DevOps practices — but it’s not just an intersection. It’s an enablement layer.

The key distinction:
Cloud = Infrastructure layer
DevOps = Process layer
Platform Engineering = Enablement layer for developers

As companies scale, platform engineering becomes critical for reducing cognitive load on dev teams and ensuring consistency across the SDLC.

What’s your take?

Author: vsadhwani

Check out this practical cheatsheet to ace the Cloud Networking portion of your interviews — with scenario-based questions and use-cases

1. Network Isolation (VPC & Subnets)
→ Default vs Custom VPCs
→ Multi-tier architecture design
→ Subnet CIDR planning
Real scenario: "How would you isolate prod/dev environments?"

2. Access Controls
→ Security Groups vs NACLs
→ Cloud-native firewalls
Real scenario: "Design security for a public-facing API"

3. DNS & Name Resolution
→ Private vs Public zones
→ Custom routing policies
Real scenario: "Design global DNS failover"

4. Load Balancing
→ Application vs Network level
→ Global load balancing patterns
Real scenario: "Handle traffic spikes across regions"

5. NAT & Internet Access
→ NAT Gateway vs Instances
→ Internet Gateway design
Real scenario: "Secure outbound traffic flows"

6. Hybrid Connectivity
→ VPN vs Direct Connect
→ Transit Gateway architecture
Real scenario: "Connect legacy datacenter to cloud"

7. Interconnect Architecture
→ VPC Peering strategies
→ Hub-spoke models
Real scenario: "Design multi-region connectivity"

8. Private Service Access
→ PrivateLink/Endpoints
→ Service discovery patterns
Real scenario: "Secure access to managed databases"

9. IP Management
→ IPv4/IPv6 strategy
→ BYOIP implementation
Real scenario: "Plan IP addressing at scale"

10. Traffic Control
→ Route tables
→ BGP routing patterns
Real scenario: "Optimize application routing"

11. Network Monitoring
→ Flow logs
→ Packet analysis
Real scenario: "Debug cross-region connectivity"

12. High Availability
→ Multi-AZ design
→ Failover strategies
Real scenario: "Design for 99.99% availability"

Instead of diving into each cloud networking service individually — focus on scenarios.
Ask yourself: "What is used when?"

That’s the mindset interviewers look for — not just theory, but real-world decision making.

My 2 cents study cloud networking:
→ Understand the core concepts
→ Be ready to explain trade-offs
→ Think through cost and complexity
→ Consider operational impact over time

Breaking into an SRE role is all about how you respond during critical incidents
Here's a scenario-based checklist (and an AI-based project) to practice

When it comes to SRE interviews, theory isn’t enough.
You’re expected to handle real-world failures:
- What happens when a Kubernetes pod keeps crashing?
- How do you debug a latency spike across microservices? (the toughest one)
- What steps would you take if CI/CD pipelines start failing mid-deployment?
and a bunch of the complex scenarios..

These aren’t “Stack Overflow” type of questions.
They’re scenario-driven — designed to test how you think, act, and prioritize under pressure.

That’s why having a scenario-based checklist is so powerful.
It helps you structure your answers, show clarity of thought, and stand out in interviews.

Big thanks to Jatin Bansal for curating these SRE + Kubernetes scenario-based questions into a neat PDF 🙌

Also, check out this AI-based SRE project ~ Agentic-Ops-Dev (Azure based).
→ It simulates incident management with AI-driven agents, giving you hands-on practice for modern SRE workflows.
→ Github Repo: https://lnkd.in/dY49C7Um

Hope this helps!

--- Post 303 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Feb 16, 2026
Engagement: 398 reactions, 26 comments, 50 reposts (Score: 2,350)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7427022313583267840

If you’re learning DevOps or ML systems, these deployment strategies are must-knows.

So deployments aren’t just about “pushing code.”

They decide → uptime, user experience, rollback safety, and scaling efficiency.

Here’s a quick breakdown:

1. Blue/Green
→ Spin up two environments, switch traffic instantly. 
→ Fast rollback, zero downtime.
(less common for ML — duplicating GPU infra is expensive).

2. Canary
→ Ship new versions to a small % of users. 
→ Rollout confidence without risking the whole system.
(widely used for ML inference endpoints — test with 5–10% traffic).

3. A/B Testing
→ Test multiple versions simultaneously. 
→ Perfect when data-driven decisions matter.
(ideal for ML models — compare accuracy, latency, and user outcomes live).

4. Feature Flags
→ Control features at runtime. 
→ Toggle on/off for specific users without redeploying.
(great for shadow testing or gradual rollout of AI-powered features).

5. Rolling
→ Gradually replace instances. 
→ Smooth balance of risk + stability.
(better for app services than ML workloads).

6. Live A/B Testing (ML focus)
→ Run different model versions in production and staging 
→ You can compare performance before committing

⸻

In the AI era, deployment ≠ delivery.

Scaling GPUs and experimenting with models need controlled rollouts, not blind pushes.

Which strategy do you use most often in your projects?

Author: vsadhwani

𝗜𝗳 𝘆𝗼𝘂 𝗮𝗿𝗲 𝗹𝗼𝗼𝗸𝗶𝗻𝗴 𝘁𝗼 𝗲𝗻𝘁𝗲𝗿 𝗶𝗻𝘁𝗼 𝗖𝗹𝗼𝘂𝗱 𝗗𝗲𝘃𝗼𝗽𝘀 → 𝗕𝗹𝘂𝗲𝗽𝗿𝗶𝗻𝘁 𝗳𝗶𝗿𝘀𝘁

Easy to feel overwhelmed when you try to learn all the cloud specific devOps tools and technologies at once. 

Focus on mastering one skill before moving on to the next. 

Here's a step by step breakdown:

1. Linux Foundations
- Command-line proficiency
- System administration basics

2. Networking Core Concepts
- TCP/IP, DNS, routing, subnetting
- Virtual Private Clouds (VPCs)

3. Cloud Services
- Focus on one cloud provider first (AWS, GCP, Azure)
- Compute, storage, networking, security services

4. Security
- Core concepts such as access management, encryption, vulnerability scanning
- Best practices for securing cloud-native applications

5. Containers and Orchestration (Docker, Kubernetes)
- How to package and run applications consistently across environments
- Core concepts and containerization best practices

6. Infrastructure as Code (Ansible, Terraform, Cloudformation)
- How to automate infrastructure provisioning and management

7. Observability & Monitoring
- Core components such as Logging, Monitoring, Tracing 
- Leverage managed services to gather insights into application performance and health 

8. CI/CD ~ Continuous Integration & Continuous Delivery
- What are pipelines and how to set them up.

Want to practice brushing up these skills? Try this...
----
𝗕𝗲 𝗵𝗮𝗻𝗱𝘀-𝗼𝗻
- Use free cloud credits that is available for majority of the cloud provider

𝗖𝗲𝗿𝘁𝗶𝗳𝗶𝗰𝗮𝘁𝗶𝗼𝗻𝘀
- Enroll in courses to learn in a structured way.
- Certifications like Google Cloud Digital Leader, AWS Certified Cloud Practitioner, Hashicorp Terraform Associate, and CKAD (Certified Kubernetes Application Developer) can provide a strong foundation.

𝗡𝗲𝘁𝘄𝗼𝗿𝗸 & 𝗟𝗲𝗮𝗿𝗻
- Connect with fellow Cloud Engineers to exchange ideas/insights and learn about best practices from the field professionals
----

--- Post 307 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Nov 18, 2025
Engagement: 398 reactions, 25 comments, 36 reposts (Score: 2,312)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7389319177464889344

Being cloud-native is not only about containers or Kubernetes ~ here’s what most engineers miss.

Cloud-native isn’t a stack ~ it’s a continuous flow of layers working in sync.

When we talk about being cloud-native, most people immediately think of Docker or Kubernetes ~
but those are just the delivery vehicles.

It’s a layered journey from infrastructure, app development, automation, and security, all the way to serverless and robust observability!

Here’s what that Cloud-Native Application flow looks like:

- It starts at the Infrastructure Layer, where compute, storage, and networking form the backbone.

- Then comes Provisioning & Security ~ automating deployments through IaC and enforcing access control.

- Next, the Application Development Layer ~ where your CI/CD pipelines, APIs, and databases live.

- Above that sits Orchestration & Management ~ Kubernetes, Service Mesh, and API Gateways that keep services running and connected.

- Then comes Observability ~ metrics, logs, and traces that turn complexity into visibility.

- And finally, Serverless & Extensibility ~ event-driven and managed layers where infra fades into the background.

Each arrow in the flow represents a handoff --
from infrastructure teams to platform engineers,
from developers to operators,
from systems that run → to systems that learn and adapt.

Together, they show that cloud-native isn’t about tools ~ it’s a way of building systems that constantly flow, adapt, and improve.

Author: vsadhwani
Date: May 17, 2025

Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7294539325512282112

Me, trying to figure out why my sidecar container is eating all the resources….. 

The truth about sidecar containers nobody tells you:

↳ They quietly handle the essential tasks you don't want to worry about.

What they ACTUALLY do:
• Handle cross-cutting concerns (logging, monitoring)
• Proxy your network traffic
• Sync your data
• Manage your certificates

The Good:
- Separation of concerns
- Independent scaling
- Easy to maintain
- Reusable across apps

The "Fun" Part:
⚠ Resource overhead
⚠ Complex networking
⚠ Startup order dependencies
⚠ Debugging can get tricky

Why you need to know this:
Modern cloud-native apps (especially in Kubernetes) heavily rely on sidecars. Think service mesh (Istio), logging (Fluentd), or SSL termination.

Pro tip: Always set resource limits for your sidecars. Trust me, your cluster will thank you later!

♻️ Repost to help someone
🔔 Follow for more cloud & DevOps tips

Author: vsadhwani

If you're searching for a good DevOps read, you should see this.

before diving in, ask yourself:

1. Are you interested in the principles or the practical aspects?

• foundations and principles → "The Goal" or "Lean Software Development"
• application → "The DevOps Handbook" or "Continuous Delivery"

2. Which is your primary focus: culture or implementation?

• cultural → "Team Topologies" or "The Five Dysfunctions of a Team"
• for implementation → "Site Reliability Engineering" or "Accelerate"

3. How do you learn best?

• prefer story-based learning → "The Phoenix Project" or "The Unicorn Project"
• prefer structured approaches → "Domain-Driven Design: Distilled" or "Database Reliability Engineering"

Don't try to read them all. 
Pick ONE that aligns with your current needs and actually finish it.

Also, this is not an exhaustive list of titles - there are more great reads in the market!
(happy to share a few more that I've read)

Here's what you can do ~ my 2 cents:

Start with "The Phoenix Project" for the WHY
Move to "The DevOps Handbook" for the HOW
Then begin "Site Reliability Engineering" for the next-level

Remember: Reading should complement your DevOps practice, not substitute it.

What books have been most impactful in your DevOps journey? 
I'd love to hear your recommendations.

--- Post 312 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Aug 20, 2025
Engagement: 378 reactions, 36 comments, 7 reposts (Score: 2,264)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7354531333710241792

Me, trying to figure out why my sidecar container is eating all the resources...

Welcome to the side of Kubernetes no one warned you about:)

The truth about sidecar containers :
↳ They quietly handle the essential tasks you don't want to worry about.

What they ACTUALLY do:
• Handle cross-cutting concerns (logging, monitoring)
• Proxy your network traffic
• Sync your data
• Manage your certificates

The Good:
- Separation of concerns
- Independent scaling
- Reusable across apps

The "Fun" Part:
⚠ Resource overhead
⚠ Complex networking
⚠ Startup order dependencies
⚠ Debugging can get tricky

But now there's a shift happening.

Sidecarless architectures are gaining traction.

Tools like Istio Ambient Mesh and Cilium are exploring ways to:
 ↳ Offload proxy logic to the node level
 ↳ Reduce per-pod overhead
 ↳ Simplify networking and improve performance

It’s not about sidecars being “bad” — it’s about knowing when they make sense.

My 2 cents:
Always set resource limits for your sidecars.
And if you're managing dozens of them per node… start exploring sidecarless options. Your cluster might breathe easier.

DevOps engineers aren’t afraid of AI taking their jobs.

What we are concerned about:
- Having to maintain AI systems with little to no documentation.
- AI tools getting pushed to production without enough testing or security reviews.
- Keeping cloud costs under control when GPU usage spikes.
- Pressure to deliver “AI-powered” features faster than what’s realistic.
- Managing complex infrastructure with no clear rollback plans.

And here's the thing:
- We build the pipelines that get AI models into production.
- We make sure those models scale.
- We automate the infrastructure that AI systems runs on.
- We’re behind MLOps, LLMOps, AiOps — and are a major part of it.
- We keep AI systems monitored, secure, and stable.
- We set up rollbacks for when things will go wrong (because they do).
- We help turn notebooks into products — safely, reliably, and at scale.
- We balance speed, cost, and performance — even when the pressure’s on

We’re not just supporting the AI era. We’re powering it.

And it feels great to be right in the middle of it.

If the backup lives on the same machine that just died,
that’s not a backup 🙂 

That’s just wishful thinking with extra storage.

Real backup strategy means:

• Different machine
• Different storage
• Ideally different region
• And tested restores

Because during an outage,
you don’t discover your backup strategy.

You discover the failure points.

If you’re transitioning into AI and wondering what skills matter for each path, this breakdown can help.

Here’s the real value of this matrix:
It helps you understand where to focus your time, instead of trying to learn everything at once.

A few things stand out immediately:

1. The foundational layer matters for almost every AI role
↳ Python, ML theory, SQL, data wrangling — the basics still drive the entire ecosystem.

Even roles like AI PM or Ethics end up needing enough technical grounding to make decisions that impact real systems.

2. Data pipelines are the hidden backbone
↳ Whether you’re a Data Engineer, MLOps Engineer, Cloud Architect, or LLM Engineer.. you’ll notice data orchestration, feature engineering, and pipeline tooling show up as critical everywhere.

Real AI systems are built on clean, reliable data paths.

3. The “LLMs, RAG, Agents” row is where the ecosystem is evolving fastest
↳ Even though the matrix groups them together, these are different layers in practice:
→ Prompting fundamentals
→ Retrieval-Augmented Generation
→ Multi-agent orchestration and tool-calling

Most high-impact GenAI teams now use all three.

4. Infra roles continue to play a massive part in AI
Deployment, containers, cloud platforms, CI/CD; they light up the matrix for:
→ Cloud Architects
→ MLOps Engineers
→ AI Engineers

5. Business & communication skills become critical as you move toward PM, leadership, and governance

Product direction, compliance, lifecycle risk, evaluation.. these are the drivers behind responsible AI adoption at scale.

If you’re trying to map your own path into AI, start by identifying which column looks like you.. and then follow the skills marked “critical” first.

Which role are you aiming for right now?

AI in DevOps ≠ AIOps.
AI is reshaping the DevOps toolchain ~ and it's showing up in far more places than just AIOps.

→ AIOps is one slice of a much larger picture.
It covers monitoring, alerting, and incident response. One specific layer of the stack.

→ AI in DevOps spans the entire engineering lifecyclee

Here are 4 ways it’s actually showing up in practice..
• • •

1. Infrastructure provisioning is going conversational
You describe the outcome in plain language. The system writes the Terraform, runs the preview, and opens the PR for your review.
→ You’re still in the loop ~ but you’re no longer starting from a blank file.

2. AI agents are operating inside your CI/CD pipeline
Not just autocomplete. 
Agents that maintain state, respect policy guardrails, and take action directly inside your existing workflows ~ GitHub, GitLab, Jira, all of it.
→ The interface is shifting from “write the config” to “manage the agent doing it.”

3. IaC failure analysis is getting automated
Runner logs reviewed automatically. Root cause surfaced. Actionable fix suggested ~ before you even open the terminal.
→ The unglamorous, time-consuming part of DevOps is exactly where AI is winning first.

4. Multi-model infrastructure is becoming the default
No single AI provider dominates everything. Teams are designing systems to swap models based on the task ~ and building secrets management across multiple LLM backends from day one.
→ Model-agnostic infrastructure isn’t optional anymore. It’s the architecture decision many teams will be making soon.

• • •

The pattern across all four:

AI isn’t replacing the DevOps engineer.
It’s absorbing the repetitive, manual, high context-switching parts of the job.

The engineers who understand what’s happening under the hood will be the ones designing the systems .. not just using them.

• • •

Curious ~ which of these are you already seeing in your stack?

============================================================

If you're a DevOps engineer in your 20s, read this carefully:

It shouldn’t be Tools vs Experience.
It’s always the impact(%) you create >>>> how many tools you’ve used.

It shouldn’t be Certifications vs Projects.
It’s always real-world practice >>>> badge collecting.

It shouldn’t be “I only do infra” vs “I only write code.”
It’s always end-to-end ownership >>>> staying in your lane

It shouldn’t be Kubernetes vs Serverless.
It’s always Use the Right Tool >>>> use the hype tool.

It shouldn’t be Specialization vs Breadth.
It’s always T-shaped skills >>>> just doing one thing really well.

It shouldn’t be Technical vs Business.
It’s always delivering value >>>> just building for the sake of it.

It shouldn’t be Startup vs Enterprise.
It’s always a clear growth path (for you) >>>> company size debates.

Stop getting caught up in the wrong trade-offs.

Focus on what actually moves your career forward — and makes your work matter.

I took *this* certification, is this good enough?

Or what certification should I take to get into DevOps within Cloud?

I get this question quite often these days 

So here’s what I would do if I was starting from scratch.

1. 𝗖𝗵𝗼𝗼𝘀𝗲 𝗮 𝗦𝘁𝗿𝘂𝗰𝘁𝘂𝗿𝗲𝗱 𝗣𝗮𝘁𝗵: Certifications offer a well-designed learning roadmap. 

𝗠𝘆 𝘁𝗼𝗽 𝗽𝗶𝗰𝗸𝘀 𝗳𝗼𝗿 𝗯𝗲𝗴𝗶𝗻𝗻𝗲𝗿𝘀:
- LFCS (Linux Foundation Certified System Administrator)
- HashiCorp Terraform Associate
- AWS Cloud Practitioner
- Azure Fundamentals
- Google Cloud Digital Leader

2. 𝗕𝘂𝗶𝗹𝗱 𝗮 𝗦𝘁𝗿𝗼𝗻𝗴 𝗙𝗼𝘂𝗻𝗱𝗮𝘁𝗶𝗼𝗻: Consider these to round out your skills:
- CompTIA Network+
- CKAD (Certified Kubernetes Application Developer)
- Prometheus Certified Associate

3. 𝗥𝗲𝗺𝗲𝗺𝗯𝗲𝗿: 𝗖𝗲𝗿𝘁𝘀 ≠ 𝗝𝗼𝗯 𝗚𝘂𝗮𝗿𝗮𝗻𝘁𝗲𝗲
Certifications are valuable, but they're not a silver bullet. Complement your learning with hands-on projects to truly stand out.

4. 𝗟𝗲𝗮𝗿𝗻 𝗯𝘆 𝗗𝗼𝗶𝗻𝗴:
The most effective way to master cloud skills is through practical application. Build, experiment, and create real-world projects.

👉 Industry pros: What was your first DevOps certification? Share your experience below!

Save and share this list with others!

Author: vsadhwani
Date: May 17, 2025

Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7322643262303797248

I never planned to move to the U.S. — and yet, it changed everything about my career..

Honestly, I had no clear idea what I would be doing here. 
No five-year vision, no crystal-clear dream..

Straight out of my bachelor's, with a decent GPA, one research paper, and a profile that was, let's just say, "work in progress" — I applied to seven universities.

4 admits came through — a mix of ambitious and moderate schools.
I'd even paid a consultancy to help me with applications and guide me a bit, but when they stopped responding on time, I took full ownership.

So continued following the "rough plan" with just instincts, and a little blind faith that maybe — just maybe — I was doing okay.

When I arrived, few things flipped my world upside down:
— Culture and DIY theme
— Education system
— Work-life balance with part-time jobs
— Unknown and new opportunities
— And, of course, the currency

Had zero control over any of it. And that was exactly what worked, I guess.

I learned that:
- you need to be proactive and diligent with every action
- every step you take is connected to your status in the country (not embracing fear here, just awareness)
- it's important to carefully assess situations rather than blindly follow obvious paths
- you have to fight imposter syndrome (still fighting it at times)
- sometimes, you simply need to switch on survival mode..

Most importantly, I learned how to adapt — fast.

Not having a perfect plan didn’t hurt me.
In fact, it opened doors I didn’t even know existed.

I’m not saying life in my own country wouldn’t have taught me the same lessons — maybe it would have, maybe even better!
But this route took me on a different ride — one worth sharing.

So if you're thinking about moving to the U.S., be prepared for a few world flips.
Take notes, stay open, and trust that you'll find your way.

Sometimes, all you need is the courage to move — even when you don't know exactly where you're going.

============================================================

Weekends weren't meant for more work.
I often forget this, until life reminds me.

Over time, I’ve come to value rest just as much as progress. 
There was a phase when I was constantly chasing deadlines, setting new goals every weekend, and feeling guilty for not doing "enough." 

But it didn’t take long to realize that constant hustle isn’t sustainable — even the most driven minds need a pause.

This weekend made me realize this again. 
Witnessing the sunrise from Cadillac Mountain at Acadia National Park was a powerful reminder of why it’s essential to unplug.

That moment of awe — the morning air, the endless view, the quiet (even if I paused to snap it on my phone) - it still reminded me how small the daily grind really is.'s 

Now, I am focussed to use my weekends intentionally:
→ Disconnect from the pings and dings
→ Explore the unexplored or just sleep
→ Let my mind wander, without agendas
→ Practice the art of being, not just doing.

And you know what? 
Taking time off has helped me enjoy my weeks a lot more.
I won’t say it always makes me feel super focused or full of energy — but it does help me notice the small wins, get through the tough moments, and let go of any stress on my mind.


• • •

P.S. Some of my most refreshing moments have always been on those hiking trails.

What's your weekend ritual?

--- Post 332 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Aug 20, 2025
Engagement: 337 reactions, 25 comments, 12 reposts (Score: 1,959)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7362138936896315392

If you're adding it to your resume... know how to explain it like you have worked on it recently ☝ 

It’s funny because it happens more often than we admit — we list tools we’ve touched, but in interviews you’ll be asked about how you’ve actually used them.

If Kubernetes is on your resume, here’s what you can do - so you don’t get caught in the “used it once” trap:

1. Get hands-on again
→ Spin up a local cluster (kind, minikube, or KodeKloud labs) and deploy the resources you claim to know — StatefulSets, Deployments, DaemonSets, Ingress.

2. Go beyond “what” — explain “why”
 → If you can answer when to use a StatefulSet instead of a Deployment (and why it matters), you’re already ahead of 80% of candidates.

3. Simulate interview questions
→ Have a friend (or your fav AI chatbots) throw random “how would you” scenarios at you — scaling, failover, storage, rolling updates.

4. Learn by breaking things
→ Delete a pod, corrupt a volume, or simulate a node failure. See how Kubernetes reacts. This gives you real stories to tell.

Bottom line:
If you put a tool on your resume, make sure you could teach it in a whiteboard session tomorrow.

It’s better to be confident in a few tools than shaky in many.

Happy Friday!

If you’ve been hearing about AIOps but don’t fully understand it - here's what you need to know

How AIOps actually works (some examples):
• Collects data from multiple monitoring tools
• Uses ML to detect patterns and anomalies
• Automates incident response
• Provides predictive insights
• Reduces alert noise through correlation

How it helps?
1. Monitoring & Observability
→ AI-powered system health insights
→ Real-time performance analytics

2. Event Correlation
→ Smart grouping of related incidents
→ Dramatic reduction in alert fatigue

3. Anomaly Detection
→ Real-time pattern recognition
→ Automatic unusual behavior identification

4. Root Cause Analysis
→ AI-driven issue identification
→ Faster debugging with intelligent insights

5. Predictive Analytics
→ Outage prevention
→ Proactive bottleneck detection

6. Automated Remediation
→ Self-healing systems
→ Automatic resource scaling

How can you upskill:

Familiarize yourself with the core focus areas such as:
→ Data Analytics & Visualization,
→ Automated Incident Response
→ Predictive Modelling 
(Get an understanding of the processes)

Next, focus on the tools and platforms used in AIOps:
→ Monitoring: Prometheus, Grafana
→ Analytics: Elastic Stack
→ AIOps Platforms(to name a few): Dynatrace, Moogsoft, Splunk

Start Small:
Pick one use case and explore it via a project. 
Test it out and see how it aligns with your team’s needs.

If you're looking for a project to dive into, check out Example Project #2 - https://lnkd.in/djdFDeNb

I’ve always found the growing extensions of DevOps a bit overwhelming, especially with all the new tools and concepts - but once I started digging into it, everything started to click.
 
Sharing this to help others who might feel the same way.

Appreciation post.
A while back, I was sending some money home to pay off my parents’ car loan.

It wasn’t just another transfer. It was one of those moments when your heart races because of the large amount, but you know it will bring real relief.
The thought that my family wouldn’t have to worry about EMIs anymore made it worth it.

Everything went through, or so I thought.
The app didn’t show any update.
Money was deducted after a day, but there was no confirmation or progress update on when it would land. I assumed it might be because of the larger sum.

I reached out to support, sent a few emails, waited for hours, and heard nothing.
That’s when the real panic set in.

After several follow-ups, the money finally arrived 9 days later.
You think sending money home should be simple until something like that happens.

Since then, I’ve switched to Aspora, and what stood out wasn’t just the lower fees or faster transfers.

It’s how reliable and transparent the experience feels.
Every step has clear updates, and the exchange rate is exactly what you see on Google, with no markup.
The interface is clean and easy to navigate.

Best of all, customer support is quick, responsive, and genuinely helps reduce those panic moments.
Even for larger amounts, I get live updates until the transfer lands safely.

Small details like that make a big difference, especially when what you’re sending home carries more than just numbers.

If you’re sending money internationally, Aspora has been genuinely helpful for me.
Your first 3 transfers are free. And here's my link if you want to try it:
https://lnkd.in/dm8uGDwp, also you get $25 after your first transfer.

DevOps Engineer ≠ Platform Engineer 
(and how these roles are evolving in the AI era)

Let’s clear this up ~ they’re not interchangeable roles, even though it can get confusing because the responsibilities often overlap.

Also, AI-driven tooling and automation are blurring the lines faster than ever.

DevOps Engineers focus on:
• CI/CD pipelines and automation workflows
• Infrastructure as Code (Terraform, Ansible, etc.)
• Observability, monitoring, and incident response
• Bridging Dev + Ops for faster releases
• Managing integrations across environments and workflows

Their world is about automation, delivery speed, and reliability.

With AI entering the picture ~ they’re now:
→ Embedding LLM-based assistants into CI/CD flows
→ Using AI for anomaly detection in observability stacks
→ Automating incident triage and root cause analysis with AI-driven insights
---
Platform Engineers focus on:
• Building internal developer platforms (IDPs)
• Abstracting infra into reusable, self-service modules
• Managing multi-cluster, multi-cloud environments
• Standardizing golden paths and governance
• Enabling developer productivity at scale

Their world is about scale, standardization, and developer experience (DevEx).

And in the AI context ~ they’re now:
→ Integrating AI agents for platform observability and provisioning
→ Designing infrastructure for GPU workloads and inference pipelines
→ Building AI-ready platforms that support hybrid Dev + ML workflows
(pretty in-demand)

Here’s the key distinction:

DevOps engineers operate pipelines.
Platform engineers architect ecosystems.

DevOps is about process.
Platform is about productization.

If you’re in either of these roles ~ it’s a really good time to be here.
Both roles are steadily evolving toward AI-native engineering.

Curious to hear your take..
what other distinctions do you see between these two roles?

9 Harsh Truths About Cloud Engineering

Few years down this career path, here's what I've learned:

1. The simplest solution is usually best.
↳ Over-engineering doesn't mean better architecture.

2. DevOps tools aren't magic bullets.
↳ Their value depends on how well they fit your workflows, not their marketing.

3. Uptime improves with less cluttered code deployments.
↳ Focus on incremental, well-tested releases.

4. CI/CD isn't about speed - it's about confidence in production environments
↳ Can you sleep at night after deploying through your pipeline?

5. "Fully managed" doesn't mean no effort required.
↳ Responsibility is shared, not transferred.

6. Cloud bills will surprise you.
↳ Real optimization comes from understanding actual usage patterns, not theoretical estimates.

7. Kubernetes isn't always the answer.
↳ Sometimes simpler solutions are more effective.

8. Security is everyone's responsibility. 
↳ No exceptions.

9. 100% automation is a myth.
↳ Be prepared for manual intervention, even in the most automated systems.

The difference between junior and senior engineers ~
Isn't just technical knowledge or mastery of tools
It's understanding these truths and adapting accordingly.

What did I miss on the list?

5 Certification Exam Mistakes I Made 

(And You Probably Are Too)

7+ Certifications (AWS, GCP, Kubernetes!), plus countless recertifications...

It sounds impressive, right? But let me tell you, it wasn't always smooth sailing.

Staying up-to-date with technologies, managing a full-time job, constantly questioning my work-life balance—how could I not have made mistakes?

In Cloud & DevOps, I've learned a lot along the way, sometimes the hard way..

Here are some of the mistakes I made, so you can avoid them:

Mistakes ➡️ Fixes:

1. No Study Plan ➡️ Create a study plan before you start. Otherwise, you'll waste time switching sources, goals, and everything else.

2. Relying on outdated materials ➡️ The cloud changes FAST. Always check when a course was last updated. Outdated info can waste your time significantly!

3. Skipping hands-on labs ➡️ Learn by doing, not just watching or reading. Knowledge fades fast if you're not applying the theory!

4. Not having an exam date ➡️ Schedule your exam in advance! It'll motivate you to study consistently instead of procrastinating.

5. Being inconsistent ➡️ Don't take long breaks. Even a few minutes a day is better than nothing! It's way harder to restart after a long break

It's tough to get certified, but these common mistakes can really hold you back. 

Hopefully, you can fix these before the certification and technology become outdated 😂 

YOU GOT THIS!! Good luck! ❤

One language every DevOps engineer should know: YAML
(here’s why it matters even with LLMs — and free platforms you can use to practice)

Everything around you in Cloud & DevOps speaks YAML:
- Tools run on it
- Pipelines depend on it
- Even LLMs process it faster (fewer tokens = faster responses)

Kubernetes deployments? YAML.
GitHub Actions? YAML.
Ansible, Argo CD, Docker Compose, Cloudformation, Azure DevOps? 
All YAML-driven.

Unlike traditional programming languages, YAML isn't about logic—it's about structure. 
No loops, no conditions—just clean, readable config.

Perfect for beginners. Easy to write, easier to break (one indentation mistake - rip) - but crucial to learn.

Can you use JSON or XML instead? Technically, yes.
But in practice—YAML wins on clarity and adoption.

Interactive Practice Platforms (from beginner to advanced levels)

1. YAML Tutorial by TutorialsPoint (https://lnkd.in/gQQG4Rmq)
Covers YAML syntax, data types, and formatting with examples.

2. Code Beautify YAML Validator & Playground (https://lnkd.in/gy4m-fzf)
Paste, edit, and validate YAML live — great for testing.

3. Online YAML Parser & Linter (https://www.yamllint.com/)
Helps spot syntax errors and formatting issues instantly.

4. Play with Kubernetes YAML (https://k8syaml.com/)
Practice writing Kubernetes YAML files interactively — amazing for DevOps learners.

5. Kubernetes Official Docs - YAML Basics (https://lnkd.in/gf7rVi7X)
Learn YAML through real-world Kubernetes manifest examples.

6. GitHub Actions Workflow Syntax (https://lnkd.in/gU-fdEcy)
YAML is used to define CI/CD workflows — practice through real-world pipelines.

To delve deeper into DevOps and AI/ML use cases that leverage YAML, here's your guide.- https://lnkd.in/gTQu2gPs

--- Post 351 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Dec 18, 2025
Engagement: 269 reactions, 30 comments, 20 reposts (Score: 1,685)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7401292757283016704

If you’re working with Kubernetes - you must know how Operators work, and where they’re actually used.

Operators are the automation layer that makes complex, stateful, enterprise workloads possible.

In simple words - it extend Kubernetes’ extensibility to manage resources beyond the cluster.

They follow one simple cycle: Monitor → Compare → Apply

Here’s the breakdown :

1. Monitor (Observe Phase)
→ Operators define Custom Resource Definitions (CRDs) that introduce new resource types into k8s.
→ When you create a Custom Resource (CR), it gets stored in the k8s API Server like any native resource.
→ The Operator Controller continuously watches these CRs and the cluster’s live state.
→ This forms the “eyes and ears” of the Operator.

2. Compare (Reconcile Phase)
→ The Operator looks at the desired state defined in the CR (e.g., “run 3 pods”).
→ It compares this to the current state in the cluster (e.g., “only 2 pods are running”).
→ This evaluation happens in a continuous reconcile loop.
→ Anytime there’s a mismatch, it decides that the system needs correction.

3. Apply (Act Phase)
→ If the states don’t match, the Operator takes action ~ scale, rebalance, update, repair ~ whatever is needed.
→ It uses the Kubernetes API Server to update resources automatically.
→ Once changes apply, the “current state” is refreshed and re-evaluated in the next reconcile loop.

Why Operators are used:
→ They automate Day-2 operations (updates, scaling, healing).
→ They let teams run complex, stateful systems as if they were native Kubernetes resources.
→ They ensure the cluster always drifts back to the desired state ~ hands-off, reliable, predictable.
→ Ensures your model-serving infrastructure stays in sync. 

If you’re operating Kubernetes at any serious scale, understanding Operators is just as important as understanding Deployments..
especially now with AI workloads pushing cluster complexity.

--- Post 353 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Nov 18, 2025
Engagement: 273 reactions, 24 comments, 22 reposts (Score: 1,649)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7391151128039710721

If you’re building or scaling AI systems 
you should know these 6 API protocols and when to use each.

In traditional systems, API choice was mostly about developer convenience.
In the AI world, it’s about throughput, latency, and event flow.

When your models, pipelines, and dashboards all need to talk to each other ~ the protocol becomes part of your architecture design.
Here’s a breakdown :

1. REST
→ Still the backbone of most public and internal APIs.
→ Used to expose inference endpoints (think /predict or /classify) because it’s simple, stateless, and well supported across frameworks.
→ Most AI service providers (OpenAI, Hugging Face, Vertex AI) still default to REST for ease of integration.
When to use: Stable APIs with predictable request/response patterns.

2. SOAP
→ Enterprise-grade, schema-heavy, and XML-based.
→ Rare in modern ML pipelines but still seen in regulated sectors — finance, healthcare — where strict data contracts and auditability matter.
When to use: Legacy integrations or compliance-heavy AI systems that demand WSDL contracts.

3. gRPC
→ Designed for speed and efficiency — binary over HTTP/2, low latency, streaming supported.
→ Ideal for real-time inference pipelines, model serving microservices, or cross-language communication (Python ↔ C++).
→ For example: connecting an inference service written in Go with a data preprocessor in Python.
When to use: High-performance internal APIs and microservice-to-microservice communication in AI infra.

4. GraphQL
→ Shines in AI-powered dashboards or user personalization systems where the client defines the shape of data it needs.
→ Works great for analytics UIs that query model performance metrics, experiment runs, or user recommendations in one call.
When to use: Data-rich frontends or ML observability layers.

5. Webhooks
→ Event-driven APIs ~ the silent backbone of MLOps automation.
→ Used for model lifecycle events (model deployed, retrained, failed) or CI/CD triggers in AI pipelines.
→ Example: A training job completes → webhook notifies deployment service → new model version goes live.
When to use: Notifications, workflow chaining, and async triggers across your ML stack.

6. WebSockets
→ Persistent connection between client and server — perfect for real-time AI interactions.
→ Think LLM chat interfaces, speech-to-text streams, or live inference feedback.
→ Lets you stream responses token by token, instead of waiting for one big payload.
When to use: Conversational AI, live dashboards, or streaming inference workloads.

In AI architecture, the protocol layer is more than just a communication choice.
So be ready to justify your choices:)

Image Credits: ByteByteGo

Advice for DevOps engineers in 2026:
Learn how to monitor AI systems.

Not because it's trendy. Because it's coming to your infrastructure whether you're ready or not.

And here's the thing - AI doesn't fail like normal software.

Traditional failure: Service crashes → alert fires → you fix it.
AI failure: Model gives slightly wrong output → next step uses it → next step builds on it → 5 steps later, chaos.

Your dashboard shows a spike.
It doesn't show which model, which step, which input caused it.
This is the skill gap I see coming.

Engineers who understand AI observability will debug in minutes.
Everyone else will spend hours guessing.

I'm checking out New Relic Advance on Feb 24 to see how they're thinking about autonomous resolution and what it means for engineering teams dealing with increasingly complex systems.

If you're curious about where observability is headed, might be worth tuning in.

Register: https://lnkd.in/g-APp9TN

5 Critical Cloud Interview Mistakes

(and how to fix them)

In my experience interviewing candidates, I've observed common mistakes that often prevent them from receiving offers.

Here are 5 big ones:

1. Foundational Cloud Gaps

↳ Not understanding core principles like elasticity, scalability, and the shared responsibility model can sink your interview. 
✅ Master the fundamentals and core principles first.

2. Theory Without Practice

↳ Certification/Course knowledge isn't enough. 
✅ Use cloud free tiers to build projects and get some hands-on experience. ALSO document your journey.

3. Over-claiming Expertise

↳ Claiming expert-level knowledge in everything (especially given the vast cloud landscape) raises red flags. 
✅ Be honest about your capabilities and demonstrate a willingness to learn.

4. Missing Business Context

↳ Technical solutions alone won't cut it. 
✅ Show you understand cost implications, security requirements, and business impact.

5. Poor Problem-Solving Approach

↳ Rushing to solutions without gathering requirements or asking clarifying questions shows lack of methodology.
✅ Validate assumptions and use structured approaches (STAR/RSTAR) to formulate your response.

The truth here is that interviewers value honest problem-solvers over candidates who claim perfect knowledge.

➕➕ Make sure to review these guides before your next interview:
AWS: https://lnkd.in/dAZfjf9y
GCP: https://lnkd.in/d2zXRgK9
Azure: https://lnkd.in/d5Hhv-if

5 Critical Cloud Interview Mistakes

(and how to fix them)

In my experience interviewing candidates, I've observed common mistakes that often prevent them from receiving offers.

Here are 5 big ones:

1. Foundational Cloud Gaps

↳ Not understanding core principles like elasticity, scalability, and the shared responsibility model can sink your interview. 
✅ Master the fundamentals and core principles first.

2. Theory Without Practice

↳ Certification/Course knowledge isn't enough. 
✅ Use cloud free tiers to build projects and get some hands-on experience. ALSO document your journey.

3. Over-claiming Expertise

↳ Claiming expert-level knowledge in everything (especially given the vast cloud landscape) raises red flags. 
✅ Be honest about your capabilities and demonstrate a willingness to learn.

4. Missing Business Context

↳ Technical solutions alone won't cut it. 
✅ Show you understand cost implications, security requirements, and business impact.

5. Poor Problem-Solving Approach

↳ Rushing to solutions without gathering requirements or asking clarifying questions shows lack of methodology.
✅ Validate assumptions and use structured approaches (STAR/RSTAR) to formulate your response.

The truth here is that interviewers value honest problem-solvers over candidates who claim perfect knowledge.

➕➕ Make sure to review these guides before your next interview:
AWS: https://lnkd.in/dAZfjf9y
GCP: https://lnkd.in/d2zXRgK9
Azure: https://lnkd.in/d5Hhv-if 

What interview mistakes have you encountered in your career journey?

Check out this cloud-based AI Glossary, a list of 50 key terms every cloud professional should know 
(or at least have a sense of)

Over the past few years, AI has evolved rapidly — and with it, a wave of new terminology. Honestly, I found it hard to keep track of what all these terms actually meant (still do sometimes!).

Back when I wasn’t working in the infra/AI space, I often felt lost in conversations. So I started jotting down terms in a notebook — and that eventually turned into this cheat sheet.

The glossary is organized into 6 key areas to make it easy to navigate:

1. CORE AI CONCEPTS
→ From neural networks to deep learning fundamentals
→ Essential mathematical concepts you actually use

2. INFRASTRUCTURE & COMPUTE
→ Where cloud meets AI at scale
→ Computing resources and optimization
→ The backbone of production AI systems

3. MODELS & TRAINING
→ Different model specific terminologies
→ Training approaches and techniques

4. LANGUAGE & TEXT
→ NLP fundamentals for cloud implementations
→ Text processing at scale

5. AGENTS & ADD-ONS
→ Integration patterns
→ Extensibility frameworks

6. TRUST & SAFETY
→ Security considerations
→ Responsible AI terms

The AI landscape is vast, and I’ve definitely NOT captured every single term out there — so a quick heads-up (and respect) to the AI experts reading this.

But as cloud professionals, we’re building the infrastructure that makes AI reliable, scalable, and secure — so having a clear understanding of what does what really matters.

If you’re interviewing for a new DevOps role this week, soon, or at any point in the future - watch out for these 3 interview mistakes

#1 Getting Lost in Tool Talk
⇨ Listing tools instead of problems solved
⇨ Skipping how you chose the right tool
⇨ Missing the business impact of your solution

#2 Skipping the troubleshooting/Incident Stories
⇨ Not preparing detailed outage/incident examples 
⇨ Your learning curve - build, break, iterate
⇨ Forgetting to highlight what you learned (through those outages)
⇨ Not sharing how you prevented future issues

#3 The "I Work Alone" Trap
⇨ Forgetting to showcase cross-team collaboration
⇨ Not highlighting communication strategies (key for DevOps roles)
⇨ Missing examples of knowledge sharing
⇨ Skipping stories about mentoring others

To New Joiners: Your project experience from college, or any other relevant experience for that matter, can be valuable in place of work experience, so be sure to utilize these approaches.

📌 It's also crucial to understand and articulate how your role evolves with AI advancements. 

Highlight any experience or understanding you have with automating AI infrastructure (AIOps, MLOps, LLMOps) and the resulting career growth --interviewers are looking for your insights here!!

Author: vsadhwani
Date: Feb 16, 2026

URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7428493946957398017

If you’re building your expertise in Google Cloud this year,
here’s a 30-minute training path that can help.

Learning cloud (any cloud) isn’t hard.

Learning it without structure is. You can easily end up overwhelmed.

I’ve worked with one cloud before, and transitioning to another felt like starting from scratch.. but it doesn’t have to be that way.

There are many services, but these are the 7 buckets that actually show up in real applications and interviews 👇

1. Foundation & Security
↳ The base layer that controls identity, access, and cost from day one.
↳ Projects, IAM, service accounts, billing, Secret Manager.

2. Compute & Containers
↳ Where your applications actually run and scale.
↳ Compute Engine, Cloud Run/Functions, GKE, App Engine.

3. Storage & Databases
↳ How your data is stored, queried, and managed reliably.
↳ Cloud Storage, Cloud SQL, Firestore, BigQuery, Spanner.

4. Networking & Content Delivery
↳ How services communicate securely and reach users globally.
↳ VPC, Load Balancing, Cloud CDN, DNS, Cloud Armor.

5. DevOps & Automation
↳ How infrastructure and deployments are automated and repeatable.
↳ Terraform, Cloud Build, Artifact Registry, Pub/Sub.

6. AI & Machine Learning
↳ How you build, deploy, and integrate intelligent features.
↳ Vertex AI, Gemini API, Document AI, Vision, Speech.

7. Monitoring & Cost
↳ How you observe systems, troubleshoot issues, and control spend.
↳ Cloud Monitoring, Logging, budgets, audit logs.

Each bucket includes:
→ Hands-on tasks you can build immediately
→ Skills Boost labs (with Innovators credits)
→ 3 portfolio projects to apply everything

If you’re learning GCP this year, don’t try to learn everything at once.

Pick one bucket.
Go deep.
Build something.
Then move forward.

Which bucket are you focusing on right now?

============================================================

One of the best pieces of advice I can share after 9 years in the U.S. as an international student, immigrant, and engineer is this:

never lose sight of what keeps you grounded…

I’ve lived the hustle — from landing in the U.S. on a one-way ticket, to figuring out life as a student, to building an IT career step by step. 

Every phase felt like chasing the next thing: the next role, the next title, the next milestone.

And every time I come back to India, it’s the little things that slow me down. After a few rushed trips home, this time I finally had the space to just sit and spend time with my dog.

It wasn’t about how long we’d been apart — it was about how quickly she reminded me what slowing down feels like. 

The way she runs up, tail wagging, has a way of making the world pause… it’s grounding in a way no career milestone can ever be.

We often get restless chasing titles and skills. But they don’t define you. 
Family, home, and the small constants in life — those are what shape you and keep you humble, no matter where your career takes you.

So here’s what I am going to continue doing ~ 
grow, learn, aim high. 
But don’t lose sight of the people, the moments, and yes — even the pets❤️
~ that keep you whole.

Because in the end, success feels empty if you forget the things that make you complete.

Grateful this week — and ready to carry that energy back to work.

--- Post 365 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Dec 18, 2025
Engagement: 251 reactions, 18 comments, 25 reposts (Score: 1,485)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7396223097894338561

Your API’s status codes matter more than you think.
Especially when you’re debugging AI workloads at scale..

In reality, these codes quietly explain what is happening behind the scenes in model uploads, fine-tuning jobs, inference pipelines, GPU autoscaling, and streaming responses.

Here is the AI-workloads version of how to read them.

1xx Informational
→ 100 Continue: Server ready for next chunk during large dataset or model uploads.
→ 101 Switching Protocols: Connection upgraded, for example to WebSocket for real-time inference.
→ 102 Processing: Async fine-tune or inference job is still running.
→ 103 Early Hints: Model streaming response warming the connection.
---

2xx Success
→ 200 OK: Large dataset or model upload processed; ready for next chunk.
→ 201 Created: Connection upgraded or new inference session created.
→ 202 Accepted: Async fine-tune or inference job accepted and still running.
→ 204 No Content: Model stream warming or response not yet ready.
---

3xx Redirection
→ 301 Moved Permanently: Endpoint or model version changed; redirected to latest.
→ 302 Found: Temporary redirect to a different GPU region or inference endpoint.
→ 303 See Other: Redirect to a polling endpoint for job status or result.
→ 307 Temporary Redirect: Same-method retry to a healthy inference replica.
---

4xx Client Errors
→ 400 Bad Request: Malformed JSON, wrong schema, or invalid parameters.
→ 401 Unauthorized: Expired API key or invalid org or project scope.
→ 403 Forbidden: Safety filter or regional restriction blocked the request.
→ 429 Too Many Requests: Rate limit exceeded based on RPM or TPM caps.
---

5xx Server Errors
→ 500 Internal Server Error: GPU node down, crashed shard, or provider-side failure.
→ 502 Bad Gateway: Upstream inference container not responding.
→ 503 Service Unavailable: Autoscaler starting or backend under heavy load.
→ 504 Gateway Timeout: Inference took too long and backend timed out.

Quick Guide:
Fix the request → 400 to 403
Retry later → 408, 429, 500 to 504
Migrate or upgrade → 301, 410, 426

HTTP status codes are still one of the fastest debugging tools you have, especially when scaling AI workloads across compute, regions, and autoscaling systems.

Which category hits you the most during debugging?

============================================================

Author: vsadhwani

7 core AI workloads you must know as an AI Infrastructure Engineer.

AI systems aren’t just about models ~ they’re about the workloads behind them.

Each workload behaves differently.. and that decides how you design, scale, and optimize your infra.

Let’s see how each workload differs 👇🏻

Training Workloads
→ Where models actually learn.
→ GPU-heavy, distributed, and storage-intensive.
Infra focus: multi-GPU clusters, fast storage, networking

Inference Workloads
→ Where models serve predictions in production.
→ Real-time, batch, streaming, or edge-focused.
Infra focus: Triton, TensorRT, GPU sharing, quantization.

Data Processing & Feature Engineering
→ Prepares data before it touches any model.
→ ETL, feature stores, vectorization, embeddings.
Infra focus: Spark/Ray pipelines, Airflow/Kubeflow, RAPIDS acceleration.

Evaluation & Validation
→ Checks if the model works as expected.
→ Offline tests, online tests, drift and quality checks.
Infra focus: integrated CI/CD, GPU-light to GPU-heavy testing

Orchestration & Pipeline Workloads
→ Connects all ML steps into automated workflows.
→ Scheduling, DAGs, ML pipelines, workflow automation.
Infra focus: Kubernetes, Argo, Ray Serve, SageMaker/Vertex pipelines

Monitoring & Optimization Workloads
→ Keeps production reliable.
→ Model monitoring, autoscaling, observability, feedback loops.
Infra focus: Prometheus, Grafana, OpenTelemetry, NVIDIA DCGM.

Generative AI & Agentic Workloads
→ The newest and fastest-growing category.
→ LLM generation, RAG, agents, multi-modal inference, tool-using systems.
Infra focus: vector DBs, hybrid GPU/CPU orchestration, adaptive batching.

From data pipelines → to model pipelines → to AI systems that generate, reason, and act

The stack is changing.
And now is a great time to upskill.

============================================================

CKA vs CKAD 
Which Kubernetes Certification Should You take first?

The basic difference, in a nutshell, is:

*CKA (Certified Kubernetes Administrator) is at the cluster level.

- You're setting up the foundation - networking, storage, roles, policies, cluster maintenance.

- Think: Troubleshooting clusters, managing storage classes, setting up security policies etc.

*CKAD (Certified Kubernetes Application Developer) is at the application level.

- You're executing your workloads using pods, deployments, services.

- Think: Container image management, application deployment patterns, service exposure etc.

-------
The practical stuff:
• Both cost $445 - increased recently :(
• Both are 2-hour performance/lab-based exams
• Both give you one free retake
• Valid for 2 years (previously it was 3 years)
• CKA has 17 questions | CKAD has 17-22
• Both allow official docs access during exam

Which One Should You Take First?

↳ New to Kubernetes? CKAD has a broader scope and kinder learning curve:)

↳ Systems/DevOps folks often prefer CKA (cluster-level skills)

↳ Developers mostly find value with CKAD (application-centric approach)

Check the syllabus of each cert for a more comprehensive view.
- https://lnkd.in/dcnrDwR8
- https://lnkd.in/drC7bq6y

Also if you want to pursue CKS in the future- 
CKA is a prerequisite. 

These exams are expensive🙃

Choose the one that best fits your job profile and career goals!

Author: vsadhwani

AI Infrastructure layers you should know if you're leveling up as a cloud engineer

So, it’s not just about powerful models — it’s about the infrastructure stack that enables them to run at scale.

From training massive foundational models to serving low-latency inference, it’s these layers that make it all possible.

Here are 5 foundational layers you should know:
1. AI Workloads
 → The “what” of AI infra — training, fine-tuning, inference, evaluation
 → These workloads vary in compute and latency needs
 Think: large distributed training jobs vs. real-time predictions at the edge

2. ML Lifecycle Layer
 → Ingesting and preprocessing data
 → Managing training pipelines, experiment tracking, and versioned deployments
 → Model registries, CI/CD for ML, and drift monitoring are critical here

3. Software & Frameworks
 → Core AI libraries: PyTorch, TensorFlow, JAX etc
 → MLOps tools: Specialized tools like MLflow, DVC etc that help manage and automate the entire lifecycle of AI models
 → Optimization tools are critical to productionize models

4. Orchestration Layer
 → Kubernetes to manage containers and scale jobs
 → Ray for distributed Python workloads, Slurm with HPC etc
 → Workflow engines (Airflow, Metaflow etc) automate and scale complex pipelines

5. Infrastructure Layer
 → Compute: GPUs (A100/H100), TPUs, CPUs — spot or on-demand
 → Storage: Object, block, file systems =
 → Networking: High-speed interconnects, private VPCs
 → Infra-as-code (Terraform, Helm) ensures repeatability & compliance

These are just a few layers but there are complex functional layers that works behind the scenes to power the innovative AI solutions

Also with cloud providers - you have options..
Managed vs. Self-Managed
→ Self-managed = full control (great for foundational model R&D, but complex)
→ Managed = faster iteration, ideal for fine-tuning & inference

Read the full breakdown in the newsletter here: https://lnkd.in/dZvkpP4j

Stay tuned — in the next post: we'll deep dive into compute options for AI workloads.

"Data Is Your Generative AI Differentiator" by Kalpana Mahesh (Head of Data & Gen AI,Amazon Web Services (AWS))

Key takeaways: 
✨ The future of tech is brighter than ever – powered by brilliant women. 
🛠️ Imposter syndrome? Debugged.
💡 Community is the true driver of innovation.

A special shoutout to the NYC Community Lounge for helping me connect with incredible mentors and peers from New York – the insights and bonds I formed here are priceless. 

And, if you stopped by the Expo, you know the Rewriting the Code booth had the best view and was the perfect spot to recharge. 😎

Feeling inspired and ready to take on the world after this amazing event! 🙏 Thanks to AnitaB.org for hosting such a phenomenal experience.

--- Post 375 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Feb 16, 2026
Engagement: 220 reactions, 28 comments, 8 reposts (Score: 1,396)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7419055169692307456

If you’re wondering whether transitioning to DevOps is still worth it, here’s something you should know..

Yes, a lot of DevOps tasks are easier now ~ thanks to AI.

→ Spinning up CI/CD pipelines is no longer the hard part
→ Configuring Docker files is easy
→ Generating Kubernetes manifests is easy
→ Writing automation scripts is no longer the bottleneck
→ Terraform modules are easy to create now

AI can generate configs.
AI can scaffold pipelines.
AI can fix syntax errors.

But here’s what companies are still struggling with:

→ Migrating legacy systems (VMs → containers)
→ Maintaining and operating hybrid environments (on-prem + cloud)
→ Resource utilization inside clusters ~ CPU, memory, and now GPUs
→ Preventing compute waste, over-provisioning, and idle nodes
→ Capacity planning for AI/ML workloads 
→ Debugging why pods are pending, throttled, or evicted
→ Planning rollouts, rollbacks, and failure scenarios
→ Manually validating outputs under real load and failure conditions
→ Coordinating across dev, infra, security, and business constraints
and so much more...

AI writes code. 
Humans design systems. 
Humans fix disasters. Humans make judgment calls.

If you're chasing DevOps because you think it's "easy infrastructure work" - don't. The easy parts are automated.

So, is DevOps still worth chasing?

In my opinion, yes.
But only if you’re learning:
→ how systems fit together
→ how migrations actually work
→ how resource and cost trade-offs are made
→ how to run clusters where GPUs don’t sit idle

The question isn't "is DevOps worth it?" - it's "are you ready to solve problems AI can't?"

--- Post 377 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Dec 18, 2025
Engagement: 216 reactions, 28 comments, 11 reposts (Score: 1,382)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7402021590118436865

AI Engineer != ML Engineer

Let me clear this up once and for all ~ these two roles are related, but they’re definitely not interchangeable.

Here’s the breakdown:

AI Engineering itself branches into two distinct paths:

✓ AI Engineers (Data Science Focus):
• Strong in statistics, experimentation, and model behavior
• Fine-tune and evaluate foundation models
• Analyze data quality, bias, and performance
• Collaborate closely with ML teams on model improvements
• Build evaluation pipelines and feedback loops

✓ AI Engineers (Developer/Systems Focus):
• Strong in backend engineering and distributed systems
• Integrate AI into applications using agents and tool-calling
• Build APIs, pipelines, and orchestration layers
• Optimize inference costs, latency, and reliability
• Own deployment, scaling, and production workflows

✓ ML Engineers focus on:
• Designing and training custom ML models
• Building data pipelines and preprocessing workflows
• Running experiments and tuning hyperparameters
• Tracking performance, drift, and evaluation metrics
• Managing scalable training and retraining setups

The key distinction?
✓ ML Engineering is about the model.
✓ AI Engineering is about the system built around that model.

Both roles overlap in areas like:
→ Deploying ML models
→ Managing the ML lifecycle
→ Automating evaluation and monitoring

Whether you enjoy deep modeling or system-level engineering, there’s a path for you in AI.

What’s your take on this?

--- Post 378 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Sep 19, 2025

Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7372653401018372096

If you’re a Cloud Engineer aiming for big tech — here are 5 strategies to learn from Uber’s architecture 
(and how you can use them in practice)

Uber isn’t just moving cars and food — it’s moving billions of requests every day; and behind every ride request lies a powerful distributed tech architecture..

Here’s what you can learn (and apply in interviews):

1. Microservices & Service Mesh
↳ Uber relies on NGINX, gRPC, Thrift, and internal gateways for service-to-service communication.
↳ Practice building microservices with clear APIs, and understand how service mesh improves observability, routing, and resilience.

2. Distributed Databases & Caching
↳ From Redis to Elasticsearch to memSQL, Uber mixes relational + NoSQL + search engines.
↳ Learn trade-offs: when to pick SQL vs NoSQL, and how caching reduces query load at scale.

3. Big Data Storage & Processing
↳ Uber handles petabytes with Hadoop, Hive, Kafka, Flink, and Spark.
↳ Build projects around event-driven data pipelines — think real-time stream processing for analytics and decision-making.

4. CI/CD & Infra Automation
↳ Tools like Bazel, Spinnaker, Terraform, and Peloton keep Uber’s deployments automated.
↳ Practice setting up pipelines, IaC, and autoscaling strategies — these are common interview scenarios.

5. Observability & Reliability Engineering
↳ Uber invests heavily in monitoring (uMonitor, uMetric, M3) to keep services healthy.
↳ Know how to design end-to-end observability (metrics, logs, traces) and explain how it ties to uptime SLAs.

You don’t need to know every single tool Uber uses.
What matters is the principles and design thinking behind them:
- Know when to use which component (SQL vs NoSQL, cache vs DB, streaming vs batch).
- Understand the design principles (scalability, fault-tolerance, observability).
- Be ready to answer scenario-based questions (e.g., “How would you handle a traffic spike?”).

Detailed newsletter (cloud engineer version) coming up next week - tune in here: tech5ense.com

--- Post 379 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Nov 18, 2025
Engagement: 219 reactions, 26 comments, 11 reposts (Score: 1,377)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7386113263996071936

The AWS downtime this week shook more systems than expected - here’s what you can learn from this real-world case study.

1. Redundancy isn’t optional
Even the most reliable platforms can face downtime.
Distributing workloads across multiple AZs isn’t enough.. design for multi-region failover.

2. Visibility can’t be one-sided
When any cloud provider goes dark, so do its dashboards.
Use independent monitoring and alerting to stay informed when your provider can’t.

3. Recovery plans must be tested
A document isn’t a disaster recovery strategy.
Inject a little chaos ~ run failover drills and chaos tests before the real outage does it for you.

4. Dependencies amplify impact
One failing service can ripple across everything.
You must map critical dependencies and eliminate single points of failure early.

These moments are a powerful reminder that reliability and disaster recovery aren’t checkboxes ..
They’re habits built into every design decision.

Author: vsadhwani

Could this cloud architecture be any more accurate? 🤓 

Every cloud project ever:

starts with global assumptions
→ Of course, this reverse proxy will fix everything
→ CIDR makes sense to... someone
→ The VPN? your gateway to curiosity

then we get Containers
→ "LOOK AT ALL THESE CONTAINERS!"
→ Who’s managing them? => They manage each other.

naturally: cool databases vs real database
→ Cool DB: trending tech
→ Real DB: legacy system held together by midnight heroics

enter: the data engineer rig
→ Built from scratch
→ No one understands it but we really need it
→ Occasionally spills into the data swamp

and ultimately the blame radius
 → Services that start stuff
 → Services that stop stuff
 → Services that like to watch stuff
 → One tiny cron job keeping the world together

At the heart of it: a team of engineers bravely pretending this is exactly how it was designed to work 🫡 

Tag your cloud crew. Let’s celebrate the chaos that somehow still works.

Your job is never just your job.
You’re expected to build.

But also document.
Align with stakeholders.
Summarize decisions.

Jump between things like Slack, Jira, docs, dashboards.
And somehow still stay effective at the actual work.

In enterprise environments, the hardest part isn’t writing code.
- It’s stitching context together.
- Updates live in chat.
- Decisions live in docs.
- History lives in tickets.
Knowledge lives across systems.. and in people’s heads.

We spend hours just reconstructing what already exists.

It’s AI grounded in an enterprise context.. understanding projects, systems, permissions, and workflows; and then helping you move from insight → to execution.

That shift matters.

Because in real enterprise work, answers are not enough.
You need context.
You need traceability.
You need safe actions across systems.

For professionals.. especially those early in their careers; 
understanding how enterprise AI assistants like Glean fit into modern workflows is important.

Author: vsadhwani
