AIOps vs LLMOps vs MLOps vs DevOps — what’s the difference?
and how AI is creating new opportunities for DevOps Engineers

Here devops is the central process — the rest are extensions tailored to different workloads. 

Each has its own distinct focus 👇

DevOps → evolving with AI
→ No longer just about CI/CD and containerization — it’s the backbone of modern workflows.
→ AI helps auto-generate configs, optimize pipelines, and detect deployment risks.

📌DevOps Role: set up CI/CD, manage infra as code, and leverage AI copilots to ship features faster and safer.

⸻

MLOps → models in production
→ No longer just training models — it’s about deploying and keeping them reliable at scale.
→ Focuses on data versioning, pipeline orchestration, and drift monitoring.

📌DevOps Role: build Kubernetes pipelines for ML, integrate monitoring for models, and ensure reproducibility with tools like MLflow/DVC.

⸻

AIOps → intelligence in operations
→ Detect anomalies and incidents with AI/ML.
→ Enable predictive scaling and self-healing systems.

📌 DevOps Role: connect monitoring systems to AIOps platforms, fine-tune detection thresholds, and test auto-remediation workflows.

⸻

LLMOps → serving large language models
→ Fine-tune, serve, and scale LLMs in production.
→ Manage inference costs, latency, and guardrails for safe outputs.

📌 DevOps Role: deploy LLM inference on GPUs, optimize autoscaling, and set up guardrails and monitoring for cost and safety

⸻

So no — AI won’t replace DevOps jobs. In fact, the field is expanding for those ready to adapt.

3 Google Cloud Repos to build hands-on projects with in-demand skills like kubernetes, cloud achitectures and serverless
(based on your expertise level)

One is perfect for beginners to get hands-on quickly.
Another lets you deep-dive into microservices with Kubernetes & Istio.
And the third offers solution patterns for real-world cloud architectures.

🔷 For Beginners
1. cloud-code-samples
→ Easily build & deploy apps on Cloud Run, GKE, and Secret Manager
→ IDE-integrated code templates in Python, Java, Go, Node.js, and .NET
Link: https://lnkd.in/df9XUPTw

🔷 For Intermediate Learners
2. microservices-demo 
→ A production-grade microservices e-commerce app (aka Online Boutique)
→ Uses Kubernetes, Istio, gRPC, GKE, Cloud Spanner, Pub/Sub, and more
 Link: https://lnkd.in/eP9RqjDK

3. cloud-solutions 
→ A collection of real-world multi-service solution patterns on GCP
→ Covers data analytics, app modernization, security, and hybrid cloud architectures
Link: https://lnkd.in/dNZZGHRG

🔷 Project examples you can build:
1. Deploy containerized apps on GKE with Istio
 → Shows skills in Kubernetes, Istio, traffic management, observability

2. Implement service meshes & gRPC communications
 → Shows skills with microservices architecture, service mesh, gRPC protocols

3. Architect secure, multi-cloud data solutions
→ Shows experience with cloud architecture, security best practices, hybrid cloud design

4. Experiment with serverless via Cloud Run
→ Shows expertise in serverless computing, event-driven design, rapid deployment

There are plenty more examples in the repos for you to explore and experiment with.

These resources provide solid foundations — build on them, add some AI integrations, and keep iterating. 
Build, break, and repeat.

This hands-on experience is exactly what you can showcase and speak about in interviews. Good luck!

--- Post 393 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Jun 21, 2025
Engagement: 188 reactions, 23 comments, 4 reposts (Score: 1,178)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7335695029266493440

When you're picking compute for your ML workload... and accidentally achieve enlightenment instead!

In all seriousness, these accelerators are the heart of AI/ML workloads.

Let's break down the key compute types and their roles in real-world setups:

CPUs
→ Everyday, general-purpose processors 
→ Great for data prep, orchestration, and traditional ML 
→ Best for non-parallel tasks (like loading data or running APIs)
→ Work well with GPUs and TPUs behind the scenes
→ Flexible, cost-effective, and widely compatible

GPUs
→ Built for massive parallelism – great at matrix and tensor math 
→ Powers most modern deep learning models (e.g., GPT, DALL-E, Llama) 
→ Compatible with all major ML frameworks: PyTorch, TensorFlow, JAX 
→ Available in different memory formats (16GB, 80GB+..) 
→ Enables custom CUDA kernels and fine-grained optimization
→ Strong support for both research and production.

TPUs
→ Google's custom chips built specifically for AI. 
→ Optimized for large matrix operations using systolic arrays. 
→ Works well with leading AI frameworks like TensorFlow, JAX & Pytorch via XLA compiler. 
→ Uses "slices" to parallelize training across units. 
→ Delivers high energy efficiency and cost-performance. 
→ Ideal for large-scale training of LLMs and transformers.

Cloud Compute Modes
→ On-Demand: pay-as-you-go. Simple and flexible 
→ Spot Instances: big savings, best for fault-tolerant workloads
→ Reserved: lower rates for 1–3 year commitment
→ Dedicated/Custom Instances: high-performance for specialized workloads

Quick Tips for Choosing the Right Compute
→ Match your compute to your model size. 
→ Stick with frameworks best suited for the hardware. 
→ Keep an eye on your cloud bill. 
→ Think about latency and scalability. 
→ Consider your deployment style. 
→ Check for ecosystem maturity.

If you want to dive deeper, you can read the full newsletter here: https://lnkd.in/euZHdTqB

Stay tuned – tomorrow's edition covers data pipelines and storage: where your models get their fuel!

DevOps engineers aren't scared of AI replacing them.

They're scared:

↳ Of maintaining AI systems without proper documentation or understanding.

↳ That AI tools will be pushed into production before they're fully secure & tested.

↳ Of optimizing models to ensure cost management (because those GPUs are expensive!)

↳ That "AI-powered automation" will lead to unrealistic delivery expectations.

↳ Of managing unstable infrastructure without clear rollback strategies (because of complexity).

And here's the thing, we are:

• The ones building AI deployment pipelines.
• Enabling ML model serving at scale.
• Automating AI infrastructure provisioning.
• The backbone of AI operations, AiOps, MLOps, LLMOps....
and so much more....

-----

In all honesty - this field isn't just the supporting the AI era - we're powering it, and honestly, it's pretty awesome to be a part of it

It once took me 3+ hours to debug a simple YAML error in my infra pipeline…
Now it takes me 5 minutes with VIBE coding.

Here’s how I am leveraging VIBE coding:

→ catches YAML/JSON/Dockerfile errors in real time
→ enforces consistent patterns across infra code
→ integrates into pipelines to block bad configs before deploy
→ generates clean scripts to replace repetitive shell hacks
→ readable, maintainable code across teams
→ fewer rollbacks, faster delivery, higher stability

If I were leveling up as a cloud devops engineer right now, I'd focus on these 5 areas
(that are actually changing our field with AI)

→ What goes into building model serving pipelines
→ Understand GPU resource management

2. AI-Enhanced Monitoring
→ Learn how to implement anomaly detection pipelines
→ Understand how predictive alerting systems work
→ Build workflows for automated root cause analysis

3. Infrastructure as Code with AI
→ Use AI tools for smarter code completion (even scripts)
→ Set up automated code review processes 
→ Learn how AI can optimize your infrastructure (specific pipeline components)

4. AI-Driven Security
→ How can you integrate ML-based threat detection into your stack
→ Understand the vulnerabilities unique to AI models
→ Automate security testing in your pipeline

5. Performance Optimization
→ Learn how to scale AI workloads efficiently (HA + VA)
→ Understand how to reduce model serving latency
→ Implement smarter resource allocation strategies

Also remember - AI tools should enhance your core skills, not replace them. 
Focus on integration points where AI meets traditional infrastructure.

--- Post 414 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Oct 19, 2025
Engagement: 133 reactions, 29 comments (Score: 955)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7378454770631589890

just a few learnings for engineers picking up Kubernetes in the AI era (after spending years in this space):

→ if you’re only learning kubernetes architecture, objects and basic deployments, you better move up the stack fast — AI workloads bring new patterns.

→ you must understand how Kubernetes maps back to Linux (cgroups, namespaces, networking) ~ this is what keeps your clusters alive.

→ you must build with observability from day one: metrics, logs, traces. AI models eat GPUs, and without visibility, you’re burning $$ blind.

→ if YAML is your safety net - it's time to move on. Automation, GitOps, and operators are the new baseline.

→ your context must include scale — GPUs, distributed training, autoscaling. 
Traditional microservice scaling ≠ AI-era scaling.

→ infra success depends on orchestration. Whether it’s multi-cloud, hybrid, or on-prem GPU clusters — Kubernetes is the OS of modern infra, and those who can design with this in mind have a massive opportunity ahead.

→ right next to that: best practices like FinOps and CostOps are no longer optional. AI infra without cost governance won’t survive

⸻

The foundations haven’t changed — containers, networking, automation, security. 
But the stack is evolving fast. Master the basics, then adapt them to AI-era workflows. 

Sooner the better :)

============================================================

--- Post 415 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Dec 18, 2025
Engagement: 151 reactions, 17 comments, 4 reposts (Score: 933)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7399873256834371584

Is coding still relevant in 2026? If yes, which programming language should you pick based on your career path?

If you’re debating Python vs Rust vs anything else… 
here’s the simplest breakdown of the languages that truly matter now; 
and the roles each one leads to.

→ Python
The backbone for data, machine learning, automation, scripting, and AI engineering.
If you’re in Cloud, DevOps, ML, or AI Infra.. you can start with this.

→ TypeScript
The modern standard for anything web-facing.
Great for frontend, full-stack, internal tools, and dashboards.

→ Rust
For engineers working close to systems, infra, performance-critical AI workloads, inference runtimes, and distributed systems.
If you’re aiming for infra or high-performance engineering roles, Rust is worth exploring.

→ Go (Golang)
Designed for cloud-scale systems: Kubernetes, microservices, platform engineering, DevOps tooling, and backend infra.
If you’re in Cloud/DevOps/SRE ~ Go will open doors.

→ SQL
Still the universal interface for data. Whether you’re in Cloud, ML, analytics, backend, or AI pipelines ~ SQL never goes out of demand.

If you’ve been trying to decide what to learn next, this video might give you the clarity you need.
A simple framework to understand:
→ Where each language is used
→ Which roles need it
→ Free resources to level up

Check out the whole video here:
https://lnkd.in/duSrpVMk

TLDR;
Yes, low-code is rising. Yes, AI will help with it.
But the logic, the thinking, the problem-solving ~ that part still comes from you.

So picking one language and getting genuinely good at it is still your biggest advantage.

============================================================

5 Underrated Cloud Roles Most People Don't Know About.. 
(but you should know👇)

No joke, when I entered the Cloud Computing world in 2017, I focused solely on gaining skills and making my resume "𝗹𝗼𝗼𝗸 𝗴𝗼𝗼𝗱 𝗼𝗻 𝗽𝗮𝗽𝗲𝗿" instead of targeting specific roles.

Tech changes all the time, so it's okay if things feel uncertain. I hear you.

But good news! There are some jobs in the cloud that are here to stay, which focus on areas ranging from Cloud Adoption to Cloud Implementation Engineering.

AND, understanding these profiles IS your competitive edge

❌ Most aspiring cloud engineers focus solely on gaining skills.
✅ Understanding cloud roles and tailoring your profile provides a significant advantage

- Some roles benefit from hands-on experience with cloud technologies. 
- Some roles involve working closely with customers to address their specific needs. 
- Some roles require a strong background in specific domains such as Networking, DevOps, Security, AI/ML etc.

Check out the document below for details around the key focus areas of these roles 👇🏼
➟ Cloud Sales Representative
➟ Solutions Architect
➟ Cloud Platform Architect
➟ Cloud Support Engineer
➟ Technical Account Manager

How it will help?
#1 Help you tailor your profile to specific role requirements
#2 Stand out to recruiters looking for specialized skills
#3 Connect with professionals in your target role (VERY IMPORTANT)
#4 Demonstrate role-specific knowledge and passion

We talk a lot about mentors —
but no one really tells you why you need a sponsor... 

A mentor gives you advice.
A sponsor puts your name in rooms you're not in yet.

Just wrapped 'Lead to Win' by Carla Harris — and it’s one of those rare books that doesn’t waste your time with fluff. 

Some sharp, timely reminders we tend to forget at work:
→ Ask clearly for what you want
→ Speak your ideas — don’t wait to be invited
→ Use your voice to lift others
→ A mentor is helpful, but a sponsor changes the game
→ Real power? You grow it by giving it away

also, they hit different when you’ve been navigating your own path for a while.

I’m not a big book reader. But I happened to pick this one up (even got a signed copy by Carla!) — and I’m so glad I did.

If you’re in a career transition, stepping into leadership, or just rethinking how you show up — this is worth your time.

Happy Sunday ✨

Give me 2 minutes and I'll explain how CI/CD works

If you're new to DevOps, this chapter is the heart of software delivery.

CI/CD, aka Continuous Integration and Continuous Delivery, is your pipeline to get new software to users quickly and reliably. It's all about automating the build, test, and deployment process, so you can ship code faster and with fewer headaches.

A typical CI/CD pipeline has several connected stages:

➟ Developer commits code changes to source control (such Git)

➟ CI server detects changes and triggers build 

➟ Code is compiled, tested (unit, integration tests) 

➟ Test results reported to developer 

➟ On success, artifacts are deployed to staging environments 

➟ Further testing may be done on staging before release (e.g., User Acceptance Testing, Performance Testing) 

➟ CD system deploys approved changes to production (Manual approvals required in few cases)

➟ Continuous Monitoring of the application in production 

➟ Rollbacks in case of issues ⏪

Want to dive deeper? Check out this video for implementing a CI/CD pipeline and some of the best practices:
 https://lnkd.in/dC3denDG

--- Post 419 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: May 17, 2025
Engagement: 90 reactions, 38 comments, 2 reposts (Score: 834)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7287908633693069313

Attention DevOps Practitioners: Don't Get TOO attached with the Tools

Focus on the processes first.

Covering aspects of each stage in a DevOps pipeline below :

1. 𝗣𝗹𝗮𝗻: Define project vision and requirements strategically.
2. 𝗖𝗼𝗱𝗲: Write clean, efficient source code in collaborative environment.
3. 𝗕𝘂𝗶𝗹𝗱: Transform code into executable software artifacts.
4. 𝗧𝗲𝘀𝘁: Validate software quality through comprehensive automated testing.
5. 𝗥𝗲𝗹𝗲𝗮𝘀𝗲: Package and prepare software for production deployment.
6. 𝗗𝗲𝗽𝗹𝗼𝘆: Safely transfer software to target infrastructure.
7. 𝗢𝗽𝗲𝗿𝗮𝘁𝗲: Manage and maintain system performance and stability. 
8. 𝗠𝗼𝗻𝗶𝘁𝗼𝗿: Track real-time system health and performance metrics.

Key Points to Remember about DevOps:
➟ It's not just a toolchain.
➟ Tools enable, concepts are paramount.

Start with the core processes first:
- CI/CD, 
- Containerization, 
- Observability and 
- Automation!

Then take one tool at a time!!

Pro Tip : Tools and technology are enablers of the process, not its drivers ☝ 

Thanks to Brij Kishore Pandey for this incredible resource!!

5 AI prompts to build a powerful, company-specific Cloud DevOps Resume.
Save these👇 

1. Resume Enhancement Prompt:

"Analyze this job description [paste Job Description] and optimize my experience [paste your experience] to highlight relevant Cloud & DevOps skills. Focus on infrastructure as code, CI/CD, and containerization achievements."

——

2. Technical Skills Prompt:

"Review my skills section and suggest improvements based on current Cloud DevOps market demands. Include trending tools and certifications that align with [company name]'s tech stack."

——

3. Project Impact Prompt:

"Help me quantify the impact of this project: [paste project]. Focus on metrics like deployment frequency, recovery time, and infrastructure cost savings."

——

4. Cover Letter Hook:

"Create an engaging opening paragraph that connects my [specific experience] with [company]'s Cloud transformation goals. Emphasize automation and scalability achievements."

——

5. Cultural Fit Prompt:

"Based on [company]'s values and this job posting [paste Job description], help me highlight relevant soft skills and DevOps culture experiences."

Play around with these prompts and tailor it as needed!

Also, always review and personalize AI outputs. 
Your authentic voice matters!

Author: vsadhwani

Engagement: 125 reactions, 17 comments (Score: 795)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7341850826384003072

If only picking Kubernetes was that simple…

Welcome to the classic case of: it depends!

One minute, it feels like the future of infrastructure.
The next, it feels like overkill for a small team just trying to ship features.

Kubernetes makes sense when:
- You’ve got complex workloads (with multiple microservices)
- You’re scaling fast
- You have the resources to manage it

But if you're solo or just building a couple of APIs,
you might be solving problems you don't even have yet.

Kubernetes isn’t bad — it’s just not always the right place to start.

And skipping the basics only makes it harder to get right.

So before diving in, ask yourself:
Do I actually need Kubernetes… or do I just feel like I should use it?

Because complexity for the sake of it - helps no one.

Happy Friday!!

• • •

Software creates workloads
Workloads create traffic
Traffic creates scaling problems
Scaling problems create cloud engineers
Cloud engineers create architectures
Architectures create reliability
Reliability creates happy teams
Happy teams ship more features
More features create more workloads

…and the cycle continues.

If you’re a cloud engineer, you know:
– the only thing more endless than autoscaling groups
– is the number of meetings explaining why something didn’t scale.

If you're looking to enter the DevOps world, these 6 books should be added to your reading list…

1. 𝗧𝗵𝗲 𝗗𝗲𝘃𝗢𝗽𝘀 𝗛𝗮𝗻𝗱𝗯𝗼𝗼𝗸 𝗯𝘆 𝗚𝗲𝗻𝗲 𝗞𝗶𝗺, 𝗝𝗲𝘇 𝗛𝘂𝗺𝗯𝗹𝗲, 𝗣𝗮𝘁𝗿𝗶𝗰𝗸 𝗗𝗲𝗯𝗼𝗶𝘀 𝗮𝗻𝗱 𝗝𝗼𝗵𝗻 𝗪𝗶𝗹𝗹𝗶𝘀
↳ Offers a comprehensive guide to implementing DevOps practices and provides actionable strategies for improving workflow and collaboration

2. 𝗧𝗵𝗲 𝗣𝗵𝗼𝗲𝗻𝗶𝘅 𝗣𝗿𝗼𝗷𝗲𝗰𝘁 𝗯𝘆 𝗚𝗲𝗻𝗲 𝗞𝗶𝗺 𝗮𝗻𝗱 𝗞𝗲𝘃𝗶𝗻 𝗕𝗲𝗵𝗿
↳ A compelling novel that explains DevOps principles through an engaging business story; also shares real-world IT challenges and their solutions

3. 𝗖𝗼𝗻𝘁𝗶𝗻𝘂𝗼𝘂𝘀 𝗗𝗲𝗹𝗶𝘃𝗲𝗿𝘆 𝗯𝘆 𝗝𝗲𝘇 𝗛𝘂𝗺𝗯𝗹𝗲 𝗮𝗻𝗱 𝗗𝗮𝘃𝗶𝗱 𝗙𝗮𝗿𝗹𝗲𝘆
↳ Explores techniques for rapid, reliable software releases, followed by automation, testing, and deployment best practices

4. 𝗠𝗲𝗮𝘀𝘂𝗿𝗲 𝗪𝗵𝗮𝘁 𝗠𝗮𝘁𝘁𝗲𝗿𝘀 𝗯𝘆 𝗝𝗼𝗵𝗻 𝗗𝗼𝗲𝗿𝗿
↳ A roadmap to setting and achieving game-changing goals using the OKR (Objectives and Key Results) framework.

5. 𝗦𝗶𝘁𝗲 𝗥𝗲𝗹𝗶𝗮𝗯𝗶𝗹𝗶𝘁𝘆 𝗘𝗻𝗴𝗶𝗻𝗲𝗲𝗿𝗶𝗻𝗴 𝗯𝘆 𝗝𝗲𝗻𝗻𝗶𝗳𝗲𝗿 𝗣𝗲𝘁𝗼𝗳𝗳 𝗮𝗻𝗱 𝗡𝗶𝗮𝗹𝗹 𝗥𝗶𝗰𝗵𝗮𝗿𝗱 𝗠𝘂𝗿𝗽𝗵𝘆
↳ Explains Google's approach to large-scale system management and how to design resilient systems

6. 𝗧𝗵𝗲 𝗦𝗶𝘁𝗲 𝗥𝗲𝗹𝗶𝗮𝗯𝗶𝗹𝗶𝘁𝘆 𝗪𝗼𝗿𝗸𝗯𝗼𝗼𝗸 𝗯𝘆 𝗡𝗶𝗮𝗹𝗹 𝗥𝗶𝗰𝗵𝗮𝗿𝗱 𝗠𝘂𝗿𝗽𝗵𝘆, 𝗗𝗮𝘃𝗶𝗱 𝗞. 𝗥𝗲𝗻𝘀𝗶𝗻, 𝗞𝗲𝗻𝘁 𝗞𝗮𝘄𝗮𝗵𝗮𝗿𝗮 & 𝗦𝘁𝗲𝗽𝗵𝗲𝗻 𝗧𝗵𝗼𝗿𝗻𝗲
↳ A hands-on companion to SRE, packed with practical strategies and real-world case studies.

I'd personally suggest starting with "The Phoenix Project" - the narrative will keep you entertained; there is also a sequel called "Beyond The Phoenix Project" that you can take as your next read.

This is a great way to master those foundations, and you'll actually enjoy reading technical content.

--- Post 429 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Dec 18, 2025

Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7404533909394321408

If you’re expanding into systems programming for modern AI workloads ~ here’s why Rust keeps coming up.

As AI systems scale, the bottlenecks aren’t just in models.
They show up in memory safety, predictable latency, concurrency, and performance at scale.. and that’s where Rust quietly excels.

Here’s a simple breakdown of why Rust is rising across the AI/ML stack 👇

1. Rising in AI Systems
→ Rust delivers predictable performance, low latency, and zero-cost abstractions for accelerated workloads.
→ It’s built for concurrency and distributed execution ~ ideal for production-scale inference.

2. Rust vs Python in Inference Pipelines
→ No GIL(global interpreter lock) means true parallelism, lower latency, and tighter memory usage in serving pipelines.
→ Python stays the interface ~ Rust is increasingly the high-performance engine underneath.

3. AI Reliability & Security
→ Rust prevents memory leaks, data races, and unsafe concurrency at compile time.
→ This makes AI systems more deterministic, secure, and stable under real-world load.

4. Performance Optimization
→ Near C/C++ speeds with fine-grained control over memory and threads.
→ Inference stays predictable across large graphs, thanks to LLVM-level optimizations.

5. AI Infrastructure & Ecosystems
→ Rust powers SDK backends, inference runtimes, and vector-heavy workloads efficiently.
→ It integrates cleanly with C/C++ and forms the base for next-gen AI compilers and runtimes.

6. The Future of Rust in AI
→ Rust is becoming the systems layer for scalable AI from edge to data center.
→ Expect it to expand into accelerator scheduling, orchestration, and AI-specific runtime frameworks.

Rust isn’t replacing Python : it’s powering the AI systems that need speed, safety, and reliability.

Here are some resources you can use to start:
*The Rust Book (official intro)
https://lnkd.in/gJnCsVxz

*Rustlings (interactive exercises)
https://lnkd.in/gNPH29JP

*Rust by Example
https://lnkd.in/gMe5KuSY

What’s your perspective on Rust?

5 Must-Read Case Studies for DevOps Roles 

1. Adobe’s DevOps Acceleration
 → Use Azure DevOps, Docker & Kubernetes for faster CI/CD releases
 → Improve developer collaboration and deployment speed with Azure Pipelines
Case study link: https://lnkd.in/dcY9BT_a

2. GitLab’s Continuous Delivery Transformation
 → Scale with Google Kubernetes Engine and GitLab CI/CD pipelines
 → Achieve 99.93% uptime and reduce errors with Google Cloud Operations Suite
Case study link: https://lnkd.in/dwZRXekD

3. Oracle Red Bull Racing’s Simulation Scaling
 → Run 1B+ simulations using OCI HPC and Oracle Kubernetes Engine
 → Automate workflows with Oracle Resource Manager and Functions
Case study link: https://lnkd.in/d32J4Bzn

4. Capital One’s Cloud Modernization
 → Provision infrastructure rapidly using AWS CloudFormation and CodePipeline
 → Automate compliance via AWS Config and Amazon Inspector
Case study link: https://lnkd.in/dWTEG2Gj

5. Volusion’s Deployment Optimization
 → Use Terraform and Jenkins for Kubernetes deployments on GCP
 → Centralize logging and monitoring for quick issue resolution during deployments
Case study link: https://lnkd.in/dnvCwPFY

Here's what you should focus on:
➖ Why the DevOps toolset and architecture were chosen
➖ How automation improved scalability and deployment speed
➖ Challenges faced in continuous delivery and how they were solved
➖ Cloud services and CI/CD pipelines that were used to enable faster releases

Don’t get distracted by tools alone—start with the problem this practice solved.

I froze during my cloud interview when asked to explain the difference between IaaS and PaaS 

Even though I was aware of all the service models, I struggled to articulate the differences in a clear, concise manner.

This is where I realized - knowing something technically is different from being able to explain it effectively

Now for this question, the expectation from a candidate is to clearly be able to differentiate between what is cloud-managed vs. user-managed, essentially answering

who's responsible for what? 

Let's go through this one by one :

𝗜𝗮𝗮𝗦, 𝗣𝗮𝗮𝗦, 𝗮𝗻𝗱 𝗦𝗮𝗮𝗦: These are foundational service models that cover a broad spectrum of cloud services.

▪️ 𝗜𝗮𝗮𝗦 (Infrastructure as a Service) 
↳ On-demand access to computing resources (compute, storage, networking)
↳ Hyperscalers such as AWS, GCP, Azure are prime examples

▪️ 𝗣𝗮𝗮𝗦 (Platform as a Service) 
↳ Build and deploy apps without managing infrastructure
↳ GCP AppEngine, Elastic Beanstalk are some examples

▪️ 𝗦𝗮𝗮𝗦 (Software as a Service) 
↳ Fully managed by the provider.
↳ Think Salesforce or Google Workspace are some examles

-- 𝗦𝗽𝗲𝗰𝗶𝗮𝗹𝗶𝘇𝗲𝗱 𝗦𝘂𝗯𝘀𝗲𝘁𝘀 --

▪️ 𝗙𝗮𝗮𝗦 (Function as a Service) 
↳ Often considered a specialized subset of PaaS 
↳ Run individual functions without managing servers 
↳ Prime Examples - Cloud functions, Azure Function, AWS Lambda

▪️𝗖𝗮𝗮𝗦 (Containers as a Service) 
↳ Also considered a specialized subset of IaaS or PaaS
↳ Deploy and manage containers without worrying about the underlying infrastructure.
 ↳ Services like AWS ECS, EKS, GKE

𝗥𝗲𝗺𝗲𝗺𝗯𝗲𝗿: 𝗖𝗹𝗼𝘂𝗱 𝗔𝗽𝗽𝗹𝗶𝗰𝗮𝘁𝗶𝗼𝗻𝘀 = 𝗣𝗿𝗼𝘃𝗶𝗱𝗲𝗿 𝗠𝗮𝗻𝗮𝗴𝗲𝗱 + 𝗨𝘀𝗲𝗿 𝗠𝗮𝗻𝗮𝗴𝗲𝗱

Understand the shared responsibility approach and you'll be well-prepared for your next cloud interview or deployment decision.

Any other important subsets of service models, you'd like to add? 

♻️ Repost to help someone who's looking to enter Cloud.

🚀 Follow me, Vishakha Sadhwani for more Cloud and DevOps related Content.

A few trends I’ve been seeing around the AI Ecosystem - driven by Cloud and DevOps  (and how it's transforming in 2025)

Here's my take:

1/ Standardized CI/CD for AI Models
→ Automated validation pipelines
→ Repeatable training workflows
→ Version-controlled deployments
Key Impact: Faster time-to-production for models

2/ Infrastructure as Code (IaC) Evolution
→ GPU clusters managed via code (automated script generation for terraform)
→ Environment templating (repeatable deployments)
→ Automated scaling policies
Real Win: Consistent environments across teams

3/ Multi-Agent Orchestration
→ Agent interaction workflows
→ Dependency management
→ Collective intelligence optimization
Key Win: Significant reduction in agent conflicts

4/ Agent Observability Framework
→ Decision-path tracking
→ Resource consumption patterns (for cost-optimizations)
→ Behavioral analytics
Key Win: Full transparency into agent decisions

5/ Automated Feedback Loops
→ Real-time performance monitoring
→ Automated retraining triggers
→ Data drift detection
Impact: Self-healing AI systems

6/ Version Control 2.0
→ Dataset versioning
→ Experiment tracking
→ Model lineage
The difference? Complete reproducibility

7/ Model Governance
→ Centralized model registries
→ Automated compliance checks
→ Deployment guardrails

The shift that matters most in the current trends? 
Breaking down silos between data scientists, ML engineers and ops teams. 

Currently, it's not just about building models - it's about building sustainable, observable AI systems that work together.

Not an exhaustive list as this ecosystem is evolving incredibly quickly - and there's definitely more developments and learnings with these trends!

What did I miss??

--- Post 440 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: Jun 21, 2025
Engagement: 102 reactions, 15 comments, 2 reposts (Score: 664)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7336051623502168064

Storage is no longer just “where data lives”.. it’s how AI remembers, learns, and thinks faster than ever.

In modern ML systems, storage architecture directly impacts speed, efficiency, and cost.

As a cloud engineer, understanding how to map the right storage type to each AI workload is critical.

We can categorize storage along two key dimensions:
 → Performance vs Capacity Optimized
 → File vs Object Protocol

Here’s how storage supports each stage of the AI/ML lifecycle:

Raw Data Ingest
→ Stores large volumes of raw, unstructured data such as images, logs, or text
→ Requires scalable, cost-effective storage that supports parallel ingestion

Data Preparation
 → Uses high-performance file storage for cleaning, labeling, and transforming data
 → Requires frequent, low-latency reads/writes to enable fast iteration and processing

Training
 → Uses high-performance file or in-memory storage to feed large datasets to accelerators
 → Demands high-speed, parallel data access to keep GPU clusters fully utilized

Fine-Tuning
 → Uses high-performance file or in-memory storage for task-specific model updates
 → Requires low latency and high throughput to handle compute-intensive workloads

Inference / Deployment
 → Relies on in-memory or local storage (CPU/GPU) to serve model predictions
 → Prioritizes ultra-low latency for responsive, real-time user interactions

Archiving
 → Uses object storage for historical or infrequently accessed data
 → Optimized for long-term retention, cost-efficiency, and scalable capacity

Now, each of these use cases may rely on different storage solutions—whether it's object storage (like S3, GCS, Azure Blob), high-performance file systems, or block storage.

The key takeaway: Performance matters.

Throughput, latency, and access patterns are critical—especially when feeding data to compute-hungry accelerators like GPUs and TPUs. 

The faster you serve data, the more efficient your pipeline. And with those accelerators costing a premium, idle time is wasted money.

Read the full newsletter here for a deeper dive: https://lnkd.in/gnfpprku

Graduation pictures may look charming, but they’re a rollercoaster of emotions! 🎢

Here are a few crucial lessons I wish I had learned sooner as an international graduate:

Degrees Don't Define Your Path ~ Period!
It’s the courses and skills you acquire that truly shape your career journey!

Networking? Where? And Why? ~
Career fairs and conferences with solid networking opportunities can open doors you never knew existed!

Family Moments Matter 🥺 ~
Experiencing your family’s presence at your graduation in the States (whether virtually or in person) is truly surreal. It serves as a powerful reminder of your journey and fosters a sense of belonging, especially during uncertain times.

Quality Over Quantity ~ 
Applying to thousands of jobs online may not yield results; instead, focus on targeted applications and, if necessary, consider cold emailing recruiters and hiring managers for a slightly higher chance of engagement.

Embrace Continuous Learning ~
The tech industry evolves rapidly—staying updated with new technologies and trends (through launches, newsletters, conferences, and other forums) is essential for boosting your profile.

Soft Skills Matter Too! 🗣️ ~ 
Don't underestimate the power of communication (even reaching out to someone randomly for information); soft skills can be just as important as technical skills in getting things moving

--- Post 455 ---
Author: vsadhwani

Engagement: 75 reactions, 3 comments, 6 reposts (Score: 417)
Media: document
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7278786638787334145

Thinking of a Cloud DevOps Career in 2025? Start with Day 0!

Before diving into tools like Jenkins, Kubernetes, and Terraform, lay a strong foundation with these core concepts. This is what we call "DevOps Day 0."

Compute: The Heart of Your Infrastructure
↳ Understand the trade-offs between physical machines, virtual machines (VMs), and containers. This will guide your infrastructure choices

Networking: The Backbone of DevOps
↳ Master networking fundamentals like TCP/IP, DNS, HTTP/S, and VPNs. Understand load balancing, firewalls, and how networks function to ensure smooth application flow.

Databases: Fueling Your Applications
↳ Grasp the difference between SQL and NoSQL databases. Learn about ACID properties, data modeling, and how to choose the right database for your application's needs.

Security: Prioritize from Day 0
↳ Understand encryption, authentication, authorization, and the OWASP Top 10 vulnerabilities. Learn about security policies, risk assessments, and compliance standards like GDPR and HIPAA.

Storage: Where Your Data Lives
↳ Explore block, object, and file storage. Learn about NAS, SAN, SSD vs. HDD, and how to choose the right storage solution for your data.

Disaster Recovery: Plan for the Unexpected
↳ Understand DR concepts like backup and restore, pilot light, and warm standby. Define RTO (Recovery Time Objective) and RPO (Recovery Point Objective) for your applications.

Caching: Speed Up Your Applications
↳ Explore in-memory caches (Redis, Memcached), CDNs, and caching techniques. Understand the importance of cache invalidation and cache hit ratio

Data Replication: Ensuring High Availability
↳ Learn about different replication methods (master-slave, peer-to-peer), synchronous vs. asynchronous replication, and how to maintain data consistency.

Get the 'Day 0' basics right before jumping into tools.

PS. ~ This is just a bird's-eye view of the concepts that need to be brushed up in order to grasp the processes, approaches, and components involved in a DevOps pipeline. Explore these topics further to get the basics right.

Happy to help with any topics if needed!

URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7278631980542418944

Thinking of a Cloud DevOps Career in 2025? Start with Day 0!

Before diving into tools like Jenkins, Kubernetes, and Terraform, lay a strong foundation with these core concepts. This is what we call "DevOps Day 0."

Compute: The Heart of Your Infrastructure
↳ Understand the trade-offs between physical machines, virtual machines (VMs), and containers. This will guide your infrastructure choices

Networking: The Backbone of DevOps
↳ Master networking fundamentals like TCP/IP, DNS, HTTP/S, and VPNs. Understand load balancing, firewalls, and how networks function to ensure smooth application flow.

Databases: Fueling Your Applications
↳ Grasp the difference between SQL and NoSQL databases. Learn about ACID properties, data modeling, and how to choose the right database for your application's needs.

Security: Prioritize from Day 0
↳ Understand encryption, authentication, authorization, and the OWASP Top 10 vulnerabilities. Learn about security policies, risk assessments, and compliance standards like GDPR and HIPAA.

Storage: Where Your Data Lives
↳ Explore block, object, and file storage. Learn about NAS, SAN, SSD vs. HDD, and how to choose the right storage solution for your data.

Disaster Recovery: Plan for the Unexpected
↳ Understand DR concepts like backup and restore, pilot light, and warm standby. Define RTO (Recovery Time Objective) and RPO (Recovery Point Objective) for your applications.

Caching: Speed Up Your Applications
↳ Explore in-memory caches (Redis, Memcached), CDNs, and caching techniques. Understand the importance of cache invalidation and cache hit ratio

Data Replication: Ensuring High Availability
↳ Learn about different replication methods (master-slave, peer-to-peer), synchronous vs. asynchronous replication, and how to maintain data consistency.

Get the 'Day 0' basics right before jumping into tools.

PS. ~ This is just a bird's-eye view of the concepts that need to be brushed up in order to grasp the processes, approaches, and components involved in a DevOps pipeline. Explore these topics further to get the basics right.

Happy to help with any topics if needed!

Landing my first job in 2018 was a dream come true, but little did I know the challenges that awaited me in the world of DevOps 🫠 

Well, the reality of breaking and fixing systems, dealing with 24/7 on-call duties, conducting root cause analysis, and deep debugging often pushed me to my limits. Some days, you're basically living the 'Everything is Fine, Fire' meme!

There were moments when I almost quit, questioning whether I had made the right career choice. Was the switch from "Software Development" to the complex world of "DevOps" worth it? To be honest, I wasn't great as a software developer either:p

But fortunately, I had a supportive environment that helped me push through.
However, the fear of being overlooked for raises and promotions loomed large, and I constantly felt pressured to master everything.

In our pursuit of career advancement, we often get lost chasing the next big milestone(and some additional $$), forgetting to celebrate the small victories that pave our way.

The key thing to realize is that neither employees nor hires should expect any engineer to master every tool or technology right away.

If you're looking to enter any field, familiarize yourself with some core concepts first. Why does this tool/technology exist, and what problem does it solve? Then, DIVE IN!

For those eager to start their DevOps journey, here are some key concepts to grasp:
↳ Containerization
↳ Continuous Integration
↳ Continuous Delivery
↳ Continuous Deployment
↳ Cloud Computing
↳ Version Control Systems
↳ Infrastructure as Code (IaC) 
↳ Configuration Management (CM)

Once you have a solid understanding of what you're getting into, it can help you navigate the unexpected challenges that may arise, empowering you to continue your DevOps journey with confidence ▶️

============================================================

You might be surprised how often Linux pops up in a Cloud Devops role, even if it wasn't a major focus in your interviews. 

As a Cloud Engineer, I wasn't explicitly tested on it, but I've found it invaluable. FOR REAL! 🤯 

From scripting and automation to navigating cloud environments, a solid grasp of Linux commands provides a significant advantage for any cloud professional. 

Good part is - you don't need to memorize every command, some familiarity with basic commands and the ability to effectively use resources like cheatsheets can significantly help with troubleshooting, automating and enhance your efficiency overall!

My two cents? 
--> Start by running some basic commands to customize your environment and then practice them on various projects

Whether you're a beginner or a seasoned pro, such artifacts are always a handy resource. Check this one out 👇

Author: vsadhwani

URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7277275382045720576

You can excel as a Cloud Engineer even if...

↳ You frequently reference documentation and best practices
↳ Your currently have experience with only small scale cloud-projects 
↳ You utilize online resources like Stack Overflow for troubleshootinge valuable insights and solutions.
↳ Your current expertise is focused on a single cloud provider (e.g., AWS, Azure, GCP)
↳ You are still developing your programming skills (e.g., Python, Bash, Go)
↳ You sometimes require assistance with debugging complex cloud configurations
↳ You are still learning and exploring various cloud-native technologies (e.g., containers, serverless, microservices)

You would certainly NOT do well when...

↳ You stop being curious and willing to learn. Period.

↳ You don’t adapt to the newer technologies 

Cloud computing is a rapidly evolving field. A stagnant mindset will quickly become obsolete!

What you can certainly do::

‼️ The cloud landscape is constantly evolving, so dedicate time to learning new technologies, their use cases, best practices, and overall pros and cons.

‼️ Focus on gaining in-depth knowledge of one cloud provider before gradually expanding your expertise to others.

‼️ Utilize online communities, documentation, and established best practices to solve challenges and enhance your skills.

‼️ Practice through projects, including learning scripting languages and automation tools to streamline cloud operations and improve efficiency.

============================================================

MLOps, GitOps, FinOps, DevSecOps... the 'Ops' world can feel overwhelming! 🤯 

But what exactly does each one focus on?

Becoming a FinOps Engineer doesn't require you to be a financial analyst.

Just like DevSecOps integrates security, FinOps integrates financial management principles (such as allocating cloud costs and implementing cost-saving measures) into the DevOps workflow.

Similarly, becoming an MLOps/AIOps Engineer doesn't require you to be an ML model builder, or need to be an expert in LLM or GenAI. The focus is on automating the training and testing of ML models. 

This principle applies similarly to other niche focus areas within DevOps.

These 'Ops' are specialized extensions of DevOps, each tackling a specific domain (like machine learning, AI, cloud, finance, data, Git).

Below is a brief breakdown of some common ones👇 

**Stay tuned for a deeper dive into each one!**

P.S. This is not an exhaustive list - there are many other extensions and approaches that have emerged to address specific needs within the DevOps framework.

============================================================

--- Post 471 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: May 17, 2025
Engagement: 62 reactions, 2 comments, 9 reposts (Score: 348)
Media: document
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7284941530685935618

The "Cloud Starter Pack" 

Cloud Deployment Models. 

If you're preparing for a cloud-specific interview or already working on a cloud-native project - it's extremely important to understand cloud deployment strategies. 

Here’s a quick guide that can help you familiarize yourself with the terminologies.

Additionally, prepare for questions as below:

1. Private Cloud

↳ Knowing why an organization choose a private cloud over a public cloud (Focus on security, compliance, and control)
↳ Potential challenges of managing a private cloud (Consider costs, maintenance, and scalability)

2. Public Cloud

↳ Key benefits of using public cloud (Highlight scalability, pay-per-use pricing, and a wide range of services)
↳ Any security concerns when using a public cloud (Discuss encryption, access controls, and security audits)


3. Hybrid Cloud

↳ When hybrid cloud approach be most advantageous (Consider scenarios requiring a mix of on-premises and off-premises resources, data migration strategies) + potential challenges

4. Multi Cloud

↳ Benefits of adopting a multi-cloud strategy (Focus on vendor lock-in avoidance, optimization for specific workloads, and improved disaster recovery)
↳ How can you manage the complexity of operating across multiple cloud platforms (Discuss multi-cloud management tools and strategies)

5. Community Cloud

↳ Potential advantages and disadvantages of a community cloud (Consider cost-effectiveness, shared resources, and potential limitations) + industry sectors where this model would be particularly beneficial

Let me know your thoughts in the comments!!

============================================================

Stoked to see our panel recording from PlatformCon go live. Was super cool to discuss the future of platforms in the AI age with Vishakha Sadhwani, Rickey Zachary, and Sylvain Kalache. Some of the fun things discussed:

- There will be platforms with deterministic software
- These platforms will be composed of tools that AI agents can use via MCP
- The most important letter in the MCP is C... the context that converts human language to code in one shot in a tool call
- Runbooks are deterministic software for humans, which is an evidence proof that we can work perfectly fine having nondeterministic systems (humans) that weave themselves with deterministic systems that "do the exact transaction I asked for"
- AI as "Angry Intern"
- Some of what we are doing at NVIDIA from our Applied AI lab in this space

Thanks to Kaspar Von Grünberg and the team that runs PlatformCon for hosting the panel!

Watch the panel here: https://lnkd.in/eCgNJP5e

Your Job search might feel like:

..You have no idea what you actually want to do next
..You're sending out tons of applications but not getting interviews
..You're getting interviews but not getting past the recruiter screen
..You're getting to the final round - but not getting offers

It's going to be okay! The gaps could be anything between unclarity of your career goals, insufficient networking, lack of preparation, or literally anything.

Knowing and acknowledging the gaps is important. 

Once you identify the areas where you need to improve, you can take the necessary steps to overcome them and increase your chances of landing the job with the right compensation and impact. 

Find the niche, find the role, get your basics right -- and you will find the way!!

Find the niche, find the role, get your basics right -- and you will find the way!!

--- Post 494 ---
Author: vsadhwani
Date: May 17, 2025
Engagement: 14 reactions, 2 reposts (Score: 74)
Media: image
URL: https://www.linkedin.com/feed/update/urn:li:activity:7268819201358778368

"Add one more pod!" – a sentiment every Kubernetes enthusiast can relate to!

But what truly happens when you spin up a new pod, and why is it important?

Let’s dive in:
A Pod in Kubernetes is the smallest deployable unit that can contain one or more containers. 

When you create a pod - Kubernetes schedules the pod to run on a node, managing resources and ensuring high availability.
The containers(which can be multiple) within the pod share the same network namespace, allowing them to communicate easily and share storage volumes, streamlining application deployment.

But here’s the thing:
When deploying applications, using too few Kubernetes Pods can lead to underutilization of resources, resulting in performance bottlenecks. 

On the flip side, deploying too many Pods can overwhelm the cluster, leading to resource contention and degraded performance.

What can we do to address this?
> Resource Requests and Limits:
Set appropriate resource requests and limits for each Pod to ensure they have enough resources without overcommitting the cluster.
> Horizontal Pod Autoscaling:
Automate scaling by adjusting the number of Pods based on CPU or memory usage, ensuring optimal performance during varying loads.
> Monitoring and Metrics:
Use monitoring tools to track Pod performance metrics, helping you understand when to scale up or down based on actual usage patterns.
